Table 1
Project Risk, Planning, and Delivery: Think Slow, Act Fast
1. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 1, “Think Slow, Act Fast”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that ambitious projects are more likely to succeed when teams invest time in deliberate preparation and then execute a tested plan efficiently. “Think slow” means researching the problem, testing assumptions, comparing alternatives, coordinating interfaces, and developing a credible route to the intended outcome before major commitments. “Act fast” means maintaining disciplined momentum once the project is ready. The principle is not an invitation to delay or overanalyse. It is a way to spend time where it reduces uncertainty most cheaply. Planning is often relatively reversible; construction, production, deployment, and implementation consume significant resources and expose the project to disruption. Rushing into delivery transfers unresolved questions into the phase where mistakes are most expensive to correct.
Study instruction:
- "Divide a project you know into planning and delivery. Identify which uncertainties should be resolved before major expenditure and which activities can then be executed quickly."
Key point:
- Invest thinking time before irreversible commitments.
- Speed is most valuable after the project has been properly prepared.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
2. Q: What does the Great Belt tunnel incident demonstrate about interfaces and coordination?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes bridge works using a dredger whose support legs left holes in the seabed; one hole lay on the projected tunnel route, but the bridge and tunnelling teams did not identify the conflict. During maintenance, water entered the tunnel and flooding damaged the machine and connected works. The incident illustrates that separate contractors can perform their individual tasks while the integrated project remains exposed to a serious interface failure. Shared geography, temporary works, access, utilities, sequencing, and maintenance conditions must be coordinated across work packages. Interface risks need named owners, shared drawings and schedules, technical review, and confirmation that dependent activities are safe to proceed. The project manager remains responsible for the integrated outcome even when work is divided among contractors.
Study instruction:
- "Draw an interface map for a project. List workstreams that share physical, technical, data, schedule, or operational dependencies and assign an owner and review point to each."
Key point:
- Local work-package control does not guarantee system-wide safety.
- Interfaces and temporary conditions require explicit coordination.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
3. Q: How does the chapter define a megaproject, and why does scale matter?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a budget above US$1 billion as a practical definition of a megaproject. This is the chapter’s classification, not a universal legal or professional threshold. Scale matters because large projects concentrate capital, political attention, public expectations, technical complexity, and organizational dependencies. A failure may affect taxpayers, customers, suppliers, employees, communities, and the sponsor. However, the author warns that the same mechanisms can affect much smaller initiatives. Governance should therefore reflect complexity, exposure, reversibility, and consequences as well as budget. A modest renovation with structural uncertainty and limited financial reserves may need more careful risk management than its price suggests.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two projects with different budgets but similar dependency and consequence profiles. Explain why their governance requirements should not be determined by cost alone."
Key point:
- The US$1 billion threshold is a working definition used in the chapter.
- Governance should reflect complexity, exposure, and consequences as well as cost.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
4. Q: What three dimensions should be assessed when judging project success?
A: Show answer
The chapter evaluates cost, schedule, and benefits. Cost asks whether delivery stayed within a consistently defined budget; schedule asks whether completion occurred when promised; benefits ask whether the project produced the value that justified the investment. Benefits may include revenue, savings, passenger capacity, electricity generation, service quality, reliability, or strategic capability. These dimensions are related but not interchangeable. A project may open on time by spending more than authorized, or meet its budget by reducing scope and sacrificing benefits. A completed facility may still fail to generate forecast demand or service outcomes. A sound evaluation defines the baseline, scope, measurement date, and benefit indicators in advance, then reports each dimension separately before drawing an overall conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Create a scorecard for a project with separate cost, schedule, and benefit measures. Define the baseline and the date when each measure will be assessed."
Key point:
- Completion is not the same as successful benefit realization.
- Cost, time, and benefits should be measured separately against explicit baselines.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
5. Q: What is the Iron Law of Megaprojects, and why is it probabilistic rather than deterministic?
A: Show answer
The Iron Law is summarized as “over budget, over time, under benefits, over and over again.” It describes a persistent empirical pattern in which large projects frequently cost more, take longer, and deliver less than promised. The author cautions that “law” does not mean every individual project must experience all three outcomes. Project results are influenced by human decisions, institutions, technology, markets, and external events, so the pattern is probabilistic. The practical implication is to reject the assumption that an approved estimate is automatically a reliable forecast. Sponsors should compare the proposal with actual outcomes from similar projects, examine the distribution of possible outcomes, challenge assumptions, and maintain controls that respond to emerging problems.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between a high-probability empirical pattern and an inevitable outcome. Apply that distinction to a project approval decision."
Key point:
- The Iron Law describes a recurring tendency, not a guaranteed result.
- Use the pattern to improve forecasts and controls, not to assume that failure is unavoidable.
Source links:
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
6. Q: Why did the author build a project database, and what makes the evidence useful?
A: Show answer
The author began collecting data because there was no adequate systematic answer to a basic question: how often do projects deliver the cost, schedule, and benefits they promised? A database allows forecasts to be compared with actual outcomes across sectors and countries rather than relying on anecdotes or sponsor claims. The chapter reports that the database grew from 258 transport projects to more than 16,000 projects across more than 20 fields and 136 countries. These figures describe the author’s database as reported in the chapter; they should not be treated as proof that every project has identical risk. Useful comparison requires consistent definitions, original baselines, actual outcomes, scope information, inflation treatment, and traceable source records.
Study instruction:
- "Design a project-outcomes dataset with fields for decision date, approved cost, scope, planned completion, actual cost, actual completion, and benefits. Specify the evidence needed for each field."
Key point:
- Comparable records turn isolated failures into analyzable evidence.
- Consistent definitions and traceable records determine whether comparisons are meaningful.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
7. Q: What does the Great Belt cost-overrun dispute teach about honest numbers?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that management initially described the overall Great Belt overrun as 29 percent, while the author calculated 55 percent overall and 120 percent for the tunnel, measured in real terms from the final investment decision. A later national audit reportedly confirmed the author’s figures. The lesson is that a percentage is not self-explanatory: it depends on the baseline, scope, price basis, inflation treatment, exclusions, and comparison date. People with reputational or political interests may select favorable numbers without necessarily committing fraud. A credible review reconstructs the calculation from source records, publishes definitions, reconciles differences, and obtains independent assurance where material decisions depend on the result.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a cost-overrun reconciliation showing baseline, scope, nominal and real amounts, inflation adjustment, exclusions, actual cost, and formula."
Key point:
- A percentage is meaningful only when its baseline and calculation are transparent.
- Independent verification reduces the risk of selective or inconsistent reporting.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
8. Q: How should a project team calculate and interpret a cost overrun?
A: Show answer
A basic cost-overrun percentage is (actual comparable cost minus baseline estimated cost) divided by baseline estimated cost, multiplied by 100. The calculation is valid only when the figures cover comparable scope and use a consistent price basis. The team should identify the formal decision baseline and explain any earlier estimates that help show how expectations evolved. It should distinguish nominal amounts from inflation-adjusted real amounts and disclose treatment of scope changes, land, taxes, financing, owner costs, and exclusions. For phased projects, the report should state whether it covers the whole program or one contract. An overrun is a performance measure, not an explanation of cause; root-cause analysis remains necessary.
Study instruction:
- "Using illustrative figures of 100 million budget and 130 million actual cost, calculate the overrun and list the checks required before reporting it."
Key point:
- Use comparable scope, baseline, and price bases.
- Report the calculation transparently and investigate causes separately.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
9. Q: Why is cost and schedule performance insufficient without benefit measurement?
A: Show answer
Cost and schedule describe resources consumed and time taken, but they do not establish whether the project produced the value that justified the investment. A project may finish on time and within budget yet fail because demand is lower than forecast, services are not ready, performance is unreliable, or intended users do not adopt the result. Benefits may take time to emerge, so they need an owner, a baseline, indicators, and review dates beyond handover. The chapter includes “under benefits” in the Iron Law to prevent a narrow completion metric from disguising a project that was delivered efficiently but did not achieve its purpose. Benefit realization should therefore be managed as part of the project lifecycle, not left as an assumption after construction.
Study instruction:
- "For a project proposal, define its intended benefits, measurement baseline, accountable owner, indicators, and post-completion review date."
Key point:
- A completed asset is not proof that intended value was realized.
- Benefit ownership and measurement should continue beyond delivery.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
10. Q: What is the difference between average risk and the shape of a risk distribution?
A: Show answer
An average summarizes outcomes but does not show how they are distributed. Two project types can have the same mean overrun but very different probabilities of extreme losses. One may have relatively clustered outcomes; another may have many modest overruns and a small number of catastrophic ones. The chapter argues that many project categories have fat-tailed distributions, so relying on the mean alone can understate exposure. Decision-makers should examine percentiles, tail frequencies, dispersion, and extreme cases, while recognizing that estimates are themselves uncertain. A risk assessment should communicate a central forecast and plausible adverse scenarios rather than presenting one expected value as if it described the whole range.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two hypothetical portfolios with the same average overrun but different worst-case outcomes. Explain which creates greater financial exposure and why."
Key point:
- The mean does not reveal the probability or severity of extremes.
- Risk analysis must examine the full distribution, especially its tail.
Source links:
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
- NASA Technical Risk Management
11. Q: How does a normal distribution differ from a fat-tailed distribution in project-risk analysis?
A: Show answer
A normal distribution concentrates most outcomes near the mean and assigns relatively low probability to observations far from it. A fat-tailed distribution contains more extreme observations than a normal model would predict. The chapter uses height as an intuitive thin-tail example and wealth as an example of a highly unequal, fat-tailed variable. For projects, the danger is assuming that a typical overrun or standard deviation adequately describes the downside. If the true distribution is fat-tailed, an overrun several times larger than the central estimate may be more plausible than a normal model suggests. The exact statistical form varies by project type and dataset; “fat-tailed” is not a substitute for fitting and validating an appropriate model.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a normal and a hypothetical heavy-tailed distribution with the same mean. Explain why the probability of a very large overrun differs."
Key point:
- Extreme outcomes are more common under fat tails than a normal model implies.
- Choose risk models based on evidence rather than convenience or intuition.
Source links:
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
12. Q: Why can a contingency equal to the average overrun still leave a project exposed?
A: Show answer
A contingency sized to the average overrun assumes that the average is a reasonable representation of the downside. In a fat-tailed setting, the average may be far below the severity of the largest outcomes, and the probability of exceeding it may be material. The chapter illustrates this with a building project whose reported mean overrun is 62 percent: even a planner with a 62 percent buffer has not eliminated exposure if the distribution contains very large overruns. A reserve should be informed by the risk distribution, confidence level, reference class, and the sponsor’s ability to absorb loss. It must be paired with mitigation, scope choices, and decision gates; it is not a substitute for a credible plan.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a 15 percent reserve, a mean-based reserve, and a percentile-based reserve for a hypothetical project. State what risk each approach leaves uncovered."
Key point:
- The average is not a worst-case allowance or a guarantee.
- Reserve sizing must reflect tail risk and the sponsor’s risk tolerance.
Source links:
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
- HM Treasury: Optimism Bias Guidance
- NASA Technical Risk Management
13. Q: What do the chapter’s IT project figures reveal about extreme overruns?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that 18 percent of IT projects in its dataset had real cost overruns above 50 percent, and that the average overrun within that tail group was 447 percent. The 447 percent figure is a conditional average for the extreme subgroup, not the average for all IT projects. Quoting it without explaining the subgroup would misrepresent the result. A separate study by Flyvbjerg and co-authors analyzed 5,392 IT projects and found a power-law pattern with a smaller number of very extreme outcomes. This supports the warning that near-normal assumptions can understate large IT overruns. It does not mean every IT project will experience an extreme outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Write a data note distinguishing the 18 percent tail frequency, the 447 percent average within that tail, and the overall population."
Key point:
- Always state the population and denominator behind a risk statistic.
- IT project cost risk can include a small number of exceptionally large overruns.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
14. Q: How should the term “black swan” be understood in project failure?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses Taleb’s term “black swan” for low-probability, high-consequence events and applies it to project outcomes that can destroy value, damage organizations, or end careers. The label reminds decision-makers that ordinary planning assumptions may omit severe events. It should not become a vague name for every surprise or every overrun. Some risks are foreseeable in broad categories even when their precise timing or form is unknown; others emerge from interactions that were not anticipated. Teams should distinguish known risks, uncertain scenarios, and genuinely novel events, then build resilience through robust design, flexible capacity, staged commitments, and contingency arrangements. The point is to take extreme outcomes seriously rather than dismissing them as impossible exceptions.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five project disruptions as known risks, uncertain but imaginable scenarios, or genuinely novel events. Explain how preparation differs for each."
Key point:
- Low-frequency events can have consequences large enough to dominate project outcomes.
- Do not use “black swan” to excuse weak risk identification or preparation.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
15. Q: Why do interdependencies make projects vulnerable to cascading failure?
A: Show answer
A complex project contains components whose performance depends on other components, contractors, organizations, technologies, or external systems. When one element changes or fails, connected elements may be affected, and effects can reinforce one another rather than remain local. The chapter uses the Ever Given blockage of the Suez Canal to show how wind, ship position, canal geometry, and supply-chain dependencies combined to produce a major disruption. In projects, analogous dependencies may involve power, software interfaces, permits, materials, access, specialist labor, or shared sites. Mapping these relationships helps identify single points of failure, tight coupling, and consequences disproportionate to the initiating event.
Study instruction:
- "Create a dependency map with at least ten components. Identify one initiating failure and trace three possible downstream effects."
Key point:
- A small initiating event can have a large system-wide consequence.
- Manage dependencies and propagation paths, not only isolated hazards.
Source links:
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
- NASA Technical Risk Management
16. Q: What does nonlinear amplification mean in a project system?
A: Show answer
A nonlinear response occurs when the effect of a change is not proportional to its size. A small disturbance may have little impact in one situation but trigger a major disruption when it coincides with other conditions or passes through tightly connected components. A minor design defect may delay testing, postpone regulatory approval, miss a seasonal construction window, increase costs, and disrupt supplier commitments. The effects can amplify rather than simply add together. Teams should test scenarios across interfaces, look for critical dependencies, and avoid treating every risk as an independent line item. NASA’s risk framework similarly emphasizes scenarios, likelihood, consequences, and uncertainty rather than relying only on a single risk label.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a small defect or delay and map how it could propagate through design, procurement, approvals, construction, commissioning, and operations."
Key point:
- The consequence of a risk depends on the system in which it occurs.
- Scenario analysis should test interacting events, not only isolated hazards.
Source links:
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
- NASA Technical Risk Management
17. Q: Why are smaller projects also exposed to extreme outcomes?
A: Show answer
The author argues that project size does not remove uncertainty, interdependence, or cascading consequences. A home renovation may depend on structural discoveries, permits, specialist contractors, materials, and financing. A small business launch may depend on a supplier, a software platform, customer adoption, and regulatory approval. Smaller sponsors often have less capacity to absorb a large overrun than governments or major corporations, so an extreme outcome may be existential even when its absolute value is modest. The chapter therefore treats ambition and dependency as more important than budget alone. Proportionate planning and risk analysis are relevant to ordinary projects, not only megaprojects.
Study instruction:
- "Select a personal, community, or small-business project. Identify dependencies that could create an overrun large enough to threaten completion."
Key point:
- Smaller scale does not eliminate complexity or extreme downside.
- Limited financial resilience can make a modest project’s overrun catastrophic to its sponsor.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
18. Q: What is the “window of doom,” and how does duration affect exposure?
A: Show answer
The “window of doom” is the chapter’s metaphor for the period during which a project remains exposed to changing conditions. A longer project keeps the window open for more time, creating more opportunities for political, economic, environmental, technological, health, market, or organizational events to disrupt it. The point is not that every additional day creates a fixed amount of risk. Duration increases the period during which uncertain events can occur and interact with dependencies. Some hazards are seasonal or stage-specific, and risk may not accumulate at a constant rate. The practical lesson is to avoid unnecessary duration, reduce idle time and rework, and make exposure as short as is safely achievable.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a project timeline and mark external events that could affect each phase. Identify which delays extend exposure and which risks can be retired before delivery."
Key point:
- Longer exposure creates more opportunities for disruption.
- Shorten duration by improving readiness and flow, not by skipping essential planning.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
19. Q: Does a longer project automatically have a higher risk of failure?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter presents duration as an exposure mechanism, not a deterministic rule. A longer project may be justified by technical constraints, regulatory requirements, safety assurance, or the time needed to develop a sound solution. A short project can still fail if it is rushed, poorly specified, or dependent on untested technology. The relevant question is whether each period adds necessary value or merely leaves the project exposed to change, cost escalation, and coordination problems. Teams should distinguish productive time from waiting, rework, unresolved decisions, and avoidable handoffs. They should also consider whether schedule compression could increase defects or safety risks.
Study instruction:
- "Review a project schedule and classify periods as necessary work, decision latency, waiting, rework, or contingency. Identify safe opportunities to reduce non-value-adding duration."
Key point:
- Duration is a risk exposure factor, not a stand-alone verdict on project quality.
- Remove avoidable delay without compressing work that protects safety or reliability.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
20. Q: How can ordinary external changes become major project threats?
A: Show answer
External changes affect assumptions on which the project depends: political leadership, economic conditions, interest rates, demand, public-health restrictions, weather, supply chains, laws, or key personnel. A disruption need not be dramatic to matter. A seemingly trivial change can have serious consequences if it occurs at a sensitive point or interacts with tightly coupled activities. Teams should identify external dependencies, monitor leading indicators, define scenarios, and establish response options. A risk record should include the trigger, effect, owner, mitigation, contingency, and escalation threshold. The team cannot control every external event, but it can reduce vulnerability and improve its ability to respond.
Study instruction:
- "Identify five external assumptions in a live project. For each, define an observable trigger, likely project effect, owner, and practical response."
Key point:
- Timing and context can make a seemingly minor change consequential.
- Monitor external assumptions and prepare responses before a trigger occurs.
Source links:
- NASA Technical Risk Management
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
21. Q: What are “normal accidents,” and how do they relate to complex projects?
A: Show answer
The chapter refers to Charles Perrow’s concept of “normal accidents” to explain how accidents can arise from complex, tightly coupled systems even when individuals are not acting recklessly. When components interact in unexpected ways and there is little time or flexibility to isolate a problem, a small failure can spread before operators intervene. The term does not mean accidents are acceptable or inevitable in every complex system. It highlights the need to understand architecture, coupling, feedback, and recovery capacity. Leaders can reduce exposure by simplifying interfaces, allowing buffers between dependent activities, designing safe shutdown or rollback options, and testing failure scenarios before operation.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a complex process and identify tightly coupled components. Propose a design change that would make a failure easier to isolate."
Key point:
- Complexity and tight coupling can make local failures propagate rapidly.
- Resilience improves when systems can isolate, absorb, or reverse failures.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- The Empirical Reality of IT Project Cost Overruns
22. Q: Why does the author reject aggressive deadlines as the main way to create speed?
A: Show answer
An aggressive deadline may create urgency, but urgency is not efficient delivery. If scope, design, interfaces, procurement, permissions, and technical questions remain unresolved, a forced early start transfers uncertainty into the expensive delivery phase. Defects, rework, claims, safety problems, and schedule slippage may exceed the time supposedly saved. The chapter contrasts demanding that a project finish sooner with making it ready sooner. Real speed comes from reducing uncertainty and removing avoidable friction before delivery, then maintaining a clear execution path. A deadline is useful only when grounded in a credible plan and adequate resources.
Study instruction:
- "Test an imposed deadline against scope, capacity, dependencies, approvals, and readiness criteria. Identify what would need to change to make it credible."
Key point:
- Pressure cannot substitute for readiness or a realistic plan.
- A rushed start often creates more delay later through rework and disruption.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
23. Q: What does the Copenhagen Opera House example reveal about deadline-driven decisions?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a client who pressed for a four-year completion when the architect estimated five years. The deadline was met, but the author reports that the architect was deeply dissatisfied with the completed building and later wrote a book explaining the design problems. The example shows that meeting a visible date does not prove that a project optimized quality, cost, or long-term value. A deadline can become a dominant political or personal objective, displacing design integrity, functionality, and lifecycle consequences. Before accepting acceleration, sponsors should ask what is being compressed, who bears the consequences, and whether quality and safety criteria remain protected.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a project where a public opening date or executive milestone dominates planning. List potential trade-offs and define non-negotiable quality and safety requirements."
Key point:
- A date achieved at the expense of value may represent poor performance.
- Schedule commitments must be tested against quality, scope, cost, and safety.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
24. Q: What management failures are highlighted by the Mexico City metro overpass collapse example?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that investigations into the 2021 collapse identified construction deficiencies and described pressure to complete the line before a mayor left office as a contributing factor. The lesson is that political or reputational deadlines can distort technical decisions when governance does not protect independent engineering judgment. A credible system needs design maturity, quality assurance, inspection, testing, change control, and authority to stop unsafe work. Safety-critical evidence must not be overridden by schedule pressure. The example also shows why accountability should examine the decision system, not merely blame the last worker or contractor. Legal responsibility depends on the investigation and applicable law, but projects should preserve escalation routes for unresolved safety concerns.
Study instruction:
- "Create a safety assurance checklist for a major construction milestone, including design approval, inspection records, defect closure, independent review, and stop-work escalation."
Key point:
- Schedule pressure must never displace safety-critical verification.
- Independent assurance and stop-work authority are essential controls.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
25. Q: Why does the chapter divide projects into planning and delivery phases?
A: Show answer
The two-phase model distinguishes developing a reliable approach from committing resources to execute it. Planning includes defining the objective, exploring alternatives, researching, designing, testing, sequencing, and preparing the organization. Delivery includes building, producing, deploying, or implementing the project. Industry labels differ, but the underlying logic is similar. The division is a simplification because real projects iterate and revisit decisions. It nevertheless helps managers recognize that cost, reversibility, and exposure change when the project enters delivery. A formal transition should require evidence that the plan is sufficiently mature and that remaining uncertainty is understood and manageable.
Study instruction:
- "Map a project into planning and delivery activities. Mark the point where major irreversible expenditure begins and specify the evidence required to cross it."
Key point:
- Planning and delivery have different cost and risk characteristics.
- A readiness decision should separate exploration from major execution commitments.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
26. Q: Why does the chapter describe planning as a relatively safe harbor?
A: Show answer
Planning can often be done through analysis, drawings, prototypes, simulations, models, workshops, and limited experiments rather than committing the full delivery budget. Errors discovered during planning are generally cheaper to correct than defects found after construction, production, or deployment. Planning is not risk-free: weak analysis can waste time and assumptions can become entrenched. But its financial exposure is usually more contained and its options more reversible. The “safe harbor” metaphor does not mean planning should continue indefinitely; it means that deliberate investigation is usually less costly than learning the same lessons after large commitments have been made.
Study instruction:
- "List five questions that could be tested cheaply before delivery and estimate the relative cost of discovering each issue during construction or rollout."
Key point:
- Early learning is generally cheaper than late correction.
- Use planning to preserve options and resolve uncertainty before major commitments.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
27. Q: What should a sound planning phase accomplish before delivery begins?
A: Show answer
A sound planning phase turns a broad vision into a sufficiently researched, tested, and detailed route to the intended outcome. It should clarify the problem and benefits, define scope and acceptance criteria, compare alternatives, identify dependencies and interfaces, test technical assumptions, estimate cost and duration using evidence, establish governance, and prepare resources and procurement. It should also assign owners to material risks and define responses, approvals, and monitoring information. The chapter does not imply that uncertainty can be eliminated. The required depth depends on novelty, complexity, consequence, and reversibility. The goal is to reach a maturity level at which remaining uncertainties are understood and can be managed during execution.
Study instruction:
- "Review a project initiation document against objective, scope, alternatives, dependencies, estimates, risks, governance, resources, and acceptance criteria. Record gaps that should block delivery."
Key point:
- A plan is a tested route to an outcome, not a collection of presentation documents.
- Readiness criteria should be proportionate to uncertainty and consequence.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
28. Q: What is the significance of Pixar’s development process in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses Pixar as an example of allowing substantial time for development before expensive production. Ed Catmull is cited as describing directors spending years in development, where scripts, storyboards, and ideas can be revised at comparatively low cost. Production is where costs rise sharply, so the studio benefits from resolving creative and structural problems earlier. The lesson is not that every project should copy a film studio’s timetable. It is that organizations should distinguish inexpensive experimentation from costly execution and allow teams to revise ideas without treating every early draft as final. This is useful when uncertainty can be reduced through iteration and learning materially improves the outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a project with uncertain requirements. Design a development cycle using prototypes, feedback, revision, and a clear decision before expensive rollout."
Key point:
- Iterative development can make later production more predictable.
- Allow ideas to change while the cost of change is still low.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
29. Q: What is the difference between a vision and a plan?
A: Show answer
A vision describes the desired future state; a plan explains how the project can credibly reach it. The chapter notes that a project may begin with a compelling image of what it could become, but that image is not yet a reliable route forward. A plan requires research, analysis, testing, detail, sequencing, resources, responsibilities, and an understanding of risks and constraints. Documents, charts, and estimates can create the appearance of planning without resolving the underlying questions. If the team cannot explain how major interfaces will work, what evidence supports its estimates, what must happen before each milestone, and how it will respond to failure, it may still have an aspiration rather than a plan.
Study instruction:
- "Convert a project vision into delivery logic: outcomes, work packages, dependencies, decision points, resources, evidence, and risks."
Key point:
- A vision states what is wanted; a plan demonstrates a credible path to achieve it.
- Planning documents do not prove that planning is complete.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
30. Q: Why does the chapter say that projects often “start wrong” rather than simply “go wrong”?
A: Show answer
The phrase shifts attention from late-stage symptoms to early decisions. Projects may enter delivery with incomplete designs, weak estimates, unresolved dependencies, unclear responsibilities, or untested assumptions. Once construction or implementation begins, these weaknesses emerge as delays, defects, cost increases, and corrective work. The resulting problems may look unexpected, but many follow from authorization before adequate preparation. This does not mean every failure is predictable or that external shocks are irrelevant. It means that the quality of the start strongly shapes the project’s ability to absorb uncertainty. A mature start includes a credible plan, a clear baseline, capable leadership, and explicit readiness criteria.
Study instruction:
- "For a troubled project, trace three delivery problems back to decisions or omissions made before execution began."
Key point:
- Many delivery crises originate in weak preparation and premature commitment.
- Investigate the quality of the start, not only events visible during execution.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
31. Q: What is the break-fix cycle, and why does it damage performance?
A: Show answer
The break-fix cycle begins when a project enters delivery with unresolved problems. Teams discover defects or missing decisions, stop or redirect work to repair them, and may create new problems through rushed changes or disrupted dependencies. More resources are spent diagnosing, patching, coordinating, and correcting instead of producing planned outputs. The cycle can undermine morale and trust because teams repeatedly compensate for earlier decisions. The chapter compares a project trapped in this cycle to a large animal stuck in a tar pit. Breaking the cycle requires identifying root causes, stabilizing design and interfaces, prioritizing high-impact defects, and restoring disciplined change control rather than treating every symptom as an isolated emergency.
Study instruction:
- "Map a break-fix sequence from a real project. Separate initiating planning defects, immediate symptoms, corrective actions, and secondary consequences."
Key point:
- Repeated rework is often evidence of an upstream planning problem.
- Fix root causes and stabilize the system rather than repeatedly patching symptoms.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
32. Q: What warning signs suggest that a project is entering delivery before it is ready?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include materially unstable scope, unresolved design choices, estimates based mainly on aspiration, incomplete site or user information, untested technology, uncoordinated contractor schedules, unclear acceptance criteria, missing permits, and risks without owners or response plans. Pressure to demonstrate visible progress—such as breaking ground or announcing a launch—before the team can explain the delivery logic is another warning. Readiness should be assessed through evidence, not confidence or document volume. Sponsors should ask whether critical assumptions have been tested, dependencies are understood, resources are available, and decisions can be executed without repeated redesign. Any exception should be explicit, approved, and accompanied by mitigation.
Study instruction:
- "Build a readiness checklist and identify the three gaps that would create the greatest cost, schedule, safety, or benefit risk if left unresolved."
Key point:
- Visible activity is not evidence of delivery readiness.
- Unresolved high-impact assumptions should be escalated before major commitments.
Source links:
- NASA Technical Risk Management
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
33. Q: How should teams manage interfaces between contractors, disciplines, and work packages?
A: Show answer
Interface management identifies where one party’s work depends on another party’s information, output, timing, access, or acceptance. The Great Belt incident shows why separate contracts will not coordinate themselves. An interface register should record the parties, item or condition exchanged, required dates, technical criteria, decision authority, acceptance evidence, and escalation route. Integrated schedules and coordinated drawings should include temporary works and maintenance conditions, not only the final design. Reviews should occur before dependent work starts and whenever scope or sequencing changes. The project manager remains accountable for the integrated outcome even when contractors are responsible for their own deliverables.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an interface register for three contractors or teams. Include an owner, required information, due date, acceptance evidence, and escalation route for each interface."
Key point:
- Interfaces must be actively owned and reviewed across organizational boundaries.
- A contractor’s local success does not guarantee integrated project success.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
34. Q: What is reference class forecasting, and how can it improve project estimates?
A: Show answer
Reference class forecasting estimates a project by comparing it with actual outcomes from a relevant group of completed, comparable projects. Rather than relying only on the team’s internal narrative, it asks what happened to projects with similar characteristics and uses that empirical distribution to adjust the forecast. It can counter optimism bias and strategic misrepresentation by introducing an external evidence base. A useful reference class must be broad enough to contain meaningful observations but similar enough to be relevant. The team should document selection criteria and adjustments for genuine differences. The result is an evidence-based range or distribution, not a precise prediction that removes uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Select a reference class for a proposed project. Define inclusion criteria, gather actual cost and duration outcomes, and explain how the proposal differs from the comparison group."
Key point:
- Use actual outcomes from comparable projects to challenge internal estimates.
- Document the reference class and adjustments so the forecast can be reviewed.
Source links:
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- HM Treasury: Optimism Bias Guidance
35. Q: How do optimism bias and strategic misrepresentation differ?
A: Show answer
Optimism bias is an unintentional tendency to believe plans will go better than experience warrants. Teams may underestimate cost and duration or overestimate benefits because they focus on a favorable project story and underweight difficulties. Strategic misrepresentation is a more deliberate presentation of forecasts in ways that improve approval prospects or protect a sponsor’s interests. The two can coexist, and a low estimate alone does not prove deception. Controls should combine better forecasting with transparent governance: use comparable-project data, record assumptions, separate estimate preparation from approval, obtain independent challenge, and preserve an audit trail. The purpose is to improve decision quality without assuming that every optimistic forecast is dishonest.
Study instruction:
- "Review a business case and identify statements that may reflect optimism bias, strategic incentives, or missing evidence. Propose a control for each."
Key point:
- Unintentional optimism and deliberate misrepresentation are distinct mechanisms.
- Independent evidence and transparent assumptions help address both.
Source links:
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- HM Treasury: Optimism Bias Guidance
36. Q: How should optimism-bias adjustments be used in project appraisal?
A: Show answer
HM Treasury guidance advises that, where robust primary evidence is absent, explicit adjustments should be made to estimates of cost, benefits, and duration using data from past or similar projects and adjusted for the project’s characteristics. An adjustment should not be applied mechanically without checking the applicable appraisal framework, project category, local evidence, and current guidance. As design and risk analysis mature, the estimate can be updated when documented evidence shows that specific sources of bias or risk have been addressed. Optimism-bias adjustments are not a substitute for identifying individual risks and should not be double-counted with contingencies or other uplifts.
Study instruction:
- "Find the optimism-bias guidance applicable to a project in your jurisdiction. Record the category, evidence base, adjustment method, and controls against double-counting."
Key point:
- Adjust forecasts using evidence when project-specific data are weak.
- Use applicable guidance and document how adjustments are updated.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: Optimism Bias Guidance
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
37. Q: Why is a project contingency not the same as a realistic forecast?
A: Show answer
A forecast estimates what the project is likely to require under stated assumptions; contingency is a provision for uncertainty or specified risks, depending on the methodology. Adding a generic buffer to an unrealistic estimate does not correct weak scope, schedule, or technical assumptions. A reserve also does not automatically protect against fat-tailed outcomes, because extreme losses may exceed a conventional allowance. Teams should develop a defensible base estimate, identify risk exposure, model uncertainty, and state the confidence level associated with total funding. They should define who controls reserve release and what evidence is needed. Budgeting and approval treatment varies by organization and contract, so applicable policy must be checked.
Study instruction:
- "Separate a project’s base estimate, identified-risk allowance, management reserve, and total funding request. State the purpose and approval authority for each."
Key point:
- A buffer cannot repair a weak estimate or untested plan.
- Define reserve purpose, risk coverage, governance, and release criteria explicitly.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: Optimism Bias Guidance
- NASA Technical Risk Management
38. Q: What role should independent review and decision gates play before major commitments?
A: Show answer
Independent review challenges assumptions and evidence that the project team may be too close to see objectively. A decision gate gives the sponsor a formal choice to proceed, request more work, change design, defer, or stop. Reviewers should examine the business case, scope, estimate, schedule, technical maturity, dependencies, benefits, risk exposure, and readiness evidence. Review should be sufficiently independent of people whose reputation depends on approval. Gates are not paperwork exercises: unresolved critical issues should lead to a defined decision, owner, and due date. Formality should be proportionate to project risk and applicable governance rules.
Study instruction:
- "Design a pre-delivery gate with mandatory evidence, reviewer independence, decision options, escalation criteria, and a record of unresolved exceptions."
Key point:
- Independent challenge exposes weak assumptions before they become expensive.
- A gate must have authority to defer or stop, not merely endorse progress.
Source links:
- NASA Technical Risk Management
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
39. Q: How should a project risk register describe a risk so that it supports action?
A: Show answer
A useful risk statement identifies an initiating condition, an uncertain event or sequence, and the consequence for project objectives. NASA characterizes risk through a scenario, its likelihood, and the consequence or severity of the resulting performance shortfall. A register should therefore avoid vague entries such as “supplier risk” and instead explain how a supplier failure could affect a component, schedule, cost, or operational requirement. Each material risk needs an owner, evidence source, mitigation, contingency, trigger threshold, monitoring frequency, and escalation route. Risks should be reviewed as assumptions and circumstances change; they should not remain static entries created only for a planning document.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite five vague risks as cause-event-effect statements. Add an owner, likelihood basis, impact, mitigation, trigger, and review date to each."
Key point:
- Describe a risk as a credible scenario with likelihood and consequence.
- Assign ownership, actions, triggers, and monitoring rather than merely recording a label.
Source links:
- NASA Technical Risk Management
40. Q: How should leaders monitor risks and decide when to activate mitigation or contingency plans?
A: Show answer
Monitoring should follow the risk plan and become more frequent when a risk approaches a defined trigger. Leaders should track indicators that provide warning before the consequence occurs, report status to the appropriate management body, and confirm that mitigation is reducing exposure. A trigger should specify what evidence requires action, who has authority, what resources are available, and how stakeholders will be informed. When a risk materializes, the team should activate the contingency, assess secondary effects, update the schedule and forecast, and document decisions. NASA guidance emphasizes periodic monitoring, escalation near thresholds, implementation of mitigation when triggered, and communication of residual risk and results.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one major risk and define an early-warning indicator, trigger, response owner, contingency action, reporting recipient, and post-action review."
Key point:
- Risk monitoring must lead to timely decisions and action.
- Triggers and response authority should be agreed before the risk materializes.
Source links:
- NASA Technical Risk Management
41. Q: What should a sponsor do when continuing a troubled project may be worse than stopping or restarting?
A: Show answer
The sponsor should reassess intended benefits, remaining cost and time, technical feasibility, safety, contractual obligations, and realistic alternatives. Past expenditure is a sunk cost: it matters for accountability but should not by itself justify spending more. The Great Belt account describes engineers recommending abandonment and restart after severe flooding, while political considerations favored continuing. The lesson is that embarrassment, reputation, and prior commitment can distort rational decisions. A formal review should compare continuation, redesign, suspension, termination, and restart using forward-looking costs and benefits. Legal, contractual, safety, and public obligations must be considered, and the decision should be documented with independent challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a continuation-versus-stop note using future costs, benefits, risks, contractual consequences, and feasible alternatives. Identify sunk costs separately."
Key point:
- Do not let past expenditure or embarrassment determine future investment.
- Use transparent, forward-looking evidence and independent review for major reset decisions.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- NASA Technical Risk Management
42. Q: How can a project team apply the chapter’s lessons to a home renovation or small initiative?
A: Show answer
The same logic applies at smaller scale: define the outcome, investigate existing conditions, obtain specialist input where needed, compare options, test critical assumptions, and agree scope and acceptance criteria before major expenditure. Identify dependencies such as permits, materials, contractor availability, access, utilities, and temporary accommodation. Obtain estimates that explain exclusions and uncertainty rather than relying on one optimistic number. Agree how changes will be priced and approved, retain records, and set aside a reserve based on exposure and the sponsor’s ability to absorb loss. Small sponsors should be especially careful because they may lack the borrowing capacity or financial reserves available to governments and large corporations.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a one-page plan for a renovation or small project covering objective, scope, site checks, suppliers, dependencies, budget range, schedule, risks, and change approvals."
Key point:
- Project discipline scales down; the mechanisms of failure do not disappear.
- Limited financial resilience makes early investigation especially valuable for small sponsors.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- HM Treasury: Optimism Bias Guidance
43. Q: How can an organization learn systematically from successful and unsuccessful projects?
A: Show answer
Learning requires more than a closure workshop. The organization should retain comparable baseline and actual data, investigate variance, record which assumptions proved accurate or inaccurate, and connect lessons to future estimating, design, procurement, governance, and training. It should study successful projects as well as failures because success can reveal effective planning and delivery practices. Findings should change templates, reference classes, risk models, review questions, and capability development. A database is useful only if projects contribute consistent information and future teams can retrieve and apply it. Learning should be owned by a function with authority to update standards and verify whether changes improve performance.
Study instruction:
- "Create a lessons-learned process with required data fields, root-cause review, accountable owner, standard or tool to update, and a later check of whether the lesson was applied."
Key point:
- Capture both forecast errors and effective practices in reusable form.
- A lesson is complete only when it changes future decisions or controls.
Source links:
- What You Should Know About Megaprojects and Why
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
44. Q: How can the chapter’s ideas be combined into a practical start-to-delivery method?
A: Show answer
Define the outcome and evidence that will demonstrate its value. Establish scope and compare alternatives before selecting a solution. Gather comparable-project data, challenge internal forecasts, and model the distribution of cost and schedule outcomes rather than relying only on one estimate. Map technical, contractual, organizational, and external dependencies; identify cascading scenarios; and assign owners, triggers, mitigations, and contingencies. Use prototypes, simulations, site investigations, and stakeholder reviews to test material assumptions while change is affordable. At a formal gate, confirm that the plan is credible enough for delivery. Then execute with disciplined coordination, prompt issue resolution, transparent reporting, safety and quality assurance, and controlled change. Review benefits after completion and feed actual outcomes into future forecasts.
Study instruction:
- "Apply this sequence to a real project and prepare an initiation pack covering outcome, options, reference-class evidence, risk map, readiness gate, delivery controls, and benefit review."
Key point:
- Build a tested plan before major irreversible commitments.
- Use transparent evidence and active risk management from initiation through benefits review.
Source links:
- How Big Things Get Done — publisher overview
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- NASA Technical Risk Management
