Table 2
The Commitment Fallacy in Major Project Decisions
1. Q: What is the commitment fallacy in major project planning?
A: Show answer
The commitment fallacy is the tendency to commit prematurely to a project definition, design, site, supplier, budget, schedule, or delivery route before the purpose, alternatives, uncertainties, and consequences have been examined adequately. It is not the same as making a clear decision after disciplined appraisal. The problem is that an early choice is treated as settled while important information is still missing. Once people announce, approve, fund, contract for, or begin implementing the choice, they acquire psychological, political, financial, and reputational reasons to defend it. The project then becomes harder to improve or abandon even when better options emerge. In Chapter 2, the Pentagon's first proposed site illustrates how an urgent decision can be mistaken for a sufficiently informed decision. The chapter's corrective principle is to commit first to completing a rigorous learning and appraisal process, not to a preferred solution.
Study instruction:
- "Define the commitment fallacy and identify one decision in a current or past project that became difficult to revisit before the evidence was complete."
Key point:
- Premature commitment closes alternatives before uncertainty has been examined.
- The first commitment should be to disciplined inquiry, not to a predetermined solution.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- The Green Book (2026)
2. Q: Why must project leaders eventually commit, yet avoid committing too early?
A: Show answer
Projects cannot be delivered through endless analysis. At some point, an authorized decision-maker must select an option, allocate resources, assign responsibilities, and authorize implementation. The distinction is between a decision made after sufficient evidence and one made to create the appearance of momentum. Early commitment is dangerous when the project purpose is vague, options have not been compared, estimates are weak, or important stakeholders have not tested the proposal. Waiting has a cost too, so analysis should be proportionate to scale, complexity, risk, and reversibility. A small reversible pilot may justify a quick decision; a costly site choice or long-term construction contract usually requires deeper appraisal. The practical objective is not to eliminate uncertainty before acting, which is impossible, but to reduce avoidable uncertainty and preserve flexibility where evidence is incomplete.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five project decisions as readily reversible, costly to reverse, or effectively irreversible, and specify the level of appraisal each deserves."
Key point:
- Commitment is necessary, but its timing and evidential basis matter.
- The more irreversible and consequential a decision, the stronger the case for deliberate analysis.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
3. Q: What does the Pentagon's initial site-selection story demonstrate?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts how General Brehon Somervell rapidly advanced an initial Pentagon plan for Arlington Farm, a site that appeared to satisfy immediate engineering and space requirements. The design followed the irregular boundary of the land, producing a misshapen building and threatening a valued view from Arlington National Cemetery. A technically suitable alternative at the Quartermaster Depot site was later identified. Moving the project there allowed a symmetrical design and avoided the principal objection to the original location. The lesson is not that speed is always wrong or that aesthetic considerations must always override operational need. It is that the initial plan had been treated as the only plausible solution without a sufficiently broad search or comparison. The official Department of Defense history confirms the Pentagon's construction in 1941–43 and its rapid delivery, while the chapter supplies the detailed decision narrative used for this lesson.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a short timeline of the initial site proposal, review, challenge, and relocation; identify when a wider options analysis should have occurred."
Key point:
- A solution can satisfy immediate requirements and still be inferior to an unexamined alternative.
- Urgency does not remove the need to test location, design, function, cost, and wider impacts.
Source links:
- Pentagon History
- The Green Book (2026)
4. Q: Why did the first apparently suitable Pentagon site become treated as the only suitable site?
A: Show answer
The decision process narrowed prematurely. The first site was rejected when staff found it unsuitable, but the next site that appeared workable was accepted without a systematic search for other candidates. Once the new site had a design, a plan, and senior approvals, it acquired momentum. People then evaluated the plan as if its basic assumptions were settled rather than asking whether the site itself was best. This is a common cognitive pattern: the option that comes to mind first, or the first option that passes a minimum test, becomes the anchor for later reasoning. It is reinforced by time pressure and the desire to demonstrate progress. A sound selection process should define mandatory constraints, generate a broad longlist, apply transparent criteria, and preserve evidence about why alternatives were rejected.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a site or location longlist for a hypothetical project and record the evidence supporting each inclusion or exclusion."
Key point:
- Passing a minimum suitability test does not establish that an option is optimal.
- A documented longlist helps prevent the first workable option from becoming the default.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
5. Q: What is lock-in, and how can it begin before a formal project approval?
A: Show answer
Lock-in occurs when a project or decision path becomes difficult to change even though alternatives remain theoretically available. It can begin before a formal investment decision through public announcements, political endorsements, design work, land purchases, exclusive negotiations, consultant appointments, or internal promises. These actions create expectations and make reversal appear costly or embarrassing. The formal approval date may therefore understate how early the organization became committed in practice. Research on large transport projects distinguishes lock-in at the decision-making stage from lock-in during delivery and identifies sunk time and money, self-justification, inflexibility, and closure of alternatives as warning signs. Leaders should track not only formal approvals but also informal commitments and actions that constrain future choices.
Study instruction:
- "Map a project's formal and informal commitment points, including announcements, contracts, expenditure, and public promises."
Key point:
- Lock-in can precede the official decision to build.
- Monitor the commitments that narrow options, not just the approval register.
Source links:
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
6. Q: How does premature lock-in differ from an ordinary project commitment?
A: Show answer
An ordinary commitment is a deliberate authorization made after the decision-maker has considered the objective, feasible options, costs, benefits, risks, and implementation capacity. It includes a clear rationale and a process for responding to material new evidence. Premature lock-in occurs when the project is effectively treated as irreversible before those matters have been adequately examined. The distinction is not simply the amount already spent. A project may have spent little but still be politically locked in after a public promise, while another may have spent substantial sums yet retain genuine options to stop or redesign. The diagnostic question is whether decision-makers are still willing and able to compare the current path with alternatives on their merits. If they defend continuation mainly because the project has already been announced or started, lock-in may be operating.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two projects with different levels of expenditure and identify which is more locked in based on actual freedom to change course."
Key point:
- Lock-in is about constrained choice and defensive commitment, not expenditure alone.
- A sound commitment retains explicit procedures for reconsidering the decision when evidence changes.
Source links:
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
7. Q: What is strategic misrepresentation in project planning?
A: Show answer
Strategic misrepresentation is the deliberate distortion, selective presentation, or understatement of information to secure approval, funding, a contract, or political support. It differs from an honest forecasting error because the planner or sponsor knowingly presents a more attractive picture than the evidence justifies. Examples include suppressing difficult site conditions, excluding foreseeable costs, presenting optimistic schedules as central estimates, overstating benefits, or postponing disclosure of material risks until after the project is difficult to stop. Flyvbjerg's research treats this as a political or organizational bias that can coexist with genuine optimism bias. It should not be inferred from every overrun: a variance alone does not prove intentional deception. Evidence about incentives, internal forecasts, omitted information, and the treatment of contrary analysis is needed before making that judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Review a sample business case and distinguish unsupported optimism, innocent error, and evidence that might indicate deliberate distortion."
Key point:
- Strategic misrepresentation is intentional, whereas optimism bias is generally unintentional.
- A poor forecast is a warning sign, not by itself proof of deception.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
8. Q: Why can an unrealistically low estimate help a project win approval?
A: Show answer
A low initial cost or short schedule can make a proposal appear affordable, deliverable, and superior to competing investments. Sponsors may know that decision-makers are more likely to approve a project when the first estimate fits a political budget or promised deadline. If the true cost is disclosed later, the project may already have contracts, public support, construction activity, and sunk expenditure behind it. The sponsor has then shifted the decision from whether the project is worthwhile to how much more must be spent to finish it. This creates an incentive to understate costs or risks at the approval stage and rely on later escalation. The chapter illustrates this with public-project estimates and the observation that an initial budget may function as a political entry price rather than a realistic forecast. Independent scrutiny and transparent uncertainty ranges counter this incentive.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed project, compare the approval estimate with a technically supported estimate and identify who benefits from any gap."
Key point:
- An attractive approval estimate can be strategically useful even when it is not realistic.
- Early transparency prevents later funding decisions from being made under artificial lock-in.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- The Green Book (2026)
9. Q: How can strategic misrepresentation be distinguished from an honest forecasting error?
A: Show answer
An honest forecasting error arises when estimates are prepared in good faith but uncertainty, complexity, missing data, or unexpected events cause actual results to differ. Strategic misrepresentation involves deliberate distortion or selective omission to influence a decision. Distinguishing them requires examining the forecasting process and information available at the time, not merely comparing the original budget with final cost. Relevant evidence includes alternative estimates, risk registers, technical reviews, internal correspondence, assumptions that were excluded, changes between internal and public figures, and whether contrary expert advice was suppressed. Repeatedly optimistic estimates under strong approval pressure may justify closer investigation, but they do not prove intent on their own. An independent review should document the evidence, allow responsible parties to respond, and separate governance findings from allegations of misconduct.
Study instruction:
- "Build an evidence checklist for investigating a major estimate variance without presuming that deception occurred."
Key point:
- Intent cannot be inferred from an overrun alone.
- Compare the information known at approval with what was disclosed to decision-makers.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
10. Q: How do psychological bias and political incentives interact in major projects?
A: Show answer
Psychological bias can lead people to believe their own optimistic story, while political and organizational incentives can reward presenting that story to others. A sponsor may genuinely expect success because of overconfidence, the inside view, or selective attention to favorable evidence. At the same time, the sponsor may face pressure to secure funding, preserve reputation, satisfy a minister, win a contract, or outcompete another proposal. These forces reinforce each other: optimistic assumptions become convenient to the sponsor, and deliberate selection of favorable information further strengthens confidence. Flyvbjerg's review argues that the relative influence depends on the level of stakes and organizational pressure. In low-pressure settings cognitive bias may be more prominent; in consequential projects strategic misrepresentation may become more important, without eliminating cognitive bias. Controls must therefore address both judgment quality and incentives.
Study instruction:
- "Identify psychological and political pressures affecting one project decision and propose one control for each type."
Key point:
- Cognitive bias and strategic incentives are not mutually exclusive explanations.
- Good controls challenge both the forecast and the incentives shaping it.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
11. Q: What is optimism bias, and how does it affect project estimates?
A: Show answer
Optimism bias is the tendency to expect more favorable outcomes than the evidence warrants. In project appraisal it commonly appears as underestimation of cost, duration, and risk, together with overestimation of benefits. It can affect experienced experts as well as novices because the preferred future scenario feels plausible and salient. The resulting forecast may omit delays, design development, site constraints, stakeholder objections, procurement problems, inflation, or operational costs. The UK's 2026 Green Book defines optimism bias as a systematic tendency to be over-optimistic about key appraisal assumptions and requires explicit adjustments to costs, duration, and benefits using historical forecast-error evidence where available. These adjustments should be evidence-based, not arbitrary padding, and should be updated as the project matures and specific risks are better understood.
Study instruction:
- "Review a project estimate and identify assumptions that may systematically understate costs or time and overstate benefits."
Key point:
- Optimism bias affects costs, schedules, risks, and expected benefits.
- Use evidence-based adjustments rather than relying on confidence or intuition.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
12. Q: Why is optimism useful for project leadership but dangerous in forecasting?
A: Show answer
Optimism can motivate people to attempt difficult work, attract support, sustain effort, and persist when setbacks occur. It becomes dangerous when motivation is substituted for analysis. A leader can be confident that a project is worth pursuing while still requiring conservative, evidence-based estimates of cost, time, and risk. The chapter's flight-attendant-versus-pilot metaphor makes the distinction: optimism may help maintain morale, but the person responsible for safety and resource decisions must know the actual fuel position. In project terms, the team may be ambitious about the outcome while the forecast explicitly accounts for failure modes and adverse scenarios. A realistic optimist communicates both the intended result and the evidence about what could prevent it.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite an overly confident project statement into two parts: an inspiring objective and a cautious, evidence-based forecast."
Key point:
- Optimism can energize execution but should not determine the estimate.
- Separate ambition about outcomes from realism about resources and risk.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
13. Q: What are System 1 and System 2 thinking, and why do they matter for project decisions?
A: Show answer
System 1 and System 2 are labels for two broad modes of judgment. System 1 refers to fast, intuitive, largely automatic responses based on patterns and experience. System 2 refers to slower, effortful reasoning that can compare alternatives, test assumptions, calculate consequences, and reconsider an initial judgment. They are explanatory models rather than two literal brain compartments. System 1 usually supplies an immediate answer; System 2 must be engaged deliberately and may not challenge the first impression unless time, attention, and incentives permit. In familiar situations, rapid intuition can be effective. In unfamiliar, complex, high-cost, or irreversible project decisions, relying on the first answer is risky. Formal appraisal, independent challenge, and explicit decision records help ensure that slower analysis is actually performed.
Study instruction:
- "Take one recent decision and record the initial intuitive answer, the additional evidence considered, and whether deliberate analysis changed the choice."
Key point:
- Fast intuition is automatic; careful analysis requires deliberate effort.
- High-stakes unfamiliar decisions need structured scrutiny beyond the first impression.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
14. Q: What does WYSIATI mean, and how can it distort a project decision?
A: Show answer
WYSIATI means What You See Is All There Is. It describes the tendency to treat the information currently available as if it were the full information set relevant to a decision. In project planning, the team may have a site plan, a preliminary estimate, and a delivery deadline and feel that it understands the problem. It may fail to ask what is missing: alternative sites, land constraints, community impacts, hidden dependencies, operating costs, comparable project outcomes, or views from people outside the sponsor's team. A coherent story can feel complete even when it is based on a narrow sample. The remedy is to make missing information visible by asking what has not been tested, who has not been consulted, which assumptions lack evidence, and what alternatives have not been considered.
Study instruction:
- "Create a WYSIATI challenge sheet for a business case with separate lists of known facts, assumptions, missing evidence, and untested alternatives."
Key point:
- Available information is not necessarily complete information.
- Ask systematically what is absent from the project narrative.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
15. Q: When is intuitive first-option decision-making useful, and when is it unsuitable?
A: Show answer
Intuitive selection can be useful when a person has substantial experience in a stable environment, the situation is familiar, feedback is timely, and a quick decision is needed. It can also be useful for reversible choices where experimentation is inexpensive. It is less reliable when the problem is novel, the consequences are large, feedback is delayed, many stakeholders are involved, or the decision is difficult to reverse. Somervell's rapid choice of the first workable Pentagon site illustrates the danger of applying a familiar action-oriented approach to a problem for which the decision-maker lacked relevant local and building experience. In such cases, a minimum viable process should still define objectives, generate alternatives, obtain expert input, and test the leading option before approval.
Study instruction:
- "Assess three decisions using familiarity, feedback quality, time pressure, consequence, and reversibility to decide whether intuition is sufficient."
Key point:
- Intuition is context-dependent rather than universally good or bad.
- Novel and irreversible decisions require wider search and explicit comparison.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
16. Q: What is the planning fallacy?
A: Show answer
The planning fallacy is the tendency to underestimate the time and resources needed to complete a task or project, even when people have relevant experience showing that similar work usually takes longer. In project settings, the concept is often broadened to include understated costs and risks and overstated benefits. People focus on the particular plan they intend to execute and imagine a smooth sequence of activities, rather than using the distribution of actual outcomes from comparable projects. The result is an estimate that resembles a best-case scenario. The planning fallacy can affect personal tasks and complex capital projects, although the scale and incentives differ. It is a systematic forecasting problem, not simply a lack of effort or an isolated arithmetic mistake.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an original schedule with actual completion data from similar tasks and calculate the typical variance."
Key point:
- The planning fallacy makes estimates too close to an imagined smooth path.
- Past outcomes are essential evidence for future time and cost estimates.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- The Green Book (2026)
17. Q: What is Hofstadter's Law, and how should project teams use the idea?
A: Show answer
Hofstadter's Law is the humorous observation that work takes longer than expected even when one has already allowed for the fact that it will take longer than expected. It captures the persistence of underestimation and the tendency for planners to believe that the current estimate has finally corrected earlier mistakes. It is not a mathematical rule or a substitute for data. Its practical value is as a reminder that adding a small arbitrary buffer may not cure a forecast built on optimistic assumptions. Teams should examine actual durations, dependency chains, resource constraints, review cycles, procurement lead times, rework, and external approvals. They should also test the schedule at explicit confidence levels and update it when new evidence emerges.
Study instruction:
- "Select a recurring activity and compare three successive estimates with actual completion times; explain why previous corrections did or did not work."
Key point:
- A generic buffer cannot replace evidence-based forecasting.
- Repeated underestimation should trigger a change in method, not another optimistic promise.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
18. Q: Why do planners ignore their own past experience when estimating future work?
A: Show answer
Several mechanisms contribute. The past may not come readily to mind when attention is focused on the proposed future. Even when relevant examples are remembered, planners may dismiss them by saying that this project is different. People may also avoid the effort required to retrieve records, compare scope, and examine why prior estimates failed. In addition, the team may imagine the work itself but omit interruptions, dependencies, approvals, site conditions, procurement, rework, or competing demands. The chapter emphasizes that repeated personal experience can be disregarded even when it is highly relevant. Organizations should make actual cost and schedule data accessible, maintain consistent definitions, conduct post-project reviews, and require forecasts to explain differences from comparable outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Collect actual data from at least five completed projects and list the reasons their outcomes differed from initial plans."
Key point:
- Experience helps only when it is retrieved and used in the estimate.
- Project learning requires reliable records and explicit comparison with past outcomes.
Source links:
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
19. Q: Why are best-case scenarios a poor basis for a project estimate?
A: Show answer
A best-case scenario describes a favorable path in which complications are absent or minimal. It may be possible, but it is rarely the most likely outcome and may have a low probability of occurring. A project can be affected by a large range of events: design changes, supply delays, ground conditions, legal challenges, stakeholder objections, staff turnover, inflation, weather, and unexpected dependencies. If the estimate assumes that none of these materializes, it is not a balanced forecast. The chapter notes that people often produce a best-case scenario even when asked for their best guess. A stronger method identifies the range of plausible outcomes, estimates their likelihood using evidence, and shows what resources are needed at different confidence levels.
Study instruction:
- "Build a three-scenario estimate for one project: favorable, central, and adverse; state the assumptions and evidence behind each."
Key point:
- Best case is not synonymous with most likely case.
- Forecasts should reflect a distribution of plausible outcomes and their uncertainty.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
20. Q: What is the inside view, and why can it create unrealistic estimates?
A: Show answer
The inside view focuses on the specific project plan, its tasks, and the team's narrative about how the work will unfold. It asks how this project will be completed, often by breaking work into activities and estimating each one. This can be useful for understanding scope and building an execution plan, but it becomes biased when planners ignore how comparable projects actually performed. The team may assume that its own project will avoid the delays and overruns seen elsewhere because its circumstances appear distinctive. The inside view is especially vulnerable to missing compound effects and unknown risks that are not visible in the task breakdown. It should be supplemented, not discarded, by the outside view and historical performance data.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an inside-view schedule for a project, then annotate every assumption that lacks a comparable historical benchmark."
Key point:
- Detailed task logic does not guarantee realistic forecasts.
- Use the inside view for project structure and the outside view for calibration.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
21. Q: What is the outside view, and how does it improve project appraisal?
A: Show answer
The outside view asks how comparable projects actually performed rather than relying only on the current team's story about this project. It uses historical outcomes to estimate the likely range of cost, schedule, demand, or benefit performance. The comparison helps reveal risks that may be overlooked in a detailed plan and counters the assumption that the current project is uniquely exempt from common difficulties. The UK guidance on reference class forecasting describes selecting comparable completed projects, establishing an outcome distribution, and adjusting the current estimate. The outside view does not remove the need for project-specific engineering or risk analysis. It adds an empirical check on the assumptions generated by the inside view.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a relevant population of completed projects and compare its cost and schedule outcomes with the current proposal."
Key point:
- The outside view anchors forecasts in observed outcomes, not only intentions.
- Use it alongside detailed project analysis rather than as a replacement for it.
Source links:
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
22. Q: How is reference class forecasting carried out?
A: Show answer
Reference class forecasting is an outside-view method that calibrates a forecast using the actual performance of comparable completed projects. A practical sequence is to select a relevant reference class, collect consistent outcome data, establish the distribution of cost or schedule variation, and apply an evidence-based adjustment to the current estimate. The Homes England guidance describes these three steps and notes that a starting sample of around 20–30 projects may be useful, with larger samples preferred where available. The adjustment should be applied to a clearly defined base estimate and should not duplicate risk allowances already included. The method can reveal aggregate effects, including risks that were not individually identified in the current project's risk register. Data quality and comparability remain essential.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a small reference-class dataset, calculate outcome percentiles, and show how it would adjust a base estimate."
Key point:
- Reference class forecasting converts past project outcomes into an empirical adjustment.
- A transparent dataset and clear baseline are necessary for a defensible forecast.
Source links:
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
23. Q: How should a project team choose a suitable reference class?
A: Show answer
A reference class should be broad enough to include relevant experience but similar enough to support meaningful comparison. Candidate projects may be grouped by asset type, delivery method, size, geography, complexity, procurement model, technology, or stage of maturity. Teams should avoid selecting only successful or unusually similar examples, because that can reproduce the bias the method is intended to correct. The Homes England guidance emphasizes statistical comparability and alignment of project maturity: estimates prepared at an early business-case stage should be compared with estimates from other projects at a similar stage, because uncertainty changes as projects mature. Where the dataset is small, the limitations should be disclosed and wider evidence considered. Adjustments for unique features should be explicit and evidence-based.
Study instruction:
- "Write selection criteria for a reference class and explain why each included project is comparable and each excluded project is not."
Key point:
- Reference classes must balance breadth with genuine comparability.
- Do not cherry-pick comparators or mix estimates from materially different maturity stages.
Source links:
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
24. Q: How should project teams interpret P50, P80, and other forecast percentiles?
A: Show answer
A percentile expresses a position in an observed or modelled distribution. In a cost-overrun distribution, a P50 estimate is the level at or below which half of the observed outcomes fall, while a P80 is a more conservative level at or below which 80 percent fall, subject to the distribution's orientation and the precise convention used. The percentile is not a guarantee that the project will finish at that cost. It describes a confidence position based on the underlying data and assumptions. Decision-makers should select a confidence level consistent with their risk appetite and the consequences of exceeding the budget, and should understand the residual probability of overrun. Percentiles should be accompanied by the sample, baseline, date, and sensitivity analysis so that they are not mistaken for precise certainty.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what P50 and P80 mean for a project estimate and identify the residual risk each leaves above the selected value."
Key point:
- A percentile describes a position in a distribution, not a guaranteed outcome.
- Choose confidence levels transparently and disclose the risk that remains.
Source links:
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- The Green Book (2026)
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
25. Q: How can optimism-bias adjustments be combined with project-specific risk analysis without double counting?
A: Show answer
Optimism-bias adjustments and project-specific risk analysis address related but not identical forecasting weaknesses. A reference-class or generic adjustment captures systematic forecast error and aggregate effects observed in comparable projects. A project risk register identifies specific threats and opportunities in the current proposal. If both are applied without reconciliation, the same risk may be counted twice. The UK Green Book explains that as specific risks are identified and prevented or mitigated, the optimism-bias adjustment can be reduced in proportion to the risk prevented; residual risks and the remaining adjustment inform contingency. Teams should document the base estimate, risk allowances already included, the source of the uplift, mitigation evidence, and the final contingency calculation. The applicable sponsor or funder methodology should govern the precise treatment.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a base estimate, a risk register, a reference-class uplift, and contingency; flag any duplicated risk allowance."
Key point:
- Do not add allowances mechanically without checking for overlap.
- Document how specific mitigation changes the residual optimism-bias adjustment.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
26. Q: What makes a project cost estimate comprehensive, well documented, accurate, and credible?
A: Show answer
A robust estimate should cover the full scope and lifecycle, identify its assumptions and exclusions, use appropriate data and methods, and communicate uncertainty. Comprehensive means that all material cost elements are included. Well documented means that sources, calculations, scope, assumptions, exclusions, and changes can be traced. Accurate means that the estimate is based on the best available information and is updated as the project develops. Credible means that the estimate is realistic, tested against evidence, and accompanied by an explanation of its confidence and sensitivity to key assumptions. GAO's review of Architect of the Capitol estimates highlights that comprehensive documentation alone is not enough: missing confidence levels, risk analysis, and use of actual costs from completed phases can undermine credibility. Independent review should test all four characteristics.
Study instruction:
- "Score a project estimate against the four characteristics and identify the evidence needed to close each gap."
Key point:
- A detailed spreadsheet is not necessarily a reliable estimate.
- Credibility requires uncertainty analysis, historical evidence, and traceable assumptions.
Source links:
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
- The Green Book (2026)
27. Q: What is meant by a bias against thinking in project organizations?
A: Show answer
A bias against thinking occurs when visible action is valued more highly than analysis, planning, and questioning. Managers may feel productive when tasks are underway and regard appraisal as delay or bureaucracy. Under time pressure, this preference can become an organizational norm: teams are praised for starting quickly while those who challenge assumptions are seen as obstructive. The chapter distinguishes this from a sensible bias for action in reversible decisions. In major projects, early decisions can determine location, technology, contracts, and scope for years, so inadequate thinking can create much more delay later. Organizations should recognize planning outputs as real project progress and evaluate the quality of decisions, not simply the speed at which work begins.
Study instruction:
- "Review a project culture and identify what behaviors receive recognition: early activity, sound analysis, risk disclosure, or decision quality."
Key point:
- Visible activity is not always meaningful progress.
- Organizations should reward disciplined planning as part of delivery.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
28. Q: When is a bias for action appropriate, and when does it become dangerous?
A: Show answer
A bias for action is appropriate when the decision is reversible, the cost of trying is low, feedback arrives quickly, and experimentation can improve the result. A team can test a prototype, trial a workflow, or pilot a service and reverse course if the evidence is unfavorable. The chapter invokes the distinction in Amazon's leadership principle: many decisions are reversible and do not need extensive study. The danger comes when this caveat is lost and the same slogan is applied to decisions that are costly or impossible to undo. A site acquisition, long-term concession, major construction contract, or technology architecture can create lock-in. Decision-makers should classify reversibility explicitly and scale the depth of appraisal accordingly.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one reversible and one irreversible decision from a project and design different approval processes for each."
Key point:
- Fast experimentation is valuable when reversal is affordable.
- Irreversible choices require stronger evidence and governance before action.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
29. Q: Why does the chapter describe planning as working on the project rather than delaying the project?
A: Show answer
Planning determines what should be built, why it is needed, which option best meets the objective, what resources are required, and how delivery risks will be managed. A flawed early decision can create expensive redesign, disputes, delays, and rework once construction or implementation begins. In contrast, time spent testing options and assumptions can prevent a much larger commitment to the wrong solution. The chapter argues that planning is not administrative activity outside the project; it is one of the project's core workstreams. The Green Book similarly treats appraisal as a structured stage of proposal development that examines objectives, alternatives, costs, benefits, risks, and uncertainty before a preferred option is selected. Planning should be proportionate, evidence-led, and time-bound.
Study instruction:
- "List the decisions and deliverables produced during the planning phase and explain how each reduces downstream rework or risk."
Key point:
- Planning creates project value by improving the decision before resources are locked in.
- Measure planning progress by evidence and resolved uncertainty, not by paperwork volume.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
30. Q: What does the phrase start digging a hole mean in the context of strategic project lock-in?
A: Show answer
The phrase describes a tactic of starting work quickly and creating enough contractual, financial, physical, or reputational commitment that decision-makers feel unable to stop. Once contractors are mobilized, materials ordered, sets built, land acquired, or public promises made, cancellation becomes more difficult. The project sponsor may then argue that the organization must continue because stopping would waste what has already been spent. This can shift attention away from whether the remaining investment is worthwhile. The tactic may be deliberate, as in the chapter's account of film production, or emerge through ordinary pressure to show progress. Controls include staged authorization, cancellation rights, limits on early irreversible expenditure, and independent review before major commitments.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the first activities that would create lock-in in a proposed project and specify which should require separate authorization."
Key point:
- Early expenditure and contracts can be used to make a project hard to cancel.
- Stage commitments so that each step remains justified by current evidence.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
31. Q: How did the film Heaven's Gate illustrate the dangers of early commitment and escalation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how the studio approved an ambitious film based on a proposed budget and schedule, then became increasingly committed after production began. Early production performance was already far behind plan, yet spending continued and the director used contractual leverage to resist demands for tighter control. As the production expanded, the studio faced growing financial and reputational costs of stopping. The final project cost far more and arrived late, while the film's commercial failure damaged the studio. The case illustrates how an unrealistic initial plan can be followed by sunk costs, contractual leverage, and reluctance to admit that the original assumptions were wrong. It also shows why early warning indicators should trigger independent review before the organization becomes trapped.
Study instruction:
- "Create an early-warning dashboard for a creative or construction project, including schedule variance, burn rate, output quality, and contractual exposure."
Key point:
- Severe variance in the first delivery period should trigger scrutiny of the original assumptions.
- Contracts and sunk costs can strengthen lock-in when performance deteriorates.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
32. Q: What is the sunk cost fallacy?
A: Show answer
The sunk cost fallacy is the tendency to let unrecoverable past expenditure, time, or effort influence a decision about whether to commit additional resources. A sunk cost is already incurred and cannot be recovered by continuing or stopping. The rational decision about the next step should compare the future costs, future benefits, risks, and available alternatives from the present point forward. People nevertheless feel pressure to justify previous expenditure, avoid admitting failure, or protect the identity and reputation attached to the project. The chapter's snowstorm-and-basketball-ticket example shows how a person may take additional risk because of the ticket price already paid. In organizations, the same pattern can lead to repeated funding of a failing project.
Study instruction:
- "For a project facing a new funding request, separate sunk costs from all prospective costs and benefits before making a recommendation."
Key point:
- Past expenditure is not recovered by spending more.
- Evaluate future choices using future consequences, not the emotional weight of prior investment.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
33. Q: How should decision-makers separate sunk costs from relevant future costs?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying expenditure and effort already incurred that cannot be recovered. Record these for accountability and learning, but do not treat them as a benefit of continuing. Then estimate the remaining cost to complete, the expected operating and maintenance costs, future benefits, remaining risks, termination costs, salvage or resale value, and the value of alternative uses of the resources. Compare continuation, redesign, pause, and termination using the same assumptions and time horizon. Some past commitments may create future liabilities, such as cancellation fees or contractual claims; those are relevant because they affect future cash flows, not because the original expenditure should be recovered. The analysis should be independently challenged to reduce pressure from sponsors who have a personal stake in the earlier decision.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a continuation-versus-termination analysis that excludes unrecoverable past costs but includes future cancellation obligations."
Key point:
- A sunk cost is irrelevant to the forward choice unless it changes future consequences.
- Include genuine future liabilities and salvage values in the comparison.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
34. Q: Why do organizations keep investing in a project after evidence shows that it may be failing?
A: Show answer
Several forces can encourage escalation. Decision-makers may want to justify their original approval, protect their reputation, avoid public embarrassment, preserve jobs or influence, and defend the work of their team. They may believe that stopping would waste prior expenditure, even though that expenditure is unrecoverable. Contracts, political commitments, supplier dependence, and public announcements can also reduce practical flexibility. Optimism bias may make leaders believe that the next funding increase will finally restore the project. These motives can reinforce each other, making it difficult to acknowledge new evidence. The chapter calls this driving into the blizzard: accepting additional cost and risk because of what has already been invested rather than because the remaining case is sound.
Study instruction:
- "Interview project stakeholders about the reasons for continuation and classify each reason as forward-looking evidence, sunk-cost reasoning, or reputational pressure."
Key point:
- Escalation is driven by psychology, incentives, and real contractual constraints.
- A continuation decision must be justified by future value, not the desire to defend the past.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
35. Q: What is the difference between sunk cost, escalation of commitment, and lock-in?
A: Show answer
A sunk cost is a past investment that cannot be recovered. The sunk cost fallacy occurs when that past investment improperly influences a current decision. Escalation of commitment is the broader pattern of increasing resources or support for a failing course despite evidence that it may be wrong. Lock-in describes the way alternatives become practically unavailable or too costly to pursue, sometimes before formal approval and often through contracts, political expectations, and accumulated commitments. The concepts are related but not identical. Sunk costs can contribute to escalation; escalation can deepen lock-in; and lock-in can also arise from constraints that are not simply psychological. Distinguishing them helps teams identify whether the remedy is better decision framing, governance change, contract flexibility, or removal of structural barriers.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five project warning signs under sunk cost, escalation of commitment, or lock-in, noting where more than one applies."
Key point:
- Sunk cost is a past investment; escalation is a pattern of continuing; lock-in is constrained choice.
- Different mechanisms require different corrective controls.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
36. Q: What does the basketball game in a snowstorm illustrate about project decisions?
A: Show answer
The example illustrates that people may accept a new risk because they have already paid for something. The ticket price is gone whether the friends attend or stay home. The relevant question is whether the expected enjoyment and other benefits of travelling now outweigh the additional travel cost, danger, and inconvenience. The more expensive the ticket, the stronger the psychological pressure to proceed, even though the ticket price does not make the roads safer or the trip more worthwhile. In a project, prior design expenditure, construction progress, and political effort should not justify continuing if the remaining costs and risks exceed expected future value. The example is useful because it makes an abstract organizational bias visible in a simple personal decision.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the snowstorm analogy to a project by identifying the past investment, the new decision, and the future consequences of each option."
Key point:
- The amount already paid does not change the safety or value of the next decision.
- Reassess continuation using current evidence and future consequences.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
37. Q: When should a project be stopped, redesigned, paused, or continued after a major variance?
A: Show answer
A variance should trigger a structured reassessment rather than an automatic decision to continue or cancel. Decision-makers should establish what changed, whether the original objectives remain valid, whether the expected benefits are still achievable, the cost and time to complete, the consequences of stopping, and the value of alternatives. Continue when the revised forward-looking case remains stronger than feasible alternatives and risks are manageable. Redesign or rescope when a different configuration can preserve the essential benefits at acceptable cost. Pause when critical information is missing or a safety, legal, or contractual issue requires resolution. Stop when no credible path remains to achieve the objectives at a value proportionate to remaining resources. Thresholds should be agreed before delivery to reduce sponsor bias.
Study instruction:
- "Write stop, pause, rescope, and continue criteria for a project before its next funding gate."
Key point:
- A variance requires reassessment, not reflexive rescue or reflexive cancellation.
- Pre-agreed decision criteria reduce the influence of sunk costs and sponsor pressure.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Lock-in and Its Influence on the Project Performance of Large-Scale Transportation Infrastructure Projects
38. Q: What governance controls help prevent premature commitment and escalation?
A: Show answer
Useful controls include a staged approval process, independent assurance, documented options appraisal, realistic cost and schedule ranges, explicit risk ownership, conflict-of-interest declarations, transparent assumptions, and clear authority to pause or stop. Each gate should require evidence appropriate to the maturity and risk of the proposal. Reviewers should test the case for change, objectives, alternatives, affordability, delivery capacity, commercial arrangements, and management controls. The organization should also define what new evidence would trigger reconsideration and ensure that reviewers are not solely the people who promoted the project. Governance must be proportionate: excessive process can waste resources, but high-consequence irreversible choices warrant more scrutiny than routine reversible decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Design a three-gate approval framework with required evidence, independent reviewers, and stop conditions at each gate."
Key point:
- Staged approval keeps commitment aligned with the maturity of the evidence.
- Independent challenge and explicit stop criteria counter sponsor and sunk-cost pressures.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
39. Q: What responsibilities do senior approvers have when reviewing a project proposal?
A: Show answer
Senior approvers should not treat a polished business case or an endorsement from a powerful sponsor as a substitute for independent judgment. They should test whether the proposal addresses a real need, whether the objectives are clear, whether the full range of alternatives has been considered, whether estimates are credible, and whether major risks and uncertainties are visible. They should ask what evidence would change the recommendation, which assumptions are most fragile, who benefits from approval, and who bears the downside if the forecast is wrong. The Pentagon story in the chapter shows that the original plan passed through several senior approvals without enough probing questions. Approval bodies must therefore document the questions asked, responses received, unresolved issues, and conditions attached to authorization.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare ten challenge questions for a project board and require written answers before a major investment decision."
Key point:
- Approval is an active assurance responsibility, not a ceremonial endorsement.
- Decision records should show how material uncertainties and alternatives were challenged.

- The Green Book (2026)
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
- Pentagon History
40. Q: Why can political and organizational power increase the risk of strategic misrepresentation?
A: Show answer
Large projects often compete for scarce capital, attention, and institutional prestige. Sponsors may have incentives to secure approval before alternatives are fully tested, while contractors and advisers may gain work if the project proceeds. Senior decision-makers may also have reputational reasons to defend a public commitment. These interests can influence what information is produced, which experts are heard, and how uncertainty is presented. Strategic misrepresentation becomes more likely when the approval system rewards a persuasive case rather than a reliable one. This does not mean every politician, executive, or consultant acts dishonestly. It means that governance should assume incentives matter and create checks that make distortion harder to conceal, such as independent cost review, transparent data, competing options, and public reporting.
Study instruction:
- "Map the stakeholders in a project, their incentives, their information advantages, and the controls needed to protect decision integrity."
Key point:
- Project forecasts are shaped by institutional incentives as well as technical analysis.
- Independent evidence and transparent governance reduce opportunities for strategic distortion.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- From Nobel Prize to Project Management: Getting Risks Right
- The Green Book (2026)
41. Q: What warning signs suggest that a project is being rushed toward approval?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include a single preferred option presented without a genuine longlist, vague objectives, a compressed appraisal timetable, unsupported cost or benefit assumptions, missing operating costs, unexplained contingency, weak demand evidence, untested site constraints, and a schedule that assumes every dependency will work. Other signs include dismissing critics as obstructive, treating questions as disloyalty, using a public announcement to imply that the decision is settled, and refusing to show alternative scenarios. The presence of one warning sign does not prove a project is unsound, but several together should prompt a pause and independent review. Reviewers should ask what is missing, which assumptions drive the recommendation, and whether the case would still be attractive under a less favorable scenario.
Study instruction:
- "Use a red-flag checklist to review a business case and rank each finding by its potential effect on the decision."
Key point:
- A persuasive narrative can conceal missing analysis.
- Multiple red flags justify independent scrutiny before irreversible commitments.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
42. Q: How should options appraisal be conducted before a project solution is selected?
A: Show answer
Begin with the problem or opportunity and define the outcomes the organization needs, rather than starting with a favored design. Generate a broad longlist of feasible options, including business as usual, doing less, changing the service model, using existing assets, partnering, phasing, or building new infrastructure where relevant. Screen options against transparent critical success factors, then shortlist viable alternatives for detailed comparison of costs, benefits, risks, affordability, distributional effects, and delivery capacity. The Green Book's 2026 framework explicitly separates objective-setting, longlist appraisal, shortlist appraisal, and identification of the preferred option. The process should record why options were excluded and what evidence would cause the shortlist to change. The preferred option should emerge from the analysis rather than dictate it.
Study instruction:
- "Create a longlist and shortlist for a real or hypothetical project and document the criteria used at each stage."
Key point:
- Define the need before defining the solution.
- A transparent options process reduces premature lock-in and improves value-for-money decisions.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
43. Q: How can project teams preserve flexibility while uncertainty remains high?
A: Show answer
Teams can preserve flexibility by phasing investment, using pilots, retaining alternative designs, delaying irreversible procurement choices, setting decision points, and including options to expand, pause, or exit when new information becomes available. The value of flexibility is greatest when uncertainty is material and a later decision can be informed by better evidence. The Green Book discusses real-options analysis as a way to assess flexibility that can be exercised later, especially where initial investment is difficult to reverse. Flexibility is not free: it may require modular design, reservation fees, temporary capacity, or a slower initial schedule. The project case should compare those costs with the expected value of avoiding a premature irreversible choice.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three design or procurement choices that could be staged and estimate the cost of retaining each option."
Key point:
- Flexibility has value when important information will arrive after the initial decision.
- Preserve options deliberately and compare their cost with the risk of irreversible commitment.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
44. Q: How should project leaders combine a can-do culture with rigorous challenge?
A: Show answer
A can-do culture helps teams solve problems and maintain momentum, but it should not make dissent or bad news unwelcome. Leaders should distinguish commitment to the project's purpose from attachment to a particular design or forecast. They can encourage ambitious objectives while asking analysts to test cost, time, benefit, and risk assumptions independently. Team members should be able to raise concerns without being labelled negative, and project boards should reward early disclosure of problems rather than concealment. The chapter's lesson is to maintain an open mind until the process has exposed flaws and opportunities. Realistic optimism means believing improvement is possible while being candid about what is not yet known.
Study instruction:
- "Draft team norms that encourage challenge, evidence-based forecasts, early risk escalation, and constructive problem-solving."
Key point:
- A strong delivery culture welcomes unwelcome evidence.
- Be optimistic about solving problems, not about unsupported assumptions.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
45. Q: How should an organization monitor whether its project forecasts are becoming more reliable?
A: Show answer
Organizations should retain the original estimate at each decision stage and compare it with actual outcomes using consistent definitions of scope, price base, schedule start and finish, benefits, and risk allowances. They should record changes in assumptions, scope, and external conditions so that genuine changes can be distinguished from original forecast error. Portfolio-level analysis can reveal systematic optimism by project type, sponsor, delivery model, or approval stage. Results should feed into reference classes, contingency methods, staff training, and future business cases. The Green Book recommends using historical forecast errors to inform optimism-bias adjustments and drawing on evaluations of previous interventions. Monitoring should not punish honest disclosure; otherwise teams may conceal variances and weaken the learning system.
Study instruction:
- "Design a forecast-performance dashboard with baseline, current forecast, actual result, variance cause, and lessons for future estimates."
Key point:
- Forecast accuracy improves when actual outcomes are systematically recorded and reused.
- Learning requires stable definitions and a culture that does not punish transparent reporting.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
46. Q: How should an organization use post-project reviews to reduce future commitment errors?
A: Show answer
A post-project review should compare the approved case with the delivered result and explain differences in scope, cost, schedule, benefits, risk, and stakeholder impact. It should examine whether the original objectives were clear, whether alternatives were fairly assessed, which assumptions proved wrong, whether warnings were escalated, and when the project became difficult to change. The review should distinguish controllable planning failures from external events and deliberate misrepresentation should be investigated only where evidence supports that conclusion. Lessons need an owner and a route into estimating standards, reference-class datasets, governance templates, procurement practices, and training. Without this feedback loop, the organization repeats the same optimistic assumptions and then treats each new project as unique.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a post-project review template that traces forecast errors back to the decision stage where they could have been detected."
Key point:
- Reviews should explain how decisions and incentives shaped outcomes, not merely record final variances.
- Lessons have value only when they change future estimates and governance.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
47. Q: What practical decision protocol captures the central lesson of Chapter 2?
A: Show answer
Before committing to a major project path, define the problem and intended outcomes, establish what would happen without intervention, generate alternatives, and identify constraints that are genuinely mandatory. Test the preferred option against technical evidence, stakeholder needs, whole-life costs, benefits, risks, delivery capability, and comparable project outcomes. Separate intuitive assumptions from verified facts, and distinguish optimism bias from deliberate strategic distortion. Use an outside-view forecast, disclose uncertainty, and preserve flexibility where possible. Identify commitments that could create lock-in, agree independent review points, and specify conditions for pause, redesign, or termination. Only then authorize the next stage, with a clear record of what is known, what remains uncertain, and why the selected option is preferable.
Study instruction:
- "Apply this protocol to one proposal and produce a decision note that records objectives, alternatives, evidence, forecast uncertainty, lock-in risks, and approval conditions."
Key point:
- Commit to completing the decision process before committing to the solution.
- An open mind at the start can prevent expensive and irreversible errors later.
Source links:
- The Green Book (2026)
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- Optimism Bias and Contingency at Homes England
- GAO: Project Cost Estimate Accuracy and Credibility
48. Q: Who ultimately bears the cost when a major project is protected from cancellation despite poor performance?
A: Show answer
The costs may be transferred to taxpayers, customers, service users, shareholders, employees, or future budgets rather than borne by the individuals who promoted the original decision. The chapter's discussion of the Montreal Olympics illustrates how a political sponsor may achieve the event while the longer-term debt is carried by the wider public. Similar patterns can occur when project benefits are concentrated among sponsors or contractors while overruns are absorbed by an organization or community. This separation between decision authority and downside exposure weakens accountability and can encourage strategic misrepresentation or escalation. A responsible business case should identify who funds the project, who receives the benefits, who bears cost and schedule risk, and who has authority to stop it.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a stakeholder impact map showing who approves, benefits, pays, carries residual risk, and can authorize termination."
Key point:
- The people who promote a project may not bear its full downside.
- Transparent allocation of benefits, costs, and risk is essential to accountable approval.
Source links:
- Top Ten Behavioral Biases in Project Management
- The Green Book (2026)
Rows: 48
Table 3
Defining Project Goals and Thinking from Right to Left
1. Q: Why should a project begin by asking “Why?” rather than by choosing a solution?
A: Show answer
The question “Why?” establishes the change or outcome that justifies committing resources. Starting with a solution prematurely narrows the field of possibilities: once a team has named a bridge, a building, a software platform, or a renovation, discussion tends to focus on how to deliver that chosen answer. Asking why exposes the need beneath it, the people affected, the problem to be changed, and the evidence that the change matters. It also creates permission to discover that another intervention, a smaller intervention, or no project may be better. Exploration should precede commitment.
Study instruction:
- Take a proposed project and write five successive “why” questions until you reach an outcome that can be explained without naming a preferred solution.
Key point:
- Define the need before selecting the means.
- A solution chosen too early can conceal better alternatives.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
2. Q: What is the difference between a project and the goal it is intended to achieve?
A: Show answer
A project is a temporary undertaking and a means of producing a product, service, capability, or change. A goal is the benefit or condition that makes that undertaking worthwhile. A new building is an output; improved access to education may be the outcome. A kitchen renovation is an output; making it easier to host family and friends may be the outcome. Confusing means with ends causes teams to judge success by whether they completed the chosen deliverable rather than whether the original need was met. A project can be delivered exactly as specified and still be a poor investment if the specification was not connected to a valuable goal.
Study instruction:
- For three projects, write the deliverable in one column and the intended beneficiary outcome in another; check that neither is being mistaken for the other.
Key point:
- Projects are instruments for achieving goals, not goals in themselves.
- Completion does not prove that the intended benefit has been achieved.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
3. Q: What caused David and Deborah’s kitchen renovation to become a major cost and schedule failure?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the root cause as an inadequately defined goal, not simply poor workmanship or an unusually difficult building. The couple began with “renovate the kitchen” and commissioned detailed design work before examining what they wanted their home to enable. Hidden structural weakness then triggered reasonable additions: new floorboards, a fireplace replacement, relocation of a powder room, basement alterations, and later work on the second floor. Each addition required redesign, new drawings, approvals, and sequencing changes. A narrow initial scope had concealed related needs that were discovered after construction began. The project grew from an estimated $170,000 to roughly $800,000 and took eighteen months rather than the expected three months.
Study instruction:
- Draw a cause-and-effect chain for the renovation, separating the initial planning failure from the later discoveries and their consequences.
Key point:
- A detailed design can still be based on a weak project definition.
- Unexamined goals and dependencies can turn reasonable changes into cumulative overruns.
Source links:
- RIBA: Plan of Work 2020
4. Q: Why does the chapter argue that slow thinking is not sufficient for good planning?
A: Show answer
Time spent thinking is not automatically productive. David and Deborah reflected for years, and their architect prepared multiple detailed designs, yet the analysis was constrained by the assumption that the kitchen renovation was already the right project. Slow but narrow analysis can create a polished façade of certainty while leaving the central question unanswered. Good planning is slow because it explores, imagines, compares, tests, and revises the problem and possible responses. The quality of the questions and the rigour of the evidence matter more than the number of meetings, pages, drawings, or months spent preparing them.
Study instruction:
- Review a project plan and identify which activities genuinely reduce uncertainty and which merely add detail to an untested assumption.
Key point:
- Time is not a substitute for sound reasoning.
- Good planning tests the premise as well as the design.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
5. Q: What distinguishes exploration and rigorous planning from prolonged discussion or paperwork?
A: Show answer
Productive planning changes the quality of the decision. It identifies the problem, gathers evidence, surfaces assumptions, invites alternative explanations, develops options, assesses constraints, and records why some options are preferred or rejected. Prolonged discussion becomes unproductive when it circles around opinions without resolving uncertainties or assigning evidence-gathering tasks. Extensive paperwork is also weak if it documents a predetermined solution rather than testing it. A useful planning activity should answer a decision question, reduce a material uncertainty, expose a dependency, or improve the fit between the proposed work and the desired outcome. The effort should be proportionate to cost, complexity, uncertainty, and risk.
Study instruction:
- Label each major planning activity in a current proposal with the decision it informs and the uncertainty it is meant to reduce.
Key point:
- Planning quality is measured by better-informed choices, not document volume.
- Analysis should be proportionate and decision-focused.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
6. Q: How should a project sponsor explore a client’s needs before defining the project?
A: Show answer
The sponsor or project lead should conduct structured conversations with the people who experience the problem and those who will use, operate, fund, approve, or be affected by the proposed change. Questions should explore current conditions, desired outcomes, user groups, constraints, fears, priorities, and what success would look like in practice. The purpose is not to sell an early design or lead the client toward a preferred answer. It is to discover what the client values and what evidence supports the need. The chapter’s Frank Gehry example illustrates curiosity-led discovery: ask why the client wants the project, then probe the underlying aspirations before discussing form, location, or technical design.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an interview guide with open questions for beneficiaries, users, operators, decision makers, and affected parties; test whether it avoids assuming a solution.
Key point:
- Early conversations should discover needs, not validate a preselected design.
- Include the people who experience and deliver the proposed change.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
- RIBA: Plan of Work 2020
7. Q: What does the Guggenheim Bilbao example demonstrate about allowing goals to shape project design?
A: Show answer
The Basque officials initially considered converting an existing warehouse into a Guggenheim museum. Gehry first explored the broader purpose: attract visitors, change perceptions of Bilbao, and support economic renewal after the decline of heavy industry and shipping. He concluded that a new landmark building on a prominent riverfront site was more consistent with the ambition than renovating the warehouse. The design and location were therefore consequences of the desired impact, not expressions of architectural preference alone. The example does not prove that landmark buildings always produce economic regeneration; it illustrates how a clearly articulated goal can change the solution under consideration and the criteria by which it is judged.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the Bilbao decision: state the initial proposed output, the underlying outcome, the alternative proposed, and the assumptions that would need testing.
Key point:
- The intended impact should influence design choices.
- A compelling example is not a substitute for testing causal assumptions.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
8. Q: How can a team distinguish a project output from an outcome and a longer-term impact?
A: Show answer
An output is the direct product of activities, such as a completed bridge, renovated building, training course, or digital service. An outcome is the change experienced because the output is used, such as shorter journey times, safer accommodation, improved skills, or easier access to a service. Impact refers to wider or longer-term effects, such as improved economic opportunity or public health. The categories depend on the level of analysis: an outcome for one programme may be an output within a wider strategy. Teams should state the intended level clearly and avoid claiming that delivery of an output automatically proves an outcome or impact. Evidence is needed to establish whether the causal chain worked.
Study instruction:
- Build a three-level results chain for a project and identify one indicator and one evidence source for each level.
Key point:
- Outputs describe what the project produces; outcomes describe the change sought.
- Wider impacts require evidence beyond proof of completion.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
- HM Treasury: Magenta Book
9. Q: What does “thinking from right to left” mean in project planning?
A: Show answer
It means defining the desired end state first and reasoning backward to determine what must happen to make it real. The “right” is the final outcome or goal; the “left” contains the activities, resources, decisions, capabilities, and outputs needed to reach it. This reverses the common habit of starting with a familiar solution and constructing a justification around it. Once the goal is clear, the team can compare several pathways, identify prerequisites, and decide which sequence is most credible. The method is not an instruction to ignore delivery planning; it provides the logic that should guide delivery planning.
Study instruction:
- Draw a flowchart with the measurable end state at the right and work backward through the necessary outcomes, outputs, dependencies, and activities.
Key point:
- Start with the destination and derive the route.
- Delivery tasks should remain traceable to the intended result.
Source links:
- HM Treasury: The Green Book (2026)
10. Q: How does backcasting help planners develop a route to a desirable future state?
A: Show answer
Backcasting begins by describing a specific future condition that is considered desirable, then works backward to identify the changes, decisions, resources, and milestones required to reach it. It differs from forecasting, which generally projects forward from current conditions and trends. Backcasting is useful when the desired future is not simply an extrapolation of current patterns, or when a policy goal requires deliberate changes to those patterns. The chapter connects the method to energy and urban planning. A robust exercise should make the future state concrete, test feasibility, identify transition steps and assumptions, and compare alternative routes rather than treating the imagined future as guaranteed.
Study instruction:
- Choose a five-year outcome and prepare a backward milestone map, noting the assumptions and external conditions for each milestone.
Key point:
- Backcasting starts with a desired future and works backward.
- The route must be tested against feasibility and evidence.
Source links:
- John B. Robinson: Energy Backcasting
11. Q: How does a theory of change connect a project’s activities to its intended outcomes?
A: Show answer
A theory of change explains the causal logic through which resources and activities are expected to produce outputs, outcomes, and wider effects. It makes explicit why the proposed intervention is expected to work, for whom, under what conditions, and with which assumptions. For example, providing literacy materials and teacher training may improve classroom practice, but improved reading outcomes also depend on attendance, language, teaching quality, and access to books. A useful theory of change does not merely draw arrows between boxes; it states the mechanisms and evidence behind each link, identifies alternative explanations, and considers how context may interrupt the chain.
Study instruction:
