Speed should be assessed across the whole project, not by how quickly visible work begins. A longer learning phase can enable a faster and more predictable delivery phase.
Study instruction:
- Compare schedules with an early start and unresolved design against schedules with more testing before execution; include rework and delay.
Key point:
- Fast starts can produce slow completions.
- Judge speed by total delivery performance, not the first visible activity.
42. Q: What should be true before a project moves from iterative planning into full-scale delivery?
A: Show answer
The team should have tested the concept at an appropriate level of detail, addressed important technical and functional questions, understood budget implications, and obtained meaningful feedback from relevant reviewers or users. Gehry’s stated practice was not to let construction begin until the team was confident the building could meet requirements within the owner’s budget. This is not a claim that every uncertainty can be eliminated; it is a readiness principle that major unknowns should not simply be deferred.
Study instruction:
- Write a readiness review identifying which unresolved issues are acceptable and which make delivery premature.
Key point:
- Begin delivery after critical assumptions have been tested.
- Readiness connects design maturity, requirements, and affordability.
Rows: 42
Table 5
Experience, Practical Wisdom, and Iterative Project Planning
1. Q: What is the chapter’s central argument about experience in major projects?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that project success improves when planners deliberately maximize experience in both people and technology. Experience helps teams recognize practical constraints, anticipate failure modes, make better judgments, and avoid repeating mistakes. It is often marginalized by political priorities, the desire to be first, or the attraction of superlatives and custom solutions. The author therefore recommends using proven designs, technologies, leaders, and teams wherever possible, while applying deliberate experimentation to genuinely uncertain elements. The strongest approach combines accumulated experience with iterative testing and practical wisdom.
Study instruction:
- Explain the distinction between experience embodied in technology and experience held by people, then identify one project decision that could benefit from each.
Key point:
- Experience is a project resource that should be actively maximized, not treated as an incidental benefit.
- Proven experience and disciplined experimentation are complementary rather than competing approaches.
2. Q: What does the comparison between Jørn Utzon and Frank Gehry illustrate?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts Utzon’s limited relevant project experience when he won the Sydney Opera House commission with Gehry’s extensive record of progressively more ambitious work before the Guggenheim Museum Bilbao. The author argues that the difference was not merely chronological age: Gehry had accumulated practical experience through many projects, including difficult commissions, while Utzon entered a highly complex undertaking without comparable preparation. The comparison is used to explain why visionary design ability alone is insufficient for a major project. Experience in delivery, relationships, and institutional settings matters alongside creative talent.
Study instruction:
- Compare two project leaders by relevant completed work, increasing project complexity, and experience with the type of stakeholders they will face.
Key point:
- Relevant experience is more informative than age or reputation alone.
- A visionary concept still needs a leader prepared for the practical and political demands of delivery.
3. Q: Why is chronological age an incomplete measure of project experience?
A: Show answer
Age provides time in which experience can accumulate, but it does not show what a person has actually done, learned, or delivered. The chapter’s Utzon–Gehry comparison shows that two people of different ages can have a much larger difference in relevant project exposure than their ages suggest. Conversely, an older professional may have little experience with a particular technology, scale, operating environment, or stakeholder system. For project selection, assess the substance and relevance of prior work rather than using age, years employed, or seniority as a proxy.
Study instruction:
- Create a short experience profile for a proposed project leader, listing comparable projects, roles performed, problems encountered, and lessons applied.
Key point:
- Experience must be assessed by relevance and depth, not by age alone.
- A long career does not automatically establish competence in a new project domain.
4. Q: How does repeated work on increasingly ambitious projects build useful experience?
A: Show answer
A sequence of projects can expose a person or organization to progressively more demanding technical, managerial, and political problems. Each assignment provides opportunities to test methods, understand dependencies, refine judgment, and learn how decisions play out in practice. The chapter describes Gehry’s early work as a ladder of increasingly ambitious projects that prepared him for later commissions. The implication is that capability is often built through cumulative practice rather than acquired in one leap. Project owners should value credible developmental track records and avoid assigning unprecedented responsibilities to teams without a suitable learning history.
Study instruction:
- Map a progression of projects that would build the capabilities required for a proposed large undertaking, identifying what each stage should teach.
Key point:
- Experience compounds when lessons from earlier work are carried into more demanding assignments.
- A staged record of delivery is stronger evidence than an untested claim of readiness.
5. Q: Why can political considerations cause project owners to marginalize experience?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that major projects distribute money, contracts, jobs, prestige, and influence, making them inherently political. Decision makers may prefer domestic suppliers, local employment, or geographically balanced awards even when those choices sacrifice relevant capability or learning efficiency. The project’s delivery objective can consequently become secondary to the political benefits of who receives the work. The author treats this as a recurring source of weak project decisions. A practical governance response is to make capability, whole-life value, and delivery risk explicit in procurement decisions and disclose where political or distributional objectives are being given weight.
Study instruction:
- For a procurement decision, separate the stated project objectives from local economic or political objectives and show how each affects supplier selection.
Key point:
- Political incentives can displace the project’s core delivery objective.
- Trade-offs should be explicit rather than concealed inside technical procurement decisions.
6. Q: What lesson does the chapter draw from the choice of domestic contractors for the California High-Speed Rail project?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how experienced foreign rail organizations showed interest in participating, while California instead engaged many largely inexperienced domestic contractors and managers. The author uses this case to illustrate the risk of prioritizing local political appeal over relevant delivery experience in a country with limited high-speed rail construction experience. The lesson is not that domestic firms should never be selected. It is that local preference should not substitute for evidence of capability, and that inexperienced participants need a credible plan for acquiring expertise, integrating systems, and managing interfaces.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a major procurement package against comparable-project experience, technical capability, integration capacity, and the proposed transfer of expertise.
Key point:
- Local participation and project competence are distinct objectives.
- If inexperienced suppliers are selected, the owner must actively address the resulting capability gap.
7. Q: Why can splitting similar contracts between multiple suppliers destroy learning benefits?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s Canadian icebreaker example describes two ships being awarded to separate domestic companies rather than allowing one builder to construct the first, learn, and apply that learning to the second. Repeating similar work with the same organization can reduce uncertainty, improve productivity, and refine methods. Splitting the work may distribute contracts politically but weaken the natural learning curve. The chapter reports that the estimated cost rose substantially and cites a parliamentary budget officer’s criticism that the split would not produce the same learning improvements. This illustrates how procurement structure affects cumulative experience.
Study instruction:
- Compare a single-supplier sequence with a split award for repeated assets. Identify where learning, competition, resilience, and supplier concentration pull in different directions.
Key point:
- Repeated delivery by a team can convert first-unit learning into better subsequent performance.
- Contract packaging should consider learning effects as well as political distribution and competition.
8. Q: What is uniqueness bias, and how does it undermine project planning?
A: Show answer
Uniqueness bias is the tendency to regard a project as so exceptional or one-off that previous projects appear to offer little relevant knowledge. The chapter links this bias to planners’ failure to learn from earlier undertakings, including projects that have already exposed the risks of large-scale information technology delivery. A project may have a distinctive purpose while still sharing technologies, interfaces, organizational challenges, or delivery patterns with earlier work. Treating it as wholly unique discards potentially valuable evidence. A better approach is to identify both what is genuinely novel and what is comparable.
Study instruction:
- Break a proposed project into components and classify each as proven, adapted from precedent, or genuinely novel. Record the precedent searched for each component.
Key point:
- A project’s uniqueness should be tested, not assumed.
- Learning from analogous projects is possible even when the overall undertaking is distinctive.
9. Q: What does the chapter say about the first-mover advantage?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the assumption that being first in a market or technology is automatically advantageous. It recounts research comparing pioneer and settler brands across multiple product categories. As reported in the chapter, nearly half of pioneers failed, compared with 8 percent of settlers; surviving pioneers averaged 10 percent market share, compared with 28 percent for settlers. The researchers also found that enduring early market leaders typically entered much later than the pioneers. The author’s point is that early entry may matter, but pioneers pay for it by lacking prior market and implementation experience.
Study instruction:
- When assessing a first-mover proposal, list the potential timing advantage and the learning, reliability, and execution disadvantages that come from having no precedent.
Key point:
- Being first is not the same as becoming a durable leader.
- The value of early entry must be weighed against the cost of foregoing others’ learning.
10. Q: What is a fast follower, and why might that strategy outperform pioneering?
A: Show answer
A fast follower enters after pioneers have revealed important information about customer needs, technical feasibility, costs, and market behavior, but early enough to compete effectively. The chapter presents this strategy as a way to retain some benefits of entering an emerging market while learning from the first mover’s mistakes. Its example of Apple following BlackBerry into smartphones illustrates the distinction between being first and executing well after observing the market. The strategy is not universally superior: waiting too long can surrender network effects, scarce resources, or standards-setting opportunities. The decision depends on whether learning gained outweighs the value lost by waiting.
Study instruction:
- For an emerging product or project technology, identify what can be learned from pioneers, how quickly the opportunity may close, and what capabilities a follower must prepare in advance.
Key point:
- A follower can benefit from pioneering without bearing all of the pioneer’s uncertainty.
- Timing should balance learning opportunities against the risk of losing a viable position.
11. Q: When might being first nevertheless be a rational project strategy?
A: Show answer
The chapter does not claim that novelty or early entry is always wrong. It acknowledges that first-mover advantages can exist in particular circumstances, while emphasizing their costs. A first-of-kind project may be necessary when no established solution can meet the essential objective, when delay would forfeit a critical opportunity, or when creating a new capability is itself the project’s purpose. In such cases, the owner should recognize the experience deficit explicitly, narrow the unproven scope, test assumptions aggressively, and provide realistic contingencies. This is a professional application of the chapter’s argument, not a blanket endorsement of pioneering.
Study instruction:
- Identify the specific reason a project must be first. Test whether that reason is essential, time-critical, and strong enough to justify the loss of precedent.
Key point:
- Novelty should be justified by the project’s purpose, not by prestige.
- A necessary pioneer must compensate for its lack of precedent with stronger experimentation and risk controls.
12. Q: Why can the pursuit of the biggest, tallest, longest, or fastest create avoidable risk?
A: Show answer
Superlative ambitions often require a project to exceed the limits of established designs, equipment, construction methods, or operating experience. The chapter argues that a record-setting specification can turn an otherwise familiar undertaking into a first-of-kind technical challenge. The extra complexity may add cost and uncertainty without improving the underlying service enough to justify it. Political and reputational rewards can make the superlative attractive even when the project’s users mainly need reliable performance. Decision makers should therefore distinguish the value delivered by the asset from the publicity value of its record-setting characteristics.
Study instruction:
- For each proposed superlative, state the user benefit, the additional technical requirements, the extra uncertainty, and the evidence that the benefit justifies the risk.
Key point:
- A record is not a project outcome unless it creates real value for users.
- Unnecessary novelty can turn a manageable project into an untested one.
13. Q: What does Seattle’s State Route 99 tunnel illustrate about custom equipment?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Seattle’s decision to build the world’s largest bored tunnel, which required a custom, unprecedented tunnel-boring machine. The machine cost substantially more than a standard one, then failed after boring only part of the tunnel. Extracting and repairing it took years and added major cost. The author contrasts this with using standard-sized tunnels and established equipment operated by experienced teams. The case shows how a superlative design can force the use of unproven machinery and create a single point of failure. It also demonstrates that equipment novelty can undermine an otherwise familiar construction method.
Study instruction:
- Compare the custom-equipment option with proven standard equipment. Include acquisition cost, repairability, supplier support, operating experience, and the consequences of failure.
Key point:
- Custom equipment carries an experience deficit even when its underlying technology is familiar.
- A design choice should be evaluated as a complete delivery system, not only as an engineering specification.
14. Q: What does the phrase “technology is frozen experience” mean?
A: Show answer
The chapter adapts the idea of “frozen music” to describe technology as experience embodied in a design, tool, system, or product. A technology that has been used successfully over time contains the accumulated results of design decisions, testing, correction, and real-world operation. Its reliability is not merely an abstract property of the materials or specifications; it reflects the history of use and validation. A new or custom technology has not yet accumulated the same record. The phrase encourages planners to treat technology as a repository of learning, much as they treat an experienced worker as a repository of practical knowledge.
Study instruction:
- Choose a technology in a project and trace which aspects of its design have been validated through repeated use, testing, maintenance, or field operation.
Key point:
- A proven technology carries accumulated learning in its design and operating history.
- Newness should be treated as an uncertainty to manage, not automatically as an advantage.
15. Q: Why does the chapter warn against assuming that newer technology is better?
A: Show answer
New technology may offer genuine improvements, but it has had less opportunity to reveal defects, operating limitations, maintenance burdens, and interactions with other systems. The chapter argues that planners often mistake novelty for progress and treat custom or “unique” technology as a selling point. This can cause them to overlook the reliability earned by established solutions. The relevant comparison is not old versus new in the abstract. It is the expected value of the improvement against the uncertainty and consequences introduced by limited experience. A new technology should be adopted because its benefits are demonstrated and proportionate to its risks.
Study instruction:
- For each new technology, compare demonstrated benefits with the maturity, field history, failure modes, support arrangements, and fallback options of the proven alternative.
Key point:
- Innovation requires evidence of value as well as a plan for managing immaturity.
- Proven technology may be the better choice when reliability and delivery certainty dominate.
16. Q: How does the carrot-peeler example show that products can embody iterative experience?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts Pete Docter making a carrot peeler, observing a friend cut himself while using it, changing the design, and testing again. Each cycle of use and correction added practical knowledge to the object. The design gradually incorporated lessons that were not available from the initial idea alone. If the product then worked reliably across repeated use, subsequent users would benefit from the accumulated validation. The example makes the concept of frozen experience concrete: a well-designed object can embody the learning of its creators and the evidence gathered through use.
Study instruction:
- Select a simple product or process and outline at least three test–observe–revise cycles that would improve its safety or usability.
Key point:
- Design quality can improve through repeated contact with actual use.
- Testing turns experience into features and safeguards embedded in the product.
17. Q: How should planners decide between a proven design and a bespoke solution?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s general recommendation is to prefer proven designs, off-the-shelf technologies, and reliable methods for ordinary projects, adapting or combining them where needed. Bespoke solutions may be justified when established options cannot meet an essential requirement, but their novelty should be recognized as a source of risk rather than celebrated without scrutiny. A useful decision compares functional need, lifecycle performance, compatibility, maintainability, supply availability, and the cost of testing a custom alternative. This decision framework is a professional application of the chapter’s principle: reuse accumulated experience unless a clear project need warrants departing from it.
Study instruction:
- Write a short options appraisal that identifies which requirements can be met by standard solutions and which, if any, genuinely require custom development.
Key point:
- Reuse proven designs where they meet the need.
- Customize only for a defensible requirement, and test the resulting uncertainty.
18. Q: What does the chapter’s account of Olympic cost overruns reveal?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that every Olympic Games since 1960 for which comparable data were available exceeded its budget, with an average real cost overrun of 157 percent. It identifies Montreal in 1976 as the largest overrun in the dataset discussed, at 720 percent. The author notes that the distribution of overruns follows a power law, making extremely large overruns more common than a simple average might suggest. These figures are presented as evidence of a persistent delivery problem, not as a prediction for every future host. The chapter attributes much of the pattern to the systematic marginalization of experience and repeated pursuit of novelty.
Study instruction:
- Explain why an average overrun alone can understate exposure when extreme outcomes are possible. Distinguish the chapter’s reported historical figures from a forecast for a new event.
Key point:
- The chapter presents Olympic delivery as a recurring example of poor cost performance.
- A long tail of extreme overruns makes reliance on average outcomes hazardous.
19. Q: What is “Eternal Beginner Syndrome” in Olympic project delivery?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses “Eternal Beginner Syndrome” to describe the repeated loss of institutional experience when each Olympic Games is organized by a new host city and country. Although the event recurs every four years, the host organization often starts without a workforce that has recently delivered the same undertaking. Staff from earlier Games may be hired, but political expectations to distribute contracts and jobs locally limit the extent to which experienced teams can lead the work. As a result, the institution does not consistently climb a learning curve from one edition to the next. The recurring event behaves like a first-time project again and again.
Study instruction:
- For a recurring event or program, map where knowledge resides between cycles and identify which experienced people, suppliers, standards, and records must be retained.
Key point:
- A recurring program does not automatically accumulate experience.
- Institutional learning requires deliberate continuity across teams and delivery cycles.
20. Q: How does the Olympic host-selection model contribute to the loss of experience?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that the International Olympic Committee rotates the Games among cities and regions to promote the Olympic brand. This approach means hosts often have little recent experience, even when the city has held the Games before, because decades may separate editions and the earlier workforce has dispersed. Hiring some experienced specialists does not necessarily give the host organization the ability to lead a large, unfamiliar delivery system. Local political expectations also encourage contracts and jobs to be distributed within the host country. The result is a recurring tension between geographic rotation, local political benefits, and accumulated delivery capability.
Study instruction:
- For a rotating host model, identify which functions require local knowledge and which could be supported by a permanent expert organization or retained delivery team.
Key point:
- Geographic rotation can weaken continuity of organizational knowledge.
- Specialist individuals cannot fully substitute for an experienced delivery institution.
21. Q: What does the Montreal Olympic Stadium case teach about design novelty?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a stadium conceived as a dramatic, highly unusual structure with a distinctive tower and retractable roof. The design had not been built before and presented major practical difficulties. The author emphasizes that the project’s ambition was not matched by sufficient regard for construction methods and the conditions needed to build it. The stadium was incomplete for the Games, and later work, redesign, repairs, and roof problems extended the cost and operational consequences for decades. The case demonstrates that architectural ambition must be tested against constructability, engineering evidence, delivery capability, and long-term operation.
Study instruction:
- Review a concept design for constructability. Identify temporary works, access, sequencing, tolerances, maintenance, and components that have no demonstrated precedent.
Key point:
- A striking concept can fail if it is not buildable with available methods and experience.
- Design review must include construction and operational realities, not only visual ambition.
22. Q: Why is constructability an essential test of an ambitious design?
A: Show answer
Constructability asks whether the proposed design can be built safely, efficiently, and predictably using realistic methods, access, equipment, sequencing, and site conditions. In the Montreal stadium example, the chapter reports that the design did not provide for interior scaffolding, forcing an awkward arrangement of cranes that interfered with one another. This is a practical failure of translating an architectural vision into a workable construction process. Constructability review should therefore occur early enough to influence the design, rather than after major commitments have been made.
Study instruction:
- Take a complex design element and describe how it would be assembled, accessed, supported, inspected, and maintained. Identify any step that lacks a proven method.
Key point:
- A design is not complete until a credible construction method exists.
- Early constructability review can expose expensive problems before they become embedded.
23. Q: Why should project owners consider the post-completion life of novel assets?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s Montreal example shows that the cost of a novel design can continue long after the opening event. The stadium required later redesign, installation work, repairs, and repeated attention to a roof that remained problematic. This illustrates why a project cannot be judged only by whether it opens on a target date. Owners should consider maintainability, replacement, operating reliability, adaptability, and the resources needed to keep the asset useful. This lifecycle perspective is a practical extension of the chapter’s argument that proven technology carries valuable operating experience.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed asset, identify the components most likely to generate long-term maintenance or replacement demands and ask whether their design has a reliable operating record.
Key point:
- Completion is not the end of project risk.
- Unproven features can create long-lived operating and maintenance liabilities.
24. Q: How did the Empire State Building project maximize experience?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the Empire State Building as a contrast to the Olympic and Seattle examples. Architect William Lamb used established technologies and sought to avoid uncertainty from innovative methods. The design minimized unnecessary variety, used repeatable components, and supported assembly in a highly standardized way. The general contractor, Starrett Brothers and Eken, also had a proven record in skyscraper construction. Lamb had previously designed the Reynolds Building, which provided a relevant design precedent. Together, experienced design, familiar methods, repeatability, and capable delivery teams helped create a project that advanced rapidly without depending on untested novelty.
Study instruction:
- Identify the equivalent of Lamb’s proven design, repeatable components, experienced contractor, and prior project precedent in a current building or infrastructure proposal.
Key point:
- The chapter’s positive case combines proven methods with experienced delivery capability.
- Standardization can make a complex project easier to repeat and control.
25. Q: How can standardization accelerate project delivery?
A: Show answer
The Empire State Building example shows how reducing unnecessary variation can let workers repeat similar tasks and improve through practice. Lamb kept floor designs as similar as possible and used components that could be duplicated accurately and assembled in sequence. The chapter describes the effect as building 102 one-story buildings rather than treating the structure as 102 entirely different floors. Repetition supports learning, coordination, predictable supply, and efficient work methods. Standardization is not appropriate for every component, but it can reduce complexity where functions and conditions are genuinely repeated.
Study instruction:
- Identify repeated elements in a project and assess whether common dimensions, interfaces, specifications, or assembly sequences could reduce variation without compromising function.
Key point:
- Repeatable work allows teams to learn and improve as delivery proceeds.
- Reducing avoidable variation can simplify coordination and execution.
26. Q: What is the significance of the Reynolds Building as a precedent for the Empire State Building?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that William Lamb designed the Reynolds Building before the Empire State Building. The earlier project gave him a built precedent that he could refine and extend rather than develop every core design element from scratch. The author uses this to challenge the idea that a landmark must be wholly original to be excellent. Reusing successful design ideas can preserve what has already been learned while allowing improvement in scale, execution, or context. A precedent is valuable when it is understood and adapted intelligently, not copied without regard to changed conditions.
Study instruction:
- Find a completed project that could serve as a design precedent. Record which features are transferable, which require adaptation, and what evidence supports the transfer.
Key point:
- A successful precedent can reduce uncertainty and support improvement.
- Originality is not a substitute for proven performance.
27. Q: Why does an experienced contractor matter even when the design is strong?
A: Show answer
The chapter cautions that Lamb’s plan might not have delivered its results if it had been handed to inexperienced builders. The Empire State Building’s general contractor had a proven track record in efficient, rapid skyscraper construction. This demonstrates that a design and its delivery organization are interdependent: the plan must be executed by people who understand the work, can coordinate it, and know how to respond to practical problems. A technically sound design cannot by itself guarantee competent procurement, sequencing, supervision, or site execution.
Study instruction:
- Assess the proposed contractor’s record on comparable work, the experience of its actual delivery team, and whether key lessons and personnel will remain available throughout execution.
Key point:
- A proven plan still needs an experienced team to deliver it.
- Evaluate the people who will perform the work, not only the organization’s brand or past reputation.
28. Q: What is the difference between explicit knowledge and tacit knowledge?
A: Show answer
Explicit knowledge can be stated, documented, taught, or retrieved from instructions, manuals, and specifications. Tacit knowledge is practical understanding that people acquire through experience but cannot fully express in words. The chapter draws on Michael Polanyi’s idea that people can know more than they can tell. Knowing the written steps for using a tool is explicit; sensing how to handle it in a particular situation may be tacit. Both forms matter. Documentation can transfer explicit knowledge, while tacit knowledge usually requires observation, practice, feedback, and participation in real work.
Study instruction:
- Choose a task and separate the instructions that can be documented from the judgments that a novice would need to develop through practice.
Key point:
- Written procedures capture only part of what experienced practitioners know.
- Tacit knowledge must be developed through doing, not assumed to transfer through manuals alone.
29. Q: How does the bicycle-riding analogy explain tacit learning?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that an adult can give a child instructions for riding a bicycle, but those instructions cannot fully convey the bodily judgment needed to balance, steer, and adjust to changing conditions. The learner must try, lose balance, receive feedback, and try again. The analogy illustrates why experience cannot be replaced by more detailed explanation. Instructions are still useful, but they do not complete the learning process. The same principle applies to complex professional work, where situational judgment develops through supervised practice and exposure to real problems.
Study instruction:
- Identify a project-management or technical skill that cannot be mastered from a checklist alone. Design a supervised practice sequence that lets a novice learn safely.
Key point:
- Some essential capabilities are acquired through experience rather than verbal instruction.
- Practice and feedback complement formal training.
30. Q: Why is tacit knowledge especially important in complex project leadership?
A: Show answer
Experienced leaders encounter patterns that are difficult to reduce to rules: when a design feels unworkable, when a stakeholder’s position is changing, when a dependency is being underestimated, or when a team’s confidence is misplaced. The chapter argues that leaders such as Gehry and Docter possess large stores of tacit knowledge that improve their judgment. This does not mean their decisions are automatically correct. It means that accumulated exposure can help them notice subtle signals that less experienced people may miss. The value of this knowledge is greatest when it is applied within a leader’s genuine domain of experience.
Study instruction:
- Ask an experienced project leader to describe a decision where an early warning sign was initially difficult to articulate. Identify how the concern was subsequently tested.
Key point:
- Tacit knowledge helps experts recognize patterns that formal rules may not capture.
- Expert judgment is most credible within the domain in which experience was accumulated.
31. Q: What is skilled intuition, and how is it different from an ordinary gut feeling?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes skilled intuition as rapid judgment developed through extensive experience in a particular field. Under suitable conditions, experts may recognize that something is wrong or promising before they can fully explain why. The author distinguishes this from ordinary gut feelings, which can be unreliable when they are not grounded in repeated exposure and feedback. The example of art experts detecting a fraudulent statue illustrates how trained perception can identify subtle inconsistencies. Skilled intuition should therefore be treated as a source of hypotheses or warnings, not as unquestionable proof.
Study instruction:
- For a decision made on intuition, document the expert’s relevant experience, the pattern they believe they recognize, and the evidence or test that could confirm or challenge the judgment.
Key point:
- Intuition becomes more reliable when it is built through domain-specific experience.
- Expert instinct should inform inquiry, not replace verification.
32. Q: Under what conditions can expert intuition be reliable?
A: Show answer
The chapter states that research finds expert intuition can be highly reliable under the right conditions. Its reliability depends on substantial experience in the relevant domain and on having encountered patterns often enough to learn from outcomes. A person’s confidence alone does not establish expertise. If the environment is unfamiliar, feedback is delayed or misleading, or the expert is operating outside their area of competence, intuition may be much less dependable. A practical approach is to establish the basis of the expert’s experience and use structured testing when the decision is consequential.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an intuitive recommendation by asking whether the expert has repeated, relevant exposure and whether prior outcomes provided meaningful feedback.
Key point:
- Experience-based intuition is conditional, not universally reliable.
- The quality of the learning environment matters as well as the number of years worked.
33. Q: Why should expert intuition be tested rather than accepted without challenge?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s Pixar planning example shows an experienced creator moving between intuitive generation and deliberate evaluation. Pete Docter may create material intuitively, but then converts it into a video mock-up, observes audience reactions, identifies what works, and revises it. This process retains the speed and richness of experienced judgment while checking it against evidence. Testing also helps distinguish a useful expert insight from an assumption that merely feels right. For major decisions, a leader’s intuition should be made inspectable through prototypes, simulations, reviews, or other appropriate feedback mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- Take one expert recommendation and specify a low-cost test that could reveal whether the underlying judgment is sound.
Key point:
- Intuition and analysis can strengthen one another when used iteratively.
- Testing makes expert judgment more accountable and improves the resulting plan.
34. Q: What is “Pixar planning” in the context of the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses “Pixar planning” to describe an iterative approach in which ideas are generated, turned into something that can be experienced or evaluated, tested, and revised. It draws on Pete Docter’s process of creating scripts and images intuitively, building video mock-ups, observing audience responses, and adjusting the work. The approach alternates between creative judgment and systematic feedback rather than expecting a complete plan to emerge fully formed. Its value lies in exposing weaknesses while changes are still manageable and allowing the plan to evolve in response to evidence.
Study instruction:
- Apply an iterative cycle to a project concept: create a rough representation, define what feedback matters, test it with relevant users or experts, and revise the design.
Key point:
- A plan can improve through repeated cycles of making, testing, and learning.
- Early representations make assumptions visible before full-scale commitment.
35. Q: How does iterative planning combine intuition with careful thought?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that experienced creators can use intuition to generate ideas and recognize promising directions, then use testing and analysis to assess those ideas. These modes are not alternatives. Intuition can help produce a richer range of possibilities, while deliberate evaluation reveals whether the ideas work in practice. Repeating the cycle allows the creator to benefit from both forms of thinking. In a project setting, this can mean combining experienced design judgment with prototypes, user trials, simulations, technical reviews, and observed performance.
Study instruction:
- Design a decision cycle showing where experienced judgment generates options and where tests, data, or independent review evaluate them.
Key point:
- Creative judgment and analytical testing are complementary.
- Alternating between the two can improve both the plan and the quality of decisions.
36. Q: Why is political and stakeholder experience part of project leadership?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that large projects are shaped not only by engineering and aesthetics but also by people with different powers, interests, and agendas. Leaders must understand who can influence decisions, how support is maintained, how conflicts develop, and how to protect essential project objectives. Gehry’s experience on the Walt Disney Concert Hall taught him lessons about power, stakeholder interests, and maintaining control of a design. These capabilities are not easily acquired from technical training alone. They develop through experience navigating real project environments.
Study instruction:
- Map a project’s key decision makers, affected groups, formal authority, informal influence, and potential conflicts. Identify which relationships require active leadership.
Key point:
- Stakeholder and political navigation are core delivery capabilities.
- Technical excellence does not remove the need to manage power, interests, and support.
37. Q: What did Frank Gehry learn from the Walt Disney Concert Hall project?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the Disney Concert Hall as a difficult commission involving political complexity, conflicting views, a rushed start, delay, and substantial cost overruns. Gehry’s reputation was threatened, and supporters, including the Disney family, helped ensure he remained the architect. The difficult experience taught him lessons about who held power, whose interests shaped decisions, how to secure and maintain support, and how to retain control of the design. The author argues that these lessons helped Gehry approach the Guggenheim Bilbao with greater practical wisdom. The case shows how a painful project can become valuable experience when lessons are carried forward.
Study instruction:
- Review a difficult project from your organization and identify the leadership lessons that could be transferred to the next undertaking.
Key point:
- A difficult project can build capability if its lessons are recognized and reused.
- Political and organizational learning may be as important as technical learning.
38. Q: How does the chapter contrast Utzon’s and Gehry’s experience with project politics?
A: Show answer
Utzon entered the Sydney Opera House project without comparable experience navigating a complex political environment involving powerful actors and competing agendas. The chapter describes him as poorly prepared for those pressures. Gehry, by contrast, had encountered serious political and organizational conflict on earlier commissions, especially the Disney Concert Hall. That experience helped him understand how to maintain support and protect the design process. The contrast reinforces the chapter’s claim that major-project leadership requires practical experience with the environment in which decisions are made, not merely creative or technical ability.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed project, list the political and organizational challenges a leader is likely to face and identify evidence that the candidate has handled comparable situations.
Key point:
- Project leadership includes navigating the political context in which delivery occurs.
- Prior exposure to conflict can prepare leaders to protect project objectives under pressure.
39. Q: What does Aristotle mean by phronesis, or practical wisdom?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents Aristotle’s concept of phronesis as the practical wisdom that enables a person to recognize what is good to do and to act effectively to bring it about. It is more than knowing rules or possessing technical expertise. It integrates judgment about circumstances, people, consequences, and appropriate action. The author connects phronesis to experience, arguing that it develops through the accumulated practice of making decisions in real situations. A project leader with phronesis can combine knowledge, judgment, and action in ways that serve the project and the people affected by it.
Study instruction:
- Describe a project decision where several technically feasible options exist but the best choice depends on context, consequences, and competing human interests.
Key point:
- Phronesis is practical judgment directed toward appropriate action.
- Technical knowledge is necessary but does not by itself constitute practical wisdom.
40. Q: How is practical wisdom different from technical knowledge?
A: Show answer
Technical knowledge helps a person understand methods, specifications, calculations, or procedures. Practical wisdom helps determine what should be done in a particular situation, why it is appropriate, and how to carry it through amid uncertainty and competing interests. The chapter does not dismiss explicit or classroom knowledge; it treats it as essential but insufficient. Phronesis requires experience-based judgment about real circumstances. In a project, an expert may know how to execute a construction method but still need practical wisdom to decide whether it is suitable, how to sequence it, and how to manage its consequences.
Study instruction:
- Choose a project decision and distinguish the technical facts required from the contextual judgment needed to select and implement an option.
Key point:
- Knowing how a method works is different from knowing when and how to use it.
- Practical wisdom connects knowledge to context-sensitive action.
41. Q: Why does the author describe an experienced project leader as a project’s greatest asset?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that a leader with abundant practical wisdom can bring together technical understanding, tacit knowledge, stakeholder judgment, and the ability to act under pressure. Such a leader can recognize problems early, ask better questions, challenge unsupported novelty, and mobilize the right people. The author’s recommendation to hire a leader with phronesis follows from the view that major projects are shaped by decisions that cannot all be specified in advance. The claim is not that one individual can replace a capable team or sound governance; rather, experienced leadership can help make those resources work together.
Study instruction:
- Define the capabilities required of a project leader and assess candidates using evidence from comparable delivery, difficult decisions, and lessons applied.
Key point:
- Experienced leadership helps integrate technical, human, and organizational dimensions.
- Select leaders for demonstrated practical judgment, not prestige or presentation alone.
42. Q: How should a project owner assess whether a leader’s experience is genuinely relevant?
A: Show answer
A useful assessment examines whether the candidate has delivered projects with comparable technologies, scale, interfaces, stakeholder complexity, and uncertainty. It should distinguish direct responsibility from peripheral involvement and ask what the person learned from both success and failure. The chapter’s cases imply that experience in the political and organizational dimensions of delivery matters alongside technical experience. This assessment framework is a professional application of the chapter rather than a checklist stated verbatim by the author. A leader may need complementary specialists where no single candidate has the full range of relevant experience.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an evidence-based leadership assessment that records the candidate’s actual role, comparable challenges, decisions made, outcomes, and lessons transferred.
Key point:
- Experience is relevant when it matches the demands of the proposed work.
- A credible team can combine complementary experience rather than rely on a single résumé.
43. Q: What is the difference between frozen and unfrozen experience?
A: Show answer
Frozen experience is knowledge embedded in proven technologies, designs, tools, products, and systems. Unfrozen experience is the lived, tacit knowledge held by people who have performed the work and learned from its consequences. The chapter argues that successful planning should maximize both. A project can use reliable technology but still struggle if its team lacks practical judgment; conversely, an experienced team can be undermined by unproven equipment or a novel design. The two forms reinforce one another when experienced people select, adapt, test, and operate technologies whose performance history is understood.
Study instruction:
- For a project component, identify the experience embodied in the chosen technology and the experience required of the people who will design, build, operate, and maintain it.
Key point:
- Technology and people are both carriers of accumulated experience.
- Project plans should align the maturity of tools and methods with the capability of the team.
44. Q: Why does the chapter recommend maximizing experience for ordinary projects?
A: Show answer
The author notes that most projects are not intended to be unprecedented cultural landmarks. They are familiar forms of infrastructure, buildings, software, products, events, and organizational change that users expect to work well, reliably, on time, and within budget. For these undertakings, established designs and methods can often meet the need without introducing unnecessary novelty. The chapter recommends using proven approaches, adapting them where appropriate, hiring experienced people, and relying on reliable technologies. The objective is not to eliminate improvement but to avoid gambling when accumulated experience already offers a workable solution.
Study instruction:
- Identify which parts of a proposed project are routine and define the proven design, supplier, method, or team experience available for each.
Key point:
- For ordinary projects, reliability and demonstrated performance should usually outweigh novelty.
- Use and adapt proven solutions before inventing new ones.
45. Q: How should planners decide when experimentation is necessary?
A: Show answer
Experimentation is most important when the project includes an element that is genuinely unproven or when an existing solution cannot meet an essential requirement. The chapter recommends testing from broad concepts to small details and increasing the intensity of testing as the experience base decreases. The goal is to expose weaknesses early, when changes are relatively inexpensive and failure can be made safe. This implies that planners should not experiment indiscriminately with every component. They should distinguish stable, proven elements from uncertain ones and direct learning effort toward the latter.
Study instruction:
- Create an uncertainty map for a project. Identify what is already proven, what is an adaptation, and what requires an experiment before commitment.
Key point:
- Experimentation should be targeted at genuine uncertainty.
- The less experience available, the greater the need for deliberate testing.
46. Q: What testing methods does the chapter identify for iterative planning?
A: Show answer
The chapter lists a range of ways to test ideas, including trial and error, sketches, wood-and-cardboard models, crude videos, simulations, minimum viable products, and maximum virtual products. The appropriate method depends on what needs to be learned. A sketch may test a concept; a physical model may reveal spatial or assembly problems; a simulation may explore system behavior; and a prototype or minimum viable product may expose usability or performance issues. The chapter’s central requirement is to test both big ideas and small details rather than assume that a polished plan is correct.
Study instruction:
- For three uncertain project assumptions, choose a different low-cost test for each and state what observation would cause the design to change.
Key point:
- Use representations and prototypes to make assumptions testable.
- Choose the test method according to the uncertainty being investigated.
47. Q: Why should failure be made relatively safe during experimentation?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends testing with mechanisms that make failure relatively safe, allowing teams to take calculated risks and try new ideas. The purpose of early experiments is to discover what does not work before failure becomes expensive or dangerous at full scale. A safe test environment may involve limited scope, controlled conditions, reversible decisions, or prototypes that do not expose users or operations to unacceptable harm. These are practical ways to apply the chapter’s principle. Safe experimentation does not mean ignoring standards or tolerating uncontrolled hazards; it means designing learning activities so that mistakes produce information without disproportionate consequences.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed experiment, define its scope, safety boundaries, maximum acceptable loss, stop conditions, and the learning objective before testing begins.
Key point:
- Useful experimentation makes learning possible while containing downside.
- A test should be designed so that failure is informative and proportionate.
48. Q: What does “when something works, keep it; when it doesn’t, get rid of it” mean for project planning?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s instruction is to let the plan evolve in response to what testing reveals. A successful method or design element should be retained because it has earned support through evidence. An ineffective element should be revised or removed rather than defended because of sunk effort, personal attachment, or the prestige of the original idea. This requires decision makers to establish feedback loops and be willing to change course. The principle is not a license for arbitrary redesign; changes should be tied to observed results and assessed for their effects on interfaces, cost, schedule, and performance.
Study instruction:
- Define in advance the evidence that would justify retaining, revising, or abandoning a prototype or design assumption.
Key point:
- Plans should respond to evidence rather than attachment to an initial concept.
- Iteration requires explicit decisions about what to keep, change, or stop.
49. Q: Why should less-proven project elements receive more testing?
A: Show answer
An established technology or method carries a history of use that helps planners understand its likely behavior. A novel element has less such evidence, so more uncertainty remains about performance, reliability, interfaces, construction, or operation. The chapter states the principle directly: the less proven something is, the more it must be tested. Testing effort should therefore be proportional to the experience deficit and the consequences of failure. A small novelty with limited consequences may need modest testing; an unproven core system on which the entire project depends requires much stronger evidence before full commitment.
Study instruction:
- Rank project elements by novelty and consequence of failure. Allocate testing effort accordingly and explain why the highest-risk elements receive the most attention.
Key point:
- Testing intensity should rise as relevant experience falls.
- Critical unproven elements deserve evidence before they become irreversible commitments.
50. Q: How should a project manage a deep deficit of experience when novelty is unavoidable?
A: Show answer
Some projects must do what has never been done before because solving an unprecedented problem is the project’s central purpose. The chapter gives examples such as addressing the climate crisis, sending people to Mars, and permanently storing nuclear waste. These projects cannot simply adopt a complete proven solution. The author argues that their initial experience deficit must be converted into a surplus through relentless experimentation. In practice, this means breaking the ambition into testable elements, learning through staged development, validating assumptions, and transferring each lesson into later stages rather than treating the entire undertaking as one untested leap.
Study instruction:
- Decompose an unprecedented project into learning stages. For each stage, state the key unknown, the experiment that will address it, and the evidence required before proceeding.
Key point:
- Unprecedented projects cannot avoid uncertainty, but they can manage how it is learned.
- A major experience deficit must be addressed through sustained, cumulative experimentation.
51. Q: What is the relationship between experience and experimentation in a good project plan?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes a good plan that maximizes either experience or experimentation, a great plan that does both, and the best plan, which also benefits from a project leader and team with phronesis. Experience supplies proven methods, reliable technology, and practical judgment. Experimentation tests assumptions and develops knowledge where precedent is absent or insufficient. A plan that relies only on precedent may miss necessary innovation; a plan that relies only on experimentation may repeat avoidable mistakes. Combining them allows the project to reuse what is known while learning systematically about what is not.
Study instruction:
- Review a project plan and mark where it reuses proven experience and where it creates new knowledge through experiments. Identify any area relying on neither.
Key point:
- The strongest plans combine accumulated experience with disciplined experimentation.
- Practical wisdom helps determine where to reuse, adapt, or test.
52. Q: How can a project distinguish a genuinely novel requirement from novelty added for prestige?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s cases suggest asking whether the unusual feature is necessary to deliver the project’s purpose or mainly attractive because it is first, biggest, tallest, or visually distinctive. A genuine requirement may be tied to performance, safety, environmental constraints, or an unmet user need. Prestige-driven novelty may add technical complexity without a proportionate benefit. A disciplined review should state the required outcome, compare standard alternatives, quantify or otherwise explain the incremental value, and expose the extra uncertainty. This decision test is a professional synthesis of the chapter’s critique of superlatives and bespoke solutions.
Study instruction:
- For every unusual feature, write a one-sentence user or mission requirement that cannot be met by a proven alternative. Challenge any feature without a defensible requirement.
Key point:
- Novelty needs a clear purpose beyond publicity or status.
- A standard solution should remain the baseline unless evidence supports a departure.
53. Q: How can organizations preserve experience between projects?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s discussion of Olympic hosts and repeated contract work shows the cost of losing continuity. A practical organizational response is to retain experienced people where possible, document decisions and lessons, maintain reusable designs and standards, and involve prior delivery teams in later projects. Documentation alone cannot transfer all tacit knowledge, so mentoring, joint work, and continuity of key roles are also important. These measures are professional applications of the chapter’s emphasis on frozen and unfrozen experience. The objective is to prevent each new project team from starting as a beginner when relevant learning already exists.
Study instruction:
- Design a knowledge-continuity plan for a recurring project or program. Include records, reusable assets, experienced personnel, mentoring, and a handover process.
Key point:
- Institutional learning requires more than storing final reports.
- Preserve both documented knowledge and opportunities for people to transfer practical judgment.
54. Q: What governance practices help prevent political incentives from overriding experience?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s examples imply that project owners should make capability and delivery risk visible in decisions about suppliers, design ambition, and project organization. Governance can require evidence of comparable experience, transparent evaluation criteria, documented reasons for exceptions, and explicit treatment of local or political objectives. It can also ask decision makers to explain the cost of splitting repeat work or selecting unproven technology. These are governance applications derived from the chapter, not a formal framework specified by the author. Their purpose is to ensure that political considerations are acknowledged without silently displacing the project’s delivery requirements.
Study instruction:
- Draft procurement or design approval criteria that require decision makers to disclose the experience trade-offs behind exceptional choices.
Key point:
- Governance should make the cost of sacrificing experience visible.
- Transparent exceptions are preferable to hidden trade-offs that weaken delivery.
55. Q: How should a project team use lessons from failed projects without copying their circumstances blindly?
A: Show answer
The chapter repeatedly uses troubled projects as warnings, but learning from them requires more than treating every later project as identical. Teams should identify the mechanism behind a failure, such as untested equipment, weak constructability, lost institutional memory, or political contract allocation. They should then assess whether the same mechanism exists in the new context. This avoids both uniqueness bias and uncritical copying. A project can differ in location or purpose while still sharing a critical risk pattern. The transferable lesson is the underlying cause, not necessarily the exact solution used elsewhere.
Study instruction:
- Select a failed project and extract three causal lessons. For each, state what conditions would make the lesson relevant or irrelevant to your own project.
Key point:
- Transfer mechanisms and lessons, not surface details alone.
- Good learning avoids both dismissing precedent and copying it without context.
56. Q: What practical questions should an owner ask before approving a first-of-kind technology?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s argument supports asking what makes the technology necessary, what parts are truly unproven, what prior experience exists in adjacent applications, and how the system will be tested before full deployment. Owners should also examine whether standard alternatives could meet the need, what happens if the technology fails, whether repairs and support are available, and how learning will be captured. These questions translate the chapter’s cases into a practical approval discipline. They do not eliminate the risk of innovation; they ensure that the project understands and actively manages the experience deficit it is accepting.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a technology approval note answering the necessity, precedent, test strategy, fallback, support, and learning-transfer questions.
Key point:
- Approval of novel technology should be conditional on a credible evidence and fallback strategy.
- Understand the experience deficit before committing the project to it.
57. Q: How can an owner tell whether a project team is relying too heavily on an individual expert?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes the value of experienced leaders but also shows that project success depends on a broader combination of people, technology, and organizational learning. A team is vulnerable if critical knowledge exists only in one person’s memory or if the project cannot challenge that person’s judgment. A practical response is to pair experts with capable colleagues, make reasoning and assumptions visible, test important judgments, and build continuity into key roles. This does not diminish tacit expertise; it helps the organization benefit from it without creating an avoidable single point of failure.
Study instruction:
- Identify critical decisions that depend on one person. Determine how expertise can be shared, challenged constructively, and retained if that person leaves.
Key point:
- Expertise is valuable but should not become an unexamined single point of dependency.
- Teams should make expert learning transferable where possible while preserving room for judgment.
58. Q: How should a project balance repeatability with the need to adapt to local conditions?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends reusing proven designs and methods but does not imply that every project should be an exact copy. The Empire State Building example shows the value of repeating successful elements, while the broader argument allows proven designs to be tweaked or combined. A sound adaptation preserves the elements whose performance is understood and changes only what the new context requires. Each change should be assessed for its effect on interfaces, constructability, operations, and the experience base. This is a professional synthesis of the chapter’s call to rely on proven approaches without ignoring real differences.
Study instruction:
- Separate a reference design into elements that should remain standardized and elements requiring local adaptation. Explain the evidence behind each proposed change.
Key point:
- Reuse should preserve proven performance, not prevent necessary adaptation.
- Every deviation from a reference design should have a clear reason and an assessed effect.
59. Q: What is the chapter’s overall decision rule for choosing between relying on precedent and trying something new?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s decision rule is to rely on accumulated experience whenever it can meet the project’s purpose, and to experiment deliberately where the project truly needs to go beyond what is known. Ordinary work should generally use experienced people, proven designs, and reliable technology. Novel or unprecedented elements should be exposed to more testing, with failure made relatively safe and the plan revised as evidence emerges. The most capable leader and team then integrate these choices through practical wisdom. This approach avoids both reflexive conservatism and novelty for its own sake.
Study instruction:
- Apply the rule to one project decision: state the proven baseline, the reason to depart from it, the test needed, and who has the judgment to interpret the results.
Key point:
- Use precedent by default when it serves the objective.
- Innovate when necessary, but match novelty with testing and experienced judgment.
Rows: 59
Table 6
Forecasting, Reference Classes, and Black Swan Risk Management
1. Q: Why is the chapter’s central lesson that a project must be treated as “one of those” rather than as wholly unique?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that every project has distinctive features, but projects also belong to broader classes whose completed outcomes reveal what is likely to happen. Treating a project only as a singular undertaking encourages planners to rely on their own intentions, detailed plans, and immediate experience. That inside view can miss the time, cost, and risk patterns already visible in comparable work. Treating it as “one of those” adds the outside view: evidence from a relevant class of completed projects. The two perspectives are complementary. Project-specific knowledge helps explain differences and shape delivery, while class-level evidence supplies a reality-based starting point for forecasts and risk analysis. The practical objective is not to deny uniqueness but to prevent uniqueness from becoming an excuse to ignore experience.
Study instruction:
- Choose a current or proposed project and list three features that make it distinctive and three features it shares with completed projects. Use the shared features to identify possible reference classes.
Key point:
- Distinctive details do not erase common project patterns.
- The outside view supplies evidence that the inside view can overlook.
2. Q: What does the Hong Kong XRL case show about the relationship between forecasting and delivery failure?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Hong Kong’s underground cross-border high-speed rail project as a case in which a capable delivery organization was given an unrealistic schedule and budget. By the original completion date, less than half the work had been completed while more than half the budget had been spent. The subsequent list of construction problems mattered, but the author argues that the deeper cause lay earlier: the forecast had been anchored in MTR’s experience with urban and conventional rail rather than in sufficiently comparable high-speed rail, tunneling, and urban rail projects. Once the baseline was unrealistic, delivery teams were judged against a target they could not reasonably meet. Repeated pressure to catch up compounded the problem and contributed to demoralization. The case warns against assuming that slippage proves poor execution before testing whether the original benchmark was credible.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the XRL sequence from initial estimate to delay, escalation, and organizational response. Mark which problems arose in forecasting and which arose during delivery.
Key point:
- An unrealistic baseline can make failure likely before execution begins.
- Diagnose the forecast as well as the delivery process.
3. Q: What does Robert Caro’s seven-year biography project reveal about estimating from personal experience?
A: Show answer
Caro expected to complete his biography of Robert Moses in about nine months to a year. His estimate was based on his experience as a newspaper reporter: he could research and write a substantial article in a few weeks, so he extrapolated from the work involved in a book. The completed biography took seven years. Conversations with experienced biographers later showed him that projects of this kind commonly took many years. The chapter uses this example to distinguish poor performance from a poor estimate. Caro’s effort was not necessarily inefficient; his reference experience was inappropriate for the undertaking. A person can be competent and hardworking yet appear to underperform when judged against a forecast built from the wrong kind of work.
Study instruction:
- Identify a project where you have extrapolated from a familiar task to a substantially different one. Find completed projects that more closely resemble the actual undertaking.
Key point:
- Experience is useful only when it is relevant to the work being estimated.
- A forecast error should not automatically be interpreted as an execution failure.
4. Q: What are anchoring and adjustment, and how did they shape Caro’s estimate?
A: Show answer
Anchoring and adjustment is a judgment process in which an initial figure or experience becomes a starting point, after which a person modifies it to account for perceived differences. Caro used the time required to write long newspaper articles as his anchor, multiplied that experience across the anticipated chapters, and adjusted the result to arrive at a book schedule. The estimate remained heavily influenced by an anchor drawn from a different class of work. The chapter emphasizes that this is a natural cognitive process, not an unusual personal defect. Its danger is that a plausible calculation can still be systematically biased when the starting point is unsuitable. The arithmetic may be coherent while the underlying comparison is wrong.
Study instruction:
- Write down the anchor behind a recent estimate and explain why that anchor is or is not comparable to the project being forecast.
Key point:
- The quality of an estimate depends heavily on the quality of its starting point.
- Sound arithmetic cannot rescue an unsuitable anchor.
5. Q: Why can an irrelevant number influence a forecast?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts the wheel-of-fortune experiment associated with Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky. Participants saw a randomly selected number and were then asked to estimate the proportion of United Nations members that were African. Although the wheel’s number had no factual relevance, estimates differed depending on where the wheel stopped. The example illustrates how readily an exposed number can become an anchor. The same mechanism can affect project estimates through an early budget, an inherited schedule, a supplier’s first quotation, or a familiar but poorly matched past project. The influence may persist even when people know that the starting figure is arbitrary or incomplete. Forecast governance should therefore ask where the initial number came from, not merely whether later adjustments appear reasonable.
Study instruction:
- Review a project estimate and identify any inherited, arbitrary, politically selected, or otherwise weak starting numbers that may be anchoring later decisions.
Key point:
- Anchors can influence judgment even when they are irrelevant.
- Record and challenge the origin of the baseline before debating adjustments.
6. Q: How can optimism and organizational incentives reinforce a bad anchor?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that MTR insiders had quietly questioned the original XRL anchor and suggested a higher estimate, but their concerns were outweighed by optimism and by interests that favored low estimates. In large organizations, realism can be dismissed as pessimism when decision-makers prefer a proposal that appears affordable or achievable. The result is a reinforcing cycle: a low anchor encourages optimistic adjustment, and incentives to secure approval make it harder for dissenting evidence to change the forecast. The chapter connects this dynamic to the broader problem of strategic misrepresentation discussed earlier in the book. A credible process therefore needs more than technical estimating skill; it needs a way to surface inconvenient evidence and protect realistic challenge from being treated as disloyalty.
Study instruction:
- Identify who benefits from a lower forecast in a project you know. Compare those incentives with the responsibilities of people asked to validate the estimate.
Key point:
- Low estimates can be institutionally attractive even when they are unrealistic.
- Independent challenge is necessary when incentives favor optimism.
7. Q: What are the inside view and the outside view in project forecasting?
A: Show answer
The inside view treats a project as a distinctive undertaking and concentrates on its particular design, people, location, plan, and circumstances. The outside view treats it as a member of a class of comparable completed projects and asks what happened to those projects in practice. The chapter does not say that one should replace the other. The inside view is necessary for understanding the specific undertaking and identifying meaningful differences. The outside view is necessary because the inside view alone tends to underweight evidence from comparable outcomes. Forecasting becomes more reliable when the outside view establishes the anchor and the inside view is used carefully to assess whether a well-supported adjustment is justified.
Study instruction:
- For a live project, prepare two short assessments: one based only on its specific plan and one based on outcomes from comparable completed projects. Explain where they differ.
Key point:
- The inside view explains the particular project; the outside view grounds the forecast in experience.
- Use both views, but do not allow project-specific narratives to displace comparative evidence.
8. Q: What is a reference class, and what makes it useful?
A: Show answer
A reference class is a group of completed projects that share relevant characteristics with the project being forecast. The class provides observed outcomes such as actual cost, duration, or other performance measures. Those outcomes form a more reliable anchor than a forecast built only from the current project’s plan or from an individual’s memory. A reference class should be broad enough to include useful experience and should not be narrowed merely to make it resemble the proposed project. For example, the chapter warns against redefining kitchen renovations as only those with a very specific combination of fixtures, building type, and neighborhood. The purpose is to capture a meaningful range of actual experience, not to construct a class so narrow that little data remain.
Study instruction:
- Define a candidate reference class for a project and explain which shared characteristics make its completed projects relevant.
Key point:
- A reference class converts past outcomes into a forecasting anchor.
- The class should reflect meaningful similarity without being artificially narrow.
9. Q: How should a project team choose the scope of its reference class?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends defining the class broadly and erring on the side of inclusion. A class that is tailored too closely to the current project may exclude useful observations and make data collection difficult. A kitchen renovation, for example, should not automatically be restricted to renovations with exactly the same appliances, countertop material, building height, and neighborhood. Those features may matter when considering a specific adjustment, but they do not necessarily justify excluding all other kitchen renovations from the reference class. The team should begin with the recognizable general category, then test whether the included projects are sufficiently relevant. If a project is genuinely unusual, it may still share important cost, schedule, engineering, or delivery characteristics with a broader group.
Study instruction:
- Draft a broad reference-class definition first. Then list proposed exclusions and require evidence that each exclusion materially changes the outcome being forecast.
Key point:
- Overly narrow classes discard relevant experience and reduce the available data.
- Start broad; refine only when the evidence justifies doing so.
10. Q: When should a reference-class forecast be adjusted for project-specific differences?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises making adjustments only when there are clear and compelling reasons to expect the project to perform materially above or below the class average. An adjustment should be supported by evidence, not by a general feeling that the project is special or that its team is unusually capable. Repeated adjustments can reintroduce the very biases that the reference-class method is intended to counter. If the evidence is weak or ambiguous, the chapter recommends retaining the class mean as the anchor rather than inventing a precise correction. This does not mean that differences are irrelevant. It means that differences should influence the forecast only when their expected effect can be explained and supported.
Study instruction:
- List every proposed adjustment to a reference-class estimate. For each one, record the evidence, direction, expected effect, and reason the class average is insufficient.
Key point:
- Adjustments should be few, explicit, and evidence-based.
- When support is weak, preserve the unbiased anchor.
11. Q: What are the basic steps in applying reference-class forecasting?
A: Show answer
First, identify the outcome to be forecast, such as total cost or completion time. Second, define a broad class of completed projects that share relevant characteristics with the proposed undertaking. Third, gather actual outcome data from that class rather than relying only on original estimates. Fourth, examine the distribution and calculate an appropriate benchmark, such as the mean when the distribution supports that use. Fifth, compare the proposed project with the class and make only well-supported adjustments. Finally, communicate the forecast together with its uncertainty and the assumptions behind it. The chapter presents this as a simple process whose main difficulty is usually obtaining reliable historical data and resisting the temptation to customize the class or adjust the result excessively.
Study instruction:
