Key point: A repeatable creative process can produce originality without depending on last-minute rescue.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
28. Q: What changed in John Storyk's approach after the early Electric Lady project?
A: Show answer
Electric Lady gave Storyk an extraordinary start, but the chapter emphasizes that he did not treat the project's improvised delivery as a model for all future work. In his later career he deliberately slowed projects down, taking time to develop ideas and inspect them again before construction. He said that a second or third look helped reduce mistakes and could make the overall project faster. The shift is significant: the person whose early career began through an improvised leap became a practitioner of careful preparation. His later experience supports the chapter's distinction between benefiting from serendipity once and relying on serendipity as a repeatable operating method.
Study instruction: Compare the conditions of Storyk's first major commission with his later process. Identify what he learned about the relationship between review time, mistakes and total delivery time.
Key point: A lucky beginning need not become a permanent project method; experience can lead to more deliberate preparation.
Source links: Electric Lady Studios — History; How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
29. Q: What does the principle Think Slow, Act Fast mean in the context of this chapter?
A: Show answer
Think Slow, Act Fast means investing time in understanding the project, developing the design, testing assumptions and resolving issues before execution, then delivering decisively once the plan is sufficiently mature. The phrase does not mean that every decision should be delayed indefinitely or that planning can eliminate uncertainty. It means that speed should be sought in the overall delivery process, not by rushing into construction before key questions have been answered. Storyk's observation illustrates the logic: an additional review can prevent mistakes that would take much longer to correct later. The principle combines deliberate preparation with disciplined execution rather than treating planning and speed as opposites.
Study instruction: Apply the principle to a project you know. Identify the decisions that deserve slower analysis and the activities that should proceed rapidly once those decisions are settled.
Key point: Slow preparation can enable fast, reliable execution.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
30. Q: Why can slowing down during planning make the whole project faster?
A: Show answer
A project that begins delivery before important questions are resolved may appear fast at first but accumulate rework, redesign, idle time, procurement changes and disputes. Those problems interrupt execution and often require more expensive corrective action than an earlier review would have required. Storyk's experience, as presented in the chapter, is that taking a second and third look can reduce mistakes and ultimately make the project faster. The relevant measure is not how quickly a team starts physical work; it is how efficiently the project moves from concept to a usable outcome. Planning time is productive when it removes uncertainty and prevents avoidable work from being repeated.
Study instruction: Draw a simple timeline comparing early review with late redesign. Identify the cost and schedule consequences of discovering the same design flaw before procurement, during construction and after handover.
Key point: Measure speed across the full project lifecycle, not by the date of the first visible activity.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
31. Q: What did the underground river beneath Electric Lady reveal about the limits of an improvised project approach?
A: Show answer
The discovery of an underground river created a technical problem that had not been anticipated in the initial drawings. Sump pumps were required to manage water, but the pumps generated background noise incompatible with a professional recording environment. The team then had to find ways to reduce the noise, delaying the work for weeks. This example shows that a project can encounter interacting constraints: solving the water problem created an acoustic problem because the building's purpose imposed unusual performance requirements. Improvisation helped the team respond, but the episode also demonstrates the value of site investigation and early integration of technical requirements. The success of the final studio does not make the delay or risk disappear.
Study instruction: Create a cause-and-effect diagram showing the underground water, pump installation, noise issue, acoustic requirement and schedule impact. Identify which investigations could have reduced uncertainty before construction.
Key point: Unforeseen site conditions can create secondary problems; investigate the site and integrate performance requirements early.
Source links: Electric Lady Studios — History; How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
32. Q: Why was the ceiling experiment at Electric Lady an example of useful technical improvisation rather than proof that planning is unnecessary?
A: Show answer
A recording studio ceiling must help control sound rather than function merely as a surface for lighting. Storyk and Kramer learned that air injected into plaster could improve sound absorption, then devised a way to whip plaster with commercial eggbeaters to introduce additional air. The completed ceiling unexpectedly absorbed low-frequency sound as well as the midrange sound the team expected. This is a genuine example of experimentation producing an advantageous result. It also shows why a technical hypothesis should be tested against actual performance. The chapter celebrates the ingenuity while placing it within a larger argument: such discoveries are valuable, but they do not justify leaving all design questions until construction.
Study instruction: Explain the acoustic requirement, the material hypothesis, the improvised technique and the unexpected performance result. State what tests would be needed before repeating the method on another project.
Key point: Experimentation can create breakthroughs, but it should be evaluated and incorporated into a controlled design process.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
33. Q: How did irregular cash flow affect the construction of Electric Lady?
A: Show answer
Hendrix's income from concerts and records was substantial but irregular. Construction proceeded for a period, then stopped when money ran out and tradespeople were laid off. Work resumed after touring income arrived and cash was transported back to New York. As costs accumulated, this informal funding pattern became insufficient, and Hendrix's manager persuaded Warner Bros. to provide hundreds of thousands of dollars to get the project over the finish line. The episode shows that project affordability is not simply a question of whether the sponsor is wealthy or expects future income. Timing and liquidity matter. A project can be viable in total-value terms yet still stall if cash is unavailable when contractors and suppliers must be paid.
Study instruction: Prepare a simple monthly cash-flow profile for a construction project. Identify the months when cash requirements peak and the consequences of a funding interruption for labour, suppliers and schedule.
Key point: Funding capacity must be matched to the timing of project cash needs, not just to expected lifetime wealth.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
34. Q: What does the Electric Lady funding story teach about the difference between project cost and project financing?
A: Show answer
Project cost is the total amount required to deliver the intended asset, while financing concerns whether funds are available in the right amounts and at the right times. Hendrix's irregular concert income meant that construction could not proceed continuously even though the studio had a compelling purpose and he earned substantial sums. The later contribution from Warner Bros. illustrates how a sponsor or financing partner can bridge a funding gap, but it does not remove the underlying need for a credible funding plan. In a well-prepared project, the sponsor should understand committed funding, cash-flow timing, contingency and the consequences if an expected source is delayed or withdrawn.
Study instruction: Distinguish total budget, committed financing, forecast receipts and contingency in a one-page funding plan. Identify which sources are certain and which depend on future events.
Key point: A budget estimate does not prove that cash will be available when delivery requires it.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
35. Q: What is the difference between a contingency allowance and relying on creativity to solve unforeseen problems?
A: Show answer
A contingency allowance is a deliberate provision for uncertainty identified in the cost, schedule or risk analysis. It does not predict every event, and it cannot eliminate the need for adaptation. It gives the team a controlled reserve and a governance process for responding when an uncertain event occurs. Relying on creativity alone assumes that the team will invent a solution quickly enough and that the resulting benefits will outweigh the disruption. Chapter 7 challenges that assumption as a general strategy. Professional synthesis from the chapter's argument is that resilience should be designed into the project through investigation, reserves, decision rights and response plans, while creativity remains available to improve those responses.
Study instruction: For a sample project, list five uncertainties, estimate their possible schedule or cost effects, and explain how contingency and escalation authority would work if they materialized.
Key point: Plan for uncertainty and preserve room for ingenuity; do not substitute hope for reserves and controls.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand
36. Q: Why does the chapter treat opportunity cost as part of the true cost of a badly planned project?
A: Show answer
Opportunity cost is the value of the best alternative use of resources that is given up when a project consumes them. If weak planning causes a project to waste money, time or scarce specialist capacity, those resources cannot be used for other projects that might have delivered greater value. The visible budget overrun is therefore only part of the loss. Flyvbjerg argues that badly planned projects can also crowd out other buildings, services or investments that never get undertaken. Those foregone alternatives may not appear in the project's spreadsheet, but they are economically and socially relevant. The appropriate comparison is not simply whether the completed asset has value; it is whether that value justifies the resources consumed relative to available alternatives.
Study instruction: Identify three alternative uses for a project's budget, staff time or land. Explain how a cost overrun changes what the sponsor can no longer afford to do.
Key point: The full cost includes the value of opportunities displaced by avoidable waste.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
37. Q: How did the Sydney Opera House project impose a career cost on Jørn Utzon beyond the building's financial overrun?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that Utzon's contribution and the project's consequences cannot be evaluated only by looking at the completed building. Construction and planning were badly managed, and Utzon was blamed and dismissed during the process. He left Australia in disgrace and was denied the stream of commissions that might have followed from a successful delivery of additional major works. Flyvbjerg describes him as becoming a one-building architect. This is an example of a cost that does not appear in the building's capital budget: reputational damage and lost future professional opportunities. It also shows that project governance can distribute blame and consequences unfairly, especially when complex delivery failures are reduced to the reputation of one visible designer.
Study instruction: Construct a stakeholder-impact map for the Opera House case. Include the public owner, architect, delivery team and future clients, and identify which costs were financial, professional and reputational.
Key point: Project failure can destroy future value and careers even when the completed asset becomes iconic.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
38. Q: How should project sponsors think about the opportunity cost of tying up scarce specialist talent?
A: Show answer
A project can consume scarce skills for years, preventing those people from contributing to other initiatives. If poor planning creates repeated redesign, idle periods or prolonged crisis management, the opportunity cost includes the other work that capable people could have completed. Utzon's lost commissions illustrate the potential scale of this effect for an individual architect; the same logic applies to engineers, programme leaders, public-sector specialists and contractors. Sponsors should therefore consider not only direct expenditure but also the duration and quality of the commitment being imposed on scarce talent. Better preparation can release capacity sooner and preserve the ability of specialists to contribute to other valuable projects.
Study instruction: Identify the scarce roles in a project and estimate how long they will be committed. List the other work those people could undertake if avoidable delays and rework were reduced.
Key point: Poor delivery consumes human capacity as well as money.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
39. Q: What does the chapter imply about using a portfolio of projects to justify risky individual projects?
A: Show answer
A portfolio approach can be legitimate when a sponsor knowingly accepts that some initiatives will fail in exchange for a chance of unusually large returns. But the logic requires evidence that the gains from winners are sufficient to offset the losses from the rest of the portfolio. Chapter 7 directly tests the claim that exceptional positive surprises compensate for widespread underperformance and reports that aggregate losses exceeded gains in the analyzed data. A sponsor should therefore not use the existence of occasional spectacular successes as a blanket justification for weak planning across a programme. Portfolio governance requires explicit risk limits, comparable outcome data, stage reviews and the ability to stop or redesign projects before losses accumulate.
Study instruction: Write a portfolio decision rule specifying the evidence needed to continue funding a high-risk initiative and the aggregate indicators that would trigger a review of the overall portfolio.
Key point: Portfolio risk-taking needs an evidenced return logic, not just faith that one winner will pay for many failures.
Source links: Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand; Oxford Research Archive — Flyvbjerg (2016) accepted manuscript record
40. Q: When should a project sponsor stop, redesign or continue after planning reveals serious difficulties?
A: Show answer
The chapter rejects the idea that discovering difficulties during planning is a reason to abandon a project automatically. Better information should improve the decision, not dictate a single response. A sponsor should distinguish problems that can be mitigated through design changes, scope adjustments or new methods from problems that make the intended outcome uneconomic, unsafe or infeasible. The decision should compare the revised cost, schedule and benefit profile with alternatives, including doing nothing. If the project still offers value, the team can proceed with a stronger plan. If the case no longer holds, stopping or redesigning may protect resources for better uses. The key is to decide deliberately rather than let optimism or sunk-cost pressure govern.
Study instruction: Create a decision-gate template with continue, redesign, pause and stop options. Specify the evidence required for each decision, including updated costs, benefits, risks and alternatives.
Key point: Revealed difficulty should trigger analysis and choice, not reflexive optimism or automatic cancellation.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand
41. Q: How can a project team distinguish a genuinely unavoidable unknown from a foreseeable issue that poor planning left undiscovered?
A: Show answer
A genuinely unavoidable unknown could not reasonably have been identified with the information, tools and investigation available at the relevant decision point. A foreseeable issue was discoverable through proportionate due diligence, consultation, site investigation, technical review or comparison with similar projects. The distinction matters because not every surprise is evidence of bad planning, but teams should not label preventable omissions as unforeseeable merely because they were discovered late. Electric Lady's underground water problem illustrates the kind of site condition that may warrant early investigation, while its acoustic response illustrates adaptation to a technical constraint. A mature review asks what was knowable, what was investigated and what could reasonably have been tested before commitment.
Study instruction: Choose a project surprise and perform a hindsight review. Identify the information available before approval, the investigation that could have been performed and whether the event was reasonably foreseeable.
Key point: Separate true uncertainty from avoidable ignorance by examining what could reasonably have been known earlier.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
42. Q: How should a project leader respond when a team member argues that too much planning will prevent the project from ever starting?
A: Show answer
The leader should acknowledge that planning can become excessive if it is disconnected from decisions or continues without a clear purpose. But the alternative is not to ignore foreseeable difficulties. The team can use time-boxed planning, explicit decision questions and proportionate investigation to learn enough to make a responsible commitment. It can also test a limited prototype or pilot where uncertainty is genuinely high, rather than launch the full project blindly. The chapter's argument is that careful planning creates conditions for creativity and can make execution faster. A useful response is therefore to show how each planning activity reduces a material uncertainty or improves a decision, and to stop analysis when additional work no longer changes the choice.
Study instruction: Design a short planning sprint for a complex project. For each activity, state the uncertainty it resolves, the decision it informs and the time limit that prevents analysis from becoming open-ended.
Key point: Planning should be purposeful and proportionate, not endless; a controlled experiment is safer than an uninformed leap.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
43. Q: How can the Electric Lady example be used responsibly in a project review without romanticizing its failures?
A: Show answer
A responsible review can recognize the project's distinctive design, the team's ingenuity and the lasting value of the studio while also recording its weak initial controls, interruptions, cost and schedule consequences. The review should separate creative achievements from the delivery method that produced them. It should ask which adaptations were genuinely valuable, which problems might have been avoided through better investigation, and whether the final outcome would justify repeating the same risk profile. The official studio history confirms the conversion of the Generation into a purpose-built studio and its 1970 opening, while the chapter supplies the detailed account of design and construction challenges. Balanced learning preserves the innovation without treating poor preparation as a virtue.
Study instruction: Prepare a lessons-learned report with separate sections for project outcome, delivery performance, creative adaptations, avoidable risks and practices worth repeating.
Key point: Celebrate the achievement while learning from the weaknesses; do not confuse a valuable asset with a sound process.
Source links: Electric Lady Studios — History; How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
44. Q: What practical questions should be asked before accepting a claim that a troubled project will be rescued by innovation?
A: Show answer
A sponsor should ask what evidence supports the expected innovation, whether the team has the capability and time to develop it, and what happens if the solution does not work. The sponsor should also quantify the cost and schedule exposure while the team experiments, identify alternative solutions and define when a rescue strategy will be abandoned. The chapter's central warning is that creative recoveries are memorable but uncommon in the broader sample. A credible innovation case should therefore describe a testable mechanism and measurable expected value rather than rely on confidence, heroic language or a famous analogy. The project should have a viable baseline plan even if the innovation produces no extraordinary upside.
Study instruction: Write a challenge checklist for a project proposal that relies on an unproven innovation. Include evidence, capability, testing, downside exposure, alternatives and exit criteria.
Key point: Treat innovation as a hypothesis to test, not as an assumed rescue that will offset weak planning.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand
45. Q: How can public-sector or development-project sponsors apply Chapter 7's argument when evaluating infrastructure proposals?
A: Show answer
Public sponsors commit funds that could otherwise support other services or investments, so the opportunity cost of poor planning is especially important. A proposal should be tested against comparable completed projects, with costs, schedules and benefits defined consistently. Sponsors should examine whether forecasts depend on unusually favourable assumptions, whether site and technical risks have been investigated, and whether funding is secure across the delivery period. The chapter's project sample includes infrastructure such as dams, rail lines, tunnels and bridges, making its warning relevant to these settings. This is an application of the chapter's reasoning, not a claim that every public project behaves identically. Context, governance and the quality of evidence still matter.
Study instruction: Select an infrastructure proposal and identify its reference class, forecast assumptions, opportunity costs, funding risks and decision gates. State what information an independent reviewer should demand before approval.
Key point: Public value requires scrutiny of both the proposed asset and the resources and alternatives sacrificed to deliver it.
Source links: Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand; Oxford Research Archive — Flyvbjerg (2016) accepted manuscript record; How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
46. Q: How should an NGO or humanitarian organization use the chapter's lessons when launching a complex programme?
A: Show answer
An NGO should not confuse urgency or a compelling mission with proof that a programme is ready for delivery. It can preserve the ambition while investigating implementation capacity, partner readiness, procurement conditions, access constraints, logistics, funding timing and measurable benefits. Comparable programme data can challenge assumptions about cost and reach. A staged pilot may be appropriate where uncertainty is material, provided the pilot is designed to generate evidence and does not expose beneficiaries to unmanaged risk. The chapter's opportunity-cost argument is also relevant: resources committed to an underprepared programme may be unavailable for other interventions. This is a practical extension of the chapter's principles, not a claim that its infrastructure sample directly estimates NGO programme performance.
Study instruction: Choose a proposed programme and draft a pre-launch review covering needs, delivery assumptions, partner capacity, funding cash flow, outcome measures, risks and alternatives.
Key point: Mission urgency should accelerate disciplined learning, not replace it.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Kahneman and Lovallo, Timid Choices and Bold Forecasts
47. Q: How can project teams measure whether planning has genuinely improved creativity rather than merely increased paperwork?
A: Show answer
Planning has improved creativity when it produces better options, tests important assumptions, reveals interactions between technical requirements and leads to decisions that reduce later rework. The volume of documents or number of meetings is not evidence of creative value. Teams can track the alternatives considered, prototypes tested, significant issues discovered before delivery, design changes made while inexpensive and the frequency of major late-stage redesign. They should also assess whether planning reviews allow dissent and exploration rather than simply confirm an existing preferred solution. The chapter's examples of Gehry, Pixar and Storyk point toward iteration and deliberate development, not paperwork for its own sake.
Study instruction: Design five indicators for a planning process. At least two should measure learning or alternative generation, and at least two should measure whether late rework or avoidable surprises decline.
Key point: Evaluate planning by the quality of learning and decisions it produces, not by the number of documents.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
48. Q: What is the difference between a project that adapts successfully and one that merely survives after overrunning?
A: Show answer
A project that adapts successfully converts new information into a better outcome whose value justifies the additional resources and risks incurred. A project that merely survives may reach completion but still deliver less value than forecast, consume excessive time or displace better alternatives. Chapter 7 warns against equating completion or fame with net success. Jaws and Electric Lady are remembered because their final outcomes were strong, while many troubled projects do not receive the same attention. A proper assessment therefore examines the original objectives, actual cost, schedule, benefits, quality and opportunity cost. Persistence is not itself proof of value; it must be judged against a realistic counterfactual and the resources consumed.
Study instruction: Create a post-project evaluation template that compares the original business case with actual cost, time, benefits, quality and alternatives. Include a separate judgement on whether adaptation created net value.
Key point: Completion is not the same as success; assess net value against the original case and realistic alternatives.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand
49. Q: Why should a project sponsor consider the people and institutions affected by delivery failures, not just the asset produced?
A: Show answer
A project is not only a physical output. Its delivery process affects workers, designers, suppliers, communities, public institutions and the future capacity of the sponsor. The Opera House example shows how a celebrated building can coexist with damage to the architect's career. The Electric Lady example shows how irregular funding can disrupt tradespeople and construction continuity. Opportunity costs also affect people who would have benefited from alternative projects that were displaced. A complete review should therefore examine who bears overruns, delays, reputational consequences and lost opportunities. This broader perspective prevents a narrow focus on the final asset from concealing costs imposed on others.
Study instruction: Prepare a stakeholder consequence map for a project failure. Identify direct financial losses, work disruption, reputational effects, displaced services and longer-term capability impacts.
Key point: Project performance includes consequences for people and future opportunities, not only the completed asset.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
50. Q: What does the chapter suggest about the relationship between luck, serendipity and repeatable project success?
A: Show answer
Luck and serendipity can influence a project's trajectory, as the Electric Lady origin story demonstrates. Storyk received an unusually consequential opportunity despite limited prior studio experience, and the project became the beginning of a distinguished career. But a favourable accident is not a process that can be reliably scheduled or reproduced. Storyk's later practice of slowing down and reviewing work suggests that professional success came to depend on deliberate methods rather than repeated leaps in the dark. The chapter does not require project leaders to deny luck; it asks them not to build their operating model around it. Good planning improves the conditions under which talent and fortunate discoveries can be used effectively.
Study instruction: Separate the lucky events in a project story from the capabilities and processes that could be deliberately repeated. Identify which factors a sponsor can control.
Key point: Acknowledge luck, but build repeatable systems around preparation, learning and execution.
Source links: Electric Lady Studios — History; How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
51. Q: How can an organization avoid turning an exceptional success story into a misleading internal management myth?
A: Show answer
Organizations can prevent this by documenting unsuccessful and abandoned initiatives as carefully as celebrated successes. They should record the original forecast, actual cost, time, benefits, key changes and reasons for continuation or cancellation. Case studies should identify the denominator of comparable attempts and distinguish genuine innovation from luck or external conditions. Leaders should also invite independent review rather than allowing a charismatic story to settle a policy debate. The chapter's survivorship-bias discussion shows why an organization that repeatedly tells only its rescue stories may normalize underplanning. Balanced learning does not diminish achievement; it makes the lesson more useful and reduces the risk of repeating avoidable failures.
Study instruction: Review your organization's project case studies. Identify which failures, cancellations or ordinary outcomes are missing and propose a standard template that records them.
Key point: Institutional memory should include failures and abandoned work, not just celebrated survivors.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand
52. Q: How should a decision-maker assess whether a project's unexpected benefits truly compensate for its cost overrun?
A: Show answer
The comparison should use consistent baseline definitions and measure both sides of the business case. The decision-maker should calculate the difference between forecast and actual costs, then compare forecast and realized benefits over an appropriate period. Benefits should be valued realistically and not inferred merely from publicity, popularity or the fact that the asset was completed. The analysis should also consider delay, operating costs, quality, distribution of benefits and opportunity cost. Chapter 7's central empirical question is whether benefit overruns are large enough to exceed cost overruns; its reported sample result is that they are not, on average. For an individual project, the calculation must be transparent about assumptions and measurement limits.
Study instruction: Build a simple benefit-cost variance table for a completed project. Define the baseline, actual measurement date, cost categories, benefit measures and any important unpriced effects.
Key point: A rescue claim requires a comparable, quantified net-benefit test, not a celebratory narrative.
Source links: Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand; How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
53. Q: What is the chapter's strongest response to the claim that revealing difficulties during planning will discourage worthwhile projects?
A: Show answer
Flyvbjerg accepts that careful planning may reveal serious difficulties that make a project appear less attractive. But hiding those difficulties does not make them disappear; it only postpones their discovery until the project has committed more money, time and reputation. The team can use the information to redesign, develop new solutions, reduce scope or decide that another option offers greater value. The chapter argues that creativity can be mobilized during planning, when there is more freedom to explore. The alternative to blind optimism is therefore not paralysis. It is informed commitment, with a better chance of delivering the intended outcome and protecting resources if the case no longer holds.
Study instruction: Write a short decision memo responding to the statement 'If we investigate too much, we may decide not to start.' Explain how early discovery can lead to redesign, staged testing or a better-informed stop decision.
Key point: Information about difficulty is an input to better choices, not a reason to abandon ambition automatically.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
54. Q: How should a team balance ambition with realism when proposing a project that has never been attempted before?
A: Show answer
An unprecedented project may lack a close reference class, but that does not make forecasting impossible or justify ignoring risk. The team can decompose the work into components, compare those components with analogous projects, test critical technologies, consult experienced practitioners and identify where uncertainty remains genuinely novel. It should distinguish an ambitious outcome from assumptions about the ease of delivery. A strong proposal explains why the project is worth pursuing, what evidence supports its feasibility, what could invalidate the case and how learning will be incorporated before irreversible commitments. Chapter 7's lesson is to retain the ambition while replacing blind confidence with deliberate preparation and structured creativity.
Study instruction: For a novel project, break the concept into work packages. For each, identify an analogue, an unresolved uncertainty, a test and a decision that depends on the result.
Key point: Novelty calls for more explicit learning and risk treatment, not less planning.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Kahneman and Lovallo, Timid Choices and Bold Forecasts
55. Q: What final decision rule does Chapter 7 offer for leaders responsible for ambitious projects?
A: Show answer
Leaders should not rely on ignorance to get projects started or on desperation to generate the creativity needed to finish them. They should investigate the real problem, use relevant historical evidence, test cost and benefit assumptions, explore alternatives and develop creative solutions while the project is still being shaped. They should recognize that some uncertainty will remain and preserve the capacity to adapt, but not assume that unexpected benefits will compensate for foreseeable errors. Once the project is sufficiently understood and resourced, the team should execute decisively. The chapter's synthesis is Think Slow, Act Fast: use deliberate planning to improve the decision and reduce avoidable rework, then act with focus and speed.
Study instruction: Summarize the chapter as a one-page project leadership checklist. Include evidence, reference-class comparison, creative iteration, stress management, funding, opportunity cost, decision gates and execution readiness.
Key point: Plan carefully enough to create a credible path; preserve creativity; then execute quickly and deliberately.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page; Bent Flyvbjerg, The Fallacy of Beneficial Ignorance: A Test of Hirschman’s Hiding Hand; Byron, Khazanchi and Nazarian, The Relationship Between Stressors and Creativity: A Meta-Analysis
Rows: 55
Table 8
Building One Determined Delivery Team for Major Projects
1. Q: Why does a strong project plan still need an equally strong delivery team?
A: Show answer
A plan establishes what should be built, the sequence of work, the expected resources, and the risks that must be managed. It does not by itself make people from separate employers, professions, and interest groups coordinate their decisions and actions. Delivery requires people who can resolve interfaces, adapt to emerging problems, share information, and sustain effort toward a common outcome. The chapter therefore distinguishes the slow thinking that produces a sound plan from the fast, coordinated action needed to execute it. The plan and the team are complements: a capable team cannot reliably rescue a fundamentally poor plan, while a strong plan can still fail when the people responsible for delivery are fragmented or inexperienced.
Study instruction: Identify one project in which planning quality and delivery-team capability could be assessed separately. Explain what each contributes and where one cannot compensate for the absence of the other.
Key point: Planning defines a credible route; a capable team turns it into coordinated delivery.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by a “single, determined organism”?
A: Show answer
The phrase describes a project workforce that behaves as an integrated whole rather than as a collection of companies protecting separate interests. People may retain different employers, contracts, specialist skills, and professional obligations, but they understand that their immediate project identity and shared objective take priority when coordinating delivery. A single organism does not mean uniformity or the removal of specialist disagreement. It means that information, decisions, incentives, and problem-solving connect across organizational boundaries. The Heathrow Terminal 5 example shows how a client can deliberately create this condition through leadership, risk-sharing arrangements, shared identity, credible working conditions, worker participation, and permission to speak up.
Study instruction: Draw an organizational map of a multi-contractor project. Mark the boundaries that could obstruct cooperation, then specify the mechanisms needed to make the project operate as one delivery system.
Key point: Integration is an intentionally designed way of working, not simply a label applied to a group of contractors.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
3. Q: Why does the chapter place such value on experienced, previously tested teams?
A: Show answer
Experience is valuable because complex delivery depends on tacit knowledge as well as formal procedures. People who have worked together on comparable projects develop practical judgment about sequencing, interfaces, suppliers, equipment, likely failure points, and one another's working styles. Repeated collaboration also creates trust and reduces the time needed to establish communication and coordination. The chapter uses Frank Crowe's dam-building team and the long-standing collaborators around Frank Gehry to illustrate how accumulated project experience can become a delivery capability. Their value is not merely that individuals have impressive résumés; it is that the team has already learned how to perform together under demanding conditions.
Study instruction: Compare a team of individually qualified newcomers with a team that has completed similar work together. List the tacit knowledge, relationships, and coordination routines that the second team may bring.
Key point: Team experience includes collective learning and working relationships, not just individual credentials.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
4. Q: What does the Hoover Dam example reveal about the role of the project leader and the team?
A: Show answer
The chapter attributes much of the Hoover Dam's performance to Frank Crowe and the experienced, loyal team he had developed through years of dam construction. The project was delivered in a remote, difficult, and dangerous setting, yet it finished ahead of schedule and under budget. The example suggests that a proven leader can contribute more than personal technical expertise: the leader can bring a functioning delivery network with established trust, shared methods, and practical experience. The implication is that project owners should evaluate the leader and the team as a delivery unit, rather than assuming that appointing one celebrated individual is enough.
Study instruction: Write a short case note identifying which elements of Crowe's team appear transferable to another project and which would require local adaptation.
Key point: A proven project leader's effectiveness is amplified by a tested team that can execute with the leader.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
5. Q: Why is experience in an adjacent industry not necessarily a substitute for experience in the project's own domain?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a Canadian hydroelectric dam whose executives came from oil and gas because the owners reasoned that both sectors deliver large projects. The analogy ignored the distinctive technical, regulatory, environmental, geological, stakeholder, and operating conditions of hydroelectric development. The project became a major failure, threatening the wider provincial economy. Experience in a neighboring industry may provide useful transferable capabilities, but it does not automatically supply the domain knowledge needed to recognize sector-specific risks and dependencies. Owners should distinguish general megaproject management competence from experience with the particular asset type and its critical systems.
Study instruction: For a proposed project, list five capabilities that are genuinely transferable from an adjacent sector and five domain-specific capabilities that must be demonstrated or acquired.
Key point: Similarity in project size does not establish similarity in delivery risk or technical requirements.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
6. Q: How should an owner decide whether to hire an existing team or build one?
A: Show answer
The chapter's preferred option is to hire a proven team when one exists and is available. Doing so can reduce the time and uncertainty involved in creating shared routines, trust, and coordination capability. If the required team does not exist or is committed elsewhere, the owner must deliberately assemble one. That requires more than selecting contractors: leadership must shape incentives, identity, purpose, communication, and working conditions so the participants become a functioning team. The choice should be made early. Waiting until a project is in trouble means the owner is trying to establish relationships and solve delivery failures at the same time.
Study instruction: Prepare a procurement decision note comparing an available proven team with a newly assembled team. Include availability, domain experience, integration cost, mobilization time, and delivery risk.
Key point: Acquire proven collective capability when possible; otherwise invest deliberately in creating it before delivery pressure peaks.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
7. Q: What made Heathrow Terminal 5 an unusually demanding delivery environment?
A: Show answer
T5 was not simply a large building. It combined a major terminal with gates, tunnels, roads, parking, rail links, stations, baggage handling, catering, safety systems, electronic infrastructure, and a new air traffic control tower. The work had to fit into a constrained site between active runways while Heathrow continued operating. These elements were interdependent: a delay or design problem in one package could obstruct work elsewhere or affect the airport's continuing operations. The chapter uses this complexity to explain why technical planning alone was insufficient and why the project needed unusually strong integration across employers, disciplines, and work packages.
Study instruction: Create a dependency map for an airport expansion. Show at least eight physical or operational systems and identify the interfaces where a failure or delay could affect other systems.
Key point: A complex project is a connected operating system, not merely a collection of separate construction packages.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
8. Q: Why did BAA publicly commit to a precise T5 opening deadline so early?
A: Show answer
BAA announced a firm opening date after years of planning and consultation, despite the scale and complexity of the work. The deadline created a clear, shared target and made the consequences of ordinary project performance visible. BAA's comparison with other major UK and international construction and airport projects suggested that typical performance would leave T5 about a year late and roughly $1 billion over budget. The deadline therefore represented an explicit ambition to outperform the reference class, not a prediction that the project would naturally meet the date. Such a commitment can focus coordination, but it also increases pressure and must be supported by credible planning, risk management, and operational readiness.
Study instruction: Distinguish a target date, a forecast date, and a contractual commitment. For a project of your choice, state what evidence and governance should support each.
Key point: A demanding deadline can focus a team, but commitment alone does not create delivery capability.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
9. Q: What were the three broad strategies BAA used to improve T5 delivery?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies planning, a different construction method, and people as the three strategies. First, BAA used detailed digital representations and simulations so that the project could be tested virtually before being built. Second, it moved substantial fabrication from the worksite to factories, using digital specifications to manufacture components for later assembly. Third, it deliberately created a unified workforce with aligned interests, shared identity, a meaningful purpose, good working conditions, and open communication. These strategies reinforced one another: digital planning enabled off-site manufacture, while a cooperative team was needed to coordinate design, factories, suppliers, logistics, assembly, and airport operations.
Study instruction: Prepare a three-column review of a major project showing the planning, production-method, and people strategies. Identify one dependency between each pair of strategies.
Key point: T5's delivery approach combined digital preparation, industrialized production, and deliberate team-building.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
10. Q: How did T5's digital planning support its construction approach?
A: Show answer
The detailed digital representations provided precise specifications that could be used to simulate the creation and operation of T5 before committing to the physical work. They also supplied the information needed by factories to manufacture components accurately. This reduced reliance on measuring, cutting, shaping, and welding every element at the worksite. The digital model therefore linked design decisions to production and assembly rather than serving only as a visualization. Its value depended on the accuracy, coordination, and practical use of the information by designers, manufacturers, contractors, and site teams.
Study instruction: Trace one building component from digital design through manufacture, transport, installation, and inspection. Identify the information that must remain consistent at each handoff.
Key point: A digital model creates delivery value when it becomes a shared production and coordination tool.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Proposal for a New Approach to Building: Summary of Evidence — UK Government
11. Q: What is the difference between conventional site fabrication and T5's assembly-site approach?
A: Show answer
In conventional site fabrication, materials arrive at the worksite and are measured, cut, shaped, and joined there. T5 shifted much of that work to factories, where components were manufactured against detailed digital specifications and then transported to the site for assembly. The worksite consequently functioned more like an assembly operation than a traditional fabrication yard. This can improve repeatability, quality control, productivity, and predictability, while reducing some forms of on-site congestion and rework. It also changes the risk profile: factory capacity, transport, lifting, tolerances, interface design, and the timing of component deliveries become critical dependencies.
Study instruction: Compare the work sequence, quality controls, logistics requirements, and principal risks of site fabrication and off-site manufacture followed by assembly.
Key point: Moving work off site changes where production occurs and which interfaces must be controlled; it does not eliminate complexity.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Proposal for a New Approach to Building: Summary of Evidence — UK Government
12. Q: What is design for manufacture and assembly, and why did it matter at T5?
A: Show answer
Design for manufacture and assembly means developing the design with the production and installation processes in mind. Components are specified so that they can be manufactured efficiently and then fitted together with predictable interfaces. At T5, this approach connected precise digital design with factory production and on-site assembly. The chapter relates it to the way the automotive industry organizes production and to John Egan's advocacy for efficiency improvements in construction. It matters because design choices determine not only whether an asset can function, but also how reliably and economically it can be produced, transported, installed, and maintained.
Study instruction: Select a component from a construction project and identify design changes that could simplify manufacture, transport, assembly, inspection, and future replacement.
Key point: Design should account for how an asset will be made and assembled, not only how it will look or perform when complete.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Proposal for a New Approach to Building: Summary of Evidence — UK Government
13. Q: What conditions must be in place for off-site manufacture to improve project performance?
A: Show answer
Off-site manufacture is most useful when designs and interfaces are sufficiently mature, specifications are reliable, manufacturers have the required capacity, and transport and installation can be coordinated with the site sequence. Components must fit together within agreed tolerances, and responsibilities for design changes, quality assurance, defects, and acceptance must be clear. Digital information must be consistent across the parties using it. If these conditions are absent, off-site production can move problems upstream rather than remove them: an incorrect design may be replicated at scale, or a late component may stop several work fronts. The UK government's later work on platform-based design for manufacture and assembly similarly emphasizes standardized digital components, supply-chain readiness, and changes to commercial and technical processes.
Study instruction: Develop a readiness checklist for an off-site package covering design maturity, factory capacity, quality assurance, logistics, tolerances, interfaces, and change control.
Key point: Industrialized construction improves predictability only when design, supply chain, and assembly are integrated.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Proposal for a New Approach to Building: Summary of Evidence — UK Government
14. Q: Why did BAA treat people as a core delivery strategy rather than a human-resources side issue?
A: Show answer
Thousands of people from different organizations, occupations, management levels, and employment arrangements had to coordinate their work. Their separate cultures and interests could easily produce fragmented decisions and disputes. BAA recognized that the workforce had to become a team if the technical plan and production system were to function. Team-building therefore affected practical delivery: whether contractors helped one another, whether workers raised problems, whether designs were improved by site knowledge, and whether people kept the project moving when work packages conflicted. The chapter presents team dynamics as part of the operating system of the project, not as an optional morale program.
Study instruction: For a large project, identify three delivery risks that are fundamentally people-and-coordination risks rather than engineering-design risks. Explain how each would affect schedule or cost.
Key point: Human coordination is a production capability and should be designed into the project from the beginning.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
15. Q: How did BAA's intervention in the steelwork-concrete dispute prevent a local conflict from damaging T5?
A: Show answer
Richard Harper warned that the concrete contractor was falling behind and would leave his steelwork crew waiting. Under the original flat-fee arrangement, Harper's company risked absorbing the cost of idle workers and equipment. The two firms began blaming each other, and Harper's employer could have withdrawn. BAA recognized the wider project threat and changed the commercial arrangement to cost reimbursement with a percentage profit when milestones were achieved. This reduced the immediate incentive to defend the original fee at the expense of progress. The firms then coordinated a recovery plan: the concrete contractor added workers, while Harper's company moved some workers to other tasks until the work front was ready.
Study instruction: Reconstruct the dispute as a timeline: warning, incentive problem, conflict, client intervention, revised arrangement, and recovery actions. Identify the point at which early intervention prevented escalation.
Key point: A client can protect overall delivery by resolving interface disputes before contractors become locked into adversarial positions.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
16. Q: Why can a flat-fee contract create destructive behavior when one contractor depends on another?
A: Show answer
A flat fee can make a contractor bear the cost of delays caused by another party, even when the contractor cannot control that party's performance. In the T5 example, Harper's steelwork company faced the prospect of paying for workers and equipment that could not be used because the concrete contractor was behind. The contract encouraged the steelwork company to protect its own financial position, potentially through claims or withdrawal, rather than jointly solve the sequencing problem. The issue was not simply that one company was behaving badly; the commercial structure made cooperation costly for one side. Contract design should therefore be tested against real work dependencies, not evaluated only as a standalone bargain.
Study instruction: Map one interdependent contractor relationship and identify who bears the cost when one party's work is delayed by another. Assess whether the arrangement encourages cooperation or claims.
Key point: A contract can unintentionally reward local self-protection at the expense of total project performance.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
17. Q: How did the revised T5 payment arrangement change the contractors' incentives?
A: Show answer
BAA changed Harper's company's arrangement to a cost-reimbursable basis with a percentage profit linked to milestone achievement. The change reduced the immediate risk that the steelwork contractor would suffer a direct loss because another contractor had delayed the work. It also made progress toward shared milestones more valuable than winning an argument over fault. The firms could then discuss practical adjustments and preserve productivity. This illustrates incentive alignment: the commercial arrangement should make it rational for participants to help achieve the project outcome. It does not mean that every cost-reimbursable contract will work well; it requires transparent cost control, clear milestones, performance expectations, and governance.
Study instruction: Compare a fixed-price arrangement and a cost-reimbursable milestone-based arrangement for an interdependent work package. State which risks each party bears and what controls are needed.
Key point: Commercial incentives should support the behavior the project needs, not merely allocate blame after failure.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
18. Q: What does it mean that BAA assumed more project risk under the T5 approach?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that BAA took on more direct risk than it would have under conventional contracts. Rather than pushing every uncertainty and interface consequence onto individual suppliers, BAA accepted a larger share of the financial exposure while using positive incentives to encourage performance. The Major Projects Association's account of the T5 Agreement describes the principle that BAA held the risk and links it to changed behaviors and an integrated team environment. This did not make risks disappear. It changed who managed and absorbed them, with the intention of reducing the defensive behavior that fragmented risk transfer can create. An owner adopting such an approach must have the capability and financial resilience to manage the risks it retains.
Study instruction: For a hypothetical project, separate risks that the owner can manage centrally from risks a supplier can genuinely control. Explain which party is best placed to manage each and why.
Key point: Risk allocation is most effective when it places risk with the party able to manage it and preserves incentives for shared success.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
19. Q: Why does risk transfer not necessarily mean risk reduction?
A: Show answer
Transferring a risk contractually may change which party bears the financial consequence, but it does not remove the underlying physical, technical, schedule, or interface uncertainty. If a supplier cannot control the source of a risk, transferring it may lead to a higher price, defensive behavior, claims, insolvency exposure, or reduced cooperation. The T5 example shows an alternative: BAA retained more exposure while designing incentives around project milestones. This is not a universal argument for owners to retain every risk. It is a reminder to distinguish legal allocation from practical risk management and to ask whether the chosen allocation improves the behavior and capability needed to control the risk.
Study instruction: Choose three project risks and distinguish their physical source, the party best able to manage them, and the party contractually bearing the financial consequence.
Key point: A contractual transfer changes exposure; effective risk management requires control of the underlying cause.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
20. Q: How did positive incentives help align companies that might otherwise have competed against one another?
A: Show answer
BAA used incentives such as bonuses for meeting or beating benchmarks so that the firms had a common interest in completing T5 on time. The aim was to avoid arrangements in which one contractor could improve its own position by shifting delay or cost to another contractor. With incentives aligned, firms had more reason to exchange information, adjust their work, and solve shared problems. Positive incentives do not guarantee cooperation: milestones must be meaningful, performance must be measurable, and the project must avoid rewarding speed at the expense of safety or quality. The chapter's central point is that commercial rules shape whether contractors behave like teammates or adversaries.
Study instruction: Design three project-level incentives and test each for unintended consequences, including possible damage to safety, quality, or truthful reporting.
Key point: Rewarding shared outcomes can make cooperation commercially rational, provided the measures are balanced and credible.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
21. Q: What does the temporary ramp episode reveal about collaboration across contractor boundaries?
A: Show answer
The principal contractor's work initially prevented Harper's cranes from operating. Instead of turning the obstruction into a claim or waiting for BAA to arbitrate, the companies' executives met and worked out a practical solution: a temporary ramp that allowed both companies to continue working. The principal contractor built and paid for the ramp. The example shows how a shared delivery objective and established working relationships can turn an interface conflict into joint problem-solving. It also demonstrates that integration is visible in operational decisions: teams adapt methods and accept local costs when doing so protects the wider project.
Study instruction: Identify an interface conflict in a project you know. Propose two solutions that protect total project progress and state how the parties would agree responsibility and cost.
Key point: Integrated teams solve the work problem together instead of treating every obstruction as a claim opportunity.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
22. Q: Why did BAA prefer proven suppliers over simply selecting the lowest bidders?
A: Show answer
BAA recognized that the lowest tender price is not necessarily the lowest total project cost. A supplier unfamiliar with the owner, site, or required work may create additional coordination, quality, rework, delay, and supervision costs. BAA therefore favored companies with a demonstrated history of delivering what it needed and encouraged those companies to use experienced specialist subcontractors. The chapter's point is not that price competition is always wrong, but that procurement should evaluate delivery capability and whole-project consequences rather than initial price alone. A low bid can be expensive if it brings weak interfaces or unreliable performance.
Study instruction: Create a procurement scorecard that weighs price alongside relevant project experience, team continuity, safety, quality, capacity, and interface-management capability.
Key point: Select for total delivery value and proven capability, not just the smallest initial price.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
23. Q: How does continuity among suppliers create relational capital?
A: Show answer
When managers and specialists have worked together on previous sites, they may already understand one another's capabilities, communication habits, standards, and expectations. That familiarity can reduce the time spent negotiating routine interfaces and help people identify problems before they escalate. At T5, many managers and workers had decades of construction experience and had collaborated on projects in London and elsewhere in Britain. BAA deliberately used that existing rapport. Relational capital is not a substitute for formal scope, quality, safety, or accountability controls; rather, it makes those controls easier to apply because people have a foundation of trust and practical understanding.
Study instruction: Identify recurring supplier relationships in a project portfolio. Explain how continuity could improve coordination and what safeguards are still needed to prevent complacency or conflicts of interest.
Key point: Stable working relationships can lower coordination friction while formal controls remain essential.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
24. Q: Why did T5 tell workers that their team was T5 rather than their individual employer?
A: Show answer
Workers naturally identify with the company that hires them, pays them, and provides their usual management structure. On a multi-company project, that identity can encourage people to prioritize their employer's interests over the shared delivery outcome. BAA deliberately established a stronger project identity: everyone was told that the team was T5. Andrew Wolstenholme used the military cap-badge metaphor, asking people to set aside their usual organizational badge while working on the project. The message was reinforced through posters and repeated communication. It did not erase employers' legal responsibilities; it gave people a common identity for day-to-day collaboration.
Study instruction: Write a short project identity statement that can be understood by direct employees, contractors, subcontractors, and professional advisers. Check that it clarifies shared purpose without obscuring legal accountability.
Key point: A shared project identity helps people coordinate across company boundaries.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
25. Q: Why is a shared identity necessary for a multi-organization project team?
A: Show answer
A team is not created merely because people appear on the same organization chart or work at the same site. Team membership involves a sense of belonging and a belief that participants are working toward a common outcome. Without that identity, people may default to their employer, profession, contract, or department when priorities conflict. A shared project identity helps make cooperation across those boundaries legitimate and expected. It also clarifies which outcome should guide local decisions. Identity must be reinforced by actual behavior and fair treatment; a slogan that contradicts daily experience will not create durable commitment.
Study instruction: Assess a project using three questions: what identity do participants currently feel, what identity does leadership want, and what daily practices make that identity credible?
Key point: People cooperate as a team when they recognize themselves as members of the same purposeful group.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
26. Q: How did the “making history” message contribute to T5's sense of purpose?
A: Show answer
BAA connected routine construction work to a larger achievement by displaying images of major projects such as the Eiffel Tower, Grand Central Terminal, and the Thames Barrier alongside the message that T5 was making history too. Posters also promised that workers would be proud to say they had built T5. This gave the workforce a shared narrative: the project mattered beyond the immediate task or employer. Purpose can strengthen commitment when people can see how their work contributes to a meaningful collective result. The chapter is equally clear, however, that a purpose narrative must be supported by the way the project is actually managed.
Study instruction: Create a purpose statement for a project and connect it to three concrete contributions made by different occupational groups. Avoid relying only on grand language.
Key point: A compelling purpose can unite diverse roles when it is connected to real work and credible leadership behavior.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
27. Q: Why did T5's teamwork message gain credibility with workers who were initially cynical?
A: Show answer
Construction workers had learned from experience that management promises about teamwork often did not match site conditions. At T5, the message became credible because workers quickly saw practical differences: good toilets, showers, and canteens; prompt replacement of damaged or unsuitable protective equipment; attention to safety; consultation; and a willingness to listen. Richard Harper reported that people bought into the approach within days because BAA implemented what it said it would do. The example shows that trust is built through repeated observable actions, not slogans. Workers judge management culture by what happens when they need equipment, raise concerns, or encounter a problem.
Study instruction: Compare an organization's stated values with the daily experience of frontline workers. Identify three observable tests that would show whether management genuinely practices those values.
Key point: Credibility comes from consistent delivery on practical commitments.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
28. Q: How did worker welfare and good site facilities support productivity at T5?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats welfare facilities and equipment as operational inputs rather than decorative benefits. Workers who start a shift with functioning facilities, suitable protective equipment, and prompt replacements can concentrate on the work. Poor conditions create frustration, delay, discomfort, and safety risks that accumulate across thousands of people and working days. Harper emphasized that seemingly small provisions, such as dry gloves or clear safety glasses, made a major difference to workers. The productivity implication is cumulative: the effect of a better start to a shift is multiplied across the workforce and the duration of the project.
Study instruction: Prepare a site-readiness checklist covering welfare facilities, personal protective equipment, replacement arrangements, access, and response times for frontline requests.
Key point: Basic working conditions influence safety, morale, and productive time at scale.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
29. Q: Why did T5 involve skilled workers in design and workflow improvement?
A: Show answer
Workers who perform the tasks often possess practical knowledge that designers and managers may not see from drawings or office-based reviews. T5 invited workers to sit with designers and explore ways to improve designs and workflows. This made the design process more responsive to actual construction conditions and gave workers a stake in the resulting methods. Participation can reveal constructability problems, reduce rework, and increase acceptance of agreed standards. It should be structured so that suggestions are evaluated, decisions are recorded, and safety and engineering requirements remain intact.
Study instruction: Choose one work package and prepare five questions that a designer should ask the people who will manufacture, install, inspect, or maintain it.
Key point: Frontline expertise can improve design and implementation when it is invited early and acted upon.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
30. Q: What was the significance of the approximately 1,400 workmanship samples displayed at T5?
A: Show answer
After standards for finished work were agreed, skilled workers developed benchmarks that showed the level of workmanship expected. About 1,400 samples were photographed, cataloged, and displayed on site. Because workers helped create the benchmarks, they were more likely to regard them as legitimate and take ownership of applying them. Visual standards also make expectations concrete: people can compare actual work with an agreed reference rather than relying only on vague descriptions. Such samples can support consistency across crews and contractors, although they must be maintained and updated when specifications or approved methods change.
Study instruction: Design a visual quality benchmark for one trade or work package. Specify who approves it, how it is displayed, how nonconforming work is handled, and how revisions are controlled.
Key point: Standards are more effective when they are clear, visible, and developed with the people expected to use them.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
31. Q: What is psychological safety, and how did it matter at T5?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety is a shared belief that people can take interpersonal risks at work, such as asking questions, admitting uncertainty, offering ideas, or raising concerns, without being humiliated or punished merely for speaking. The chapter connects this concept with Amy Edmondson's research on team learning. At T5, workers were told that they had both the right and responsibility to speak up, and they knew BAA would support them. This made it more likely that practical problems and improvement ideas would reach decision-makers. Psychological safety does not mean that every suggestion is accepted or that performance standards are relaxed; it means concerns can be raised and examined openly.
Study instruction: Describe a recent situation in which a worker might have stayed silent. Identify the interpersonal risk and propose a management response that would make speaking up safer.
Key point: Psychological safety enables candid information-sharing and learning without removing accountability.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Amy Edmondson
32. Q: How does psychological safety help a project learn and perform?
A: Show answer
When people believe it is safe to ask questions, request help, report mistakes, and challenge assumptions, the team can detect problems earlier and learn from them. Edmondson's study of 51 work teams found that psychological safety was associated with learning behavior, and that learning behavior mediated the relationship between psychological safety and team performance. The chapter applies this idea to T5's expectation that bad news should travel fast. The mechanism is practical: early information gives managers more time to investigate, coordinate responses, and limit the consequences. Psychological safety is not a guarantee of success, but silence makes adaptation much harder.
Study instruction: Select one recurring project problem and trace how early reporting, open discussion, and team learning could reduce its consequences. Identify the evidence you would monitor.
Key point: Teams improve when people can surface problems early enough for the organization to learn and respond.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Amy Edmondson
33. Q: What does the principle that “bad news travels fast” require from project leadership?
A: Show answer
It requires leaders to make it safe and worthwhile for people to report unfavorable information promptly, and then to respond constructively. Workers must know that a concern will be heard, investigated, and acted upon rather than triggering retaliation or blame. Leaders also need clear channels for escalation, defined response responsibilities, and a willingness to share uncomfortable information upward and across organizational boundaries. The principle is not satisfied by inviting feedback while ignoring it. If managers punish messengers, delay decisions, or conceal inconvenient facts, people will learn to withhold information and the project will lose an important early-warning system.
Study instruction: Draft a bad-news escalation protocol showing who can report, where reports go, expected response times, protection from retaliation, and how decisions are communicated back.
Key point: Fast warning depends on safe reporting and visible management response, not on slogans about openness.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Amy Edmondson
34. Q: How can a project combine psychological safety with high performance standards?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety and accountability address different questions. Safety concerns whether people can speak honestly and take interpersonal risks; accountability concerns whether people meet clear standards and fulfill responsibilities. A strong project needs both. Workers should be able to report a defect without fear of humiliation, while the organization still requires the defect to be corrected and the underlying process improved. At T5, workers helped define workmanship benchmarks, which combined participation with explicit expectations. Leaders should distinguish honest reporting and learning from negligence or deliberate noncompliance, applying fair investigation and proportionate consequences.
Study instruction: Build a two-axis assessment of a team using psychological safety and performance accountability. Describe the likely behavior when either dimension is high or low.
Key point: Openness is compatible with discipline when expectations are clear and responses are fair.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Amy Edmondson
35. Q: Why must the project culture include frontline and blue-collar workers as fully as executives?
A: Show answer
T5 involved people across a wide range of occupations, from executives and lawyers to electricians, welders, drivers, cleaners, and many others. The chapter argues that people at every level needed to feel equally part of what was being built. Frontline workers often see practical constraints, safety hazards, and quality issues before senior managers do. If the culture treats them as peripheral, the project loses both their knowledge and their commitment. Inclusion therefore has a delivery function: it helps information move across the hierarchy and reinforces the shared identity needed for coordinated execution.
Study instruction: Map a project's occupational groups and identify whose knowledge is routinely excluded from planning, design, risk review, or lessons-learned meetings.
Key point: A project team is only as integrated as its least-heard but operationally important participants.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
