36. Q: What leadership capabilities are needed to create an integrated project team?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes leaders who understand both the “what” and the “how”: they must define the delivery objective and deliberately shape the behaviors and conditions that make delivery possible. This includes choosing experienced participants, intervening early in disputes, designing incentives, communicating a shared identity and purpose, providing good working conditions, consulting workers, and protecting the right to speak up. Leaders must also model the culture they demand. A message about cooperation loses credibility if managers reward internal competition, ignore safety concerns, or treat bad news as disloyalty.
Study instruction: Assess a project leader against six capabilities: domain judgment, team selection, incentive design, conflict intervention, frontline engagement, and response to bad news. Provide evidence for each.
Key point: Team-building is active leadership work that must be visible in decisions and operating practices.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
37. Q: Why was early intervention in disputes so important to T5's delivery?
A: Show answer
A local disagreement can become a project-wide problem when it affects a critical work sequence, creates idle labor or equipment, damages relationships, or causes a contractor to withdraw. BAA intervened when it saw the dispute between the steelwork and concrete contractors beginning to escalate. It changed the incentive arrangement before the conflict became a lawsuit or work stoppage. Early intervention allowed the parties to focus on recovering the schedule instead of defending their contractual positions. Project leaders should therefore monitor interface health and intervene when a dispute threatens the shared objective, while still ensuring that decisions are transparent and contractually governed.
Study instruction: Create an escalation trigger list for contractor disputes, including schedule exposure, blocked work fronts, safety implications, resource idling, and threats of withdrawal.
Key point: The cost of resolving a conflict is often lower before it becomes a formal claim or stoppage.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
38. Q: How did T5's shared identity, purpose, and standards reinforce one another?
A: Show answer
Identity answered who the team was: the project was T5, not simply a collection of employers. Purpose answered why the work mattered: the workforce was making history by delivering an important national infrastructure asset. Shared standards answered what good work looked like and how participants should judge it. Together, these elements made coordination more concrete. People could identify with the same project, see a reason to contribute, and use common quality expectations to guide decisions. None is sufficient alone: identity without standards can be vague, purpose without credible treatment can sound like propaganda, and standards without belonging can be experienced as imposed rules.
Study instruction: For a project, write one sentence for identity, one for purpose, and one for the standard of performance. Explain how daily decisions would reinforce all three.
Key point: A cohesive team needs a common “who,” “why,” and “what good looks like.”
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
39. Q: What can the T5 and Wembley comparison teach about team culture?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts T5's reported cooperation and pride with the conflict, work stoppages, delay, and cost escalation associated with the new Wembley Stadium. The comparison suggests that a project's symbolic importance does not automatically create a shared team identity. A national stadium may sound inherently inspiring, yet the workforce still needs leadership, aligned incentives, credible working conditions, and practical cooperation. The comparison should not be read as proof that culture alone explains every difference between the projects; projects have different designs, risks, governance, and circumstances. Its lesson is that purpose must be actively translated into working relationships and delivery practices.
Study instruction: Compare two projects with different delivery outcomes. Separate evidence about team behavior from differences in scope, technical risk, governance, and external conditions.
Key point: A project's symbolic importance does not substitute for the deliberate work of building a cooperative delivery team.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
40. Q: What evidence indicates that T5's construction delivery succeeded, and what qualifications should be retained?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that T5 was completed on budget and on time for its revised opening date, which was moved three days earlier than the publicly announced March 30 date to March 27, 2008. The Major Projects Association's 2007 case study described the £4.3 billion programme as on track against cost, time, quality, and safety objectives and documented the bespoke T5 Agreement and integrated-team approach. However, construction completion and operational opening are not identical tests of success. The terminal experienced significant early operational problems, so a balanced assessment must distinguish delivery of the physical programme from readiness of the operator, staff, systems, and baggage processes.
Study instruction: Create a project outcome scorecard with separate measures for construction completion, cost, quality, safety, commissioning, operational readiness, and user experience.
Key point: Assess megaproject success across the full asset lifecycle, not by one schedule or budget milestone alone.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
House of Commons Transport Committee: Heathrow Terminal 5
41. Q: What went wrong during T5's initial operational opening, despite the construction achievement?
A: Show answer
The chapter acknowledges that baggage-distribution problems during the first days forced British Airways to cancel flights, creating embarrassment and expense before the system stabilized. The House of Commons Transport Committee reported that 36,584 passengers were affected on the first day and identified insufficient communication between owner and operator, together with poor staff training and system testing, as major causes of the problems. The committee also noted contingency-planning weaknesses and the risks inherent in switching a complex terminal into operation. The lesson is not that the construction team-building effort was worthless; it is that operational transition requires its own integration, rehearsal, staffing, communications, and contingency arrangements.
Study instruction: Prepare an operational-readiness review for a new facility. Include operator handover, staff familiarization, end-to-end system testing, contingency plans, access arrangements, and escalation procedures.
Key point: A facility can be physically delivered on time yet still fail at opening if operational readiness is inadequate.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
House of Commons Transport Committee: Heathrow Terminal 5
42. Q: Why should commissioning and operational readiness be managed as a distinct delivery challenge?
A: Show answer
Commissioning tests whether systems work individually and together under realistic conditions; operational readiness tests whether people, procedures, suppliers, and the organization can use those systems safely and reliably. T5's early difficulties illustrate that a completed terminal and installed baggage technology did not automatically ensure a smooth opening. Staff needed training and familiarization, the owner and operator needed effective communication, and the integrated systems required adequate testing and contingency arrangements. These activities should be planned, resourced, and governed as core project work rather than compressed into the final days before opening.
Study instruction: Develop a readiness gate for a complex asset with objective pass criteria for systems, people, procedures, interfaces, and contingency response. Specify who has authority to delay opening.
Key point: Physical completion is not proof that the asset and its operator are ready for service.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
House of Commons Transport Committee: Heathrow Terminal 5
43. Q: What is the business case for spending more on team development before problems occur?
A: Show answer
BAA spent significant time, money, and management effort developing team dynamics and accepted greater direct financial risk. The chapter argues that this investment was justified because ordinary project performance could have produced a year of delay and a very large cost overrun. Team development was therefore a preventive investment: it reduced the probability that fragmented incentives, conflict, poor communication, and low commitment would undermine delivery. The relevant comparison is not team-building cost versus zero; it is the upfront cost versus the expected cost of delay, rework, claims, idle resources, reputational damage, and failure. The benefits are difficult to capture in a single line item but can be material across the project.
Study instruction: Prepare a business case for team integration. Estimate the cost of workshops, leadership time, shared systems, and improved site conditions, then compare it with plausible costs of delay and conflict.
Key point: The economics of team development should be judged against the cost of preventable delivery failure.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
44. Q: How can a project owner assess whether a team is becoming genuinely integrated?
A: Show answer
No single survey or workshop proves that integration exists. An owner should look for observable behavior: contractors share information before being asked, disputes are resolved at working level, risks are raised early, teams coordinate around common milestones, and participants help solve problems outside their narrow contractual scope. Other indicators include stable key personnel, joint planning, consistent quality standards, prompt closure of interface actions, worker participation, and evidence that management responds constructively to concerns. Measures should combine qualitative feedback with operational data, because a team may report high morale while still experiencing recurring coordination failures.
Study instruction: Design a monthly team-integration review with at least five behavioral indicators and five delivery indicators. Explain how you would validate survey responses against observed practice.
Key point: Measure integration through what people actually do together, not only through statements of team spirit.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Amy Edmondson
45. Q: What practical steps should an owner take when forming a project team from multiple organizations?
A: Show answer
The chapter's T5 case suggests a sequence. First, identify the capabilities and experience the project needs and select credible organizations and people. Second, define the project outcome and make the project identity explicit. Third, structure contracts and incentives so that participants benefit from shared progress rather than shifting costs. Fourth, establish common standards, communication routes, and procedures for resolving disputes. Fifth, provide safe, productive working conditions and involve frontline specialists in design and improvement. Finally, reinforce the culture through leadership behavior and fast responses to problems. These steps should begin during procurement and mobilization, not after the project has entered a crisis.
Study instruction: Turn the sequence into a 90-day mobilization plan with named owners, deliverables, and evidence of completion for team selection, incentives, identity, standards, and worker engagement.
Key point: Integration is built through linked procurement, commercial, operational, and cultural decisions from the outset.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
46. Q: How should project leaders prevent a shared project identity from obscuring accountability?
A: Show answer
A common identity should encourage cooperation, not blur who is responsible for decisions, safety, quality, cost, or statutory duties. Each organization and role still needs clear authority, scope, reporting lines, and obligations. The project identity provides a common purpose for resolving interfaces; it does not replace formal governance or legal accountability. A well-designed arrangement makes both visible: people know they are working for the success of the project while understanding who approves a design, controls a work activity, accepts a deliverable, or reports a safety incident. Ambiguity about responsibility can undermine the very integration the identity is intended to create.
Study instruction: For a multi-contractor project, prepare a responsibility matrix for design approval, safety, quality acceptance, change control, cost, and operational handover. Check it against the shared-team charter.
Key point: Shared identity should unite behavior while formal authority and accountability remain explicit.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
47. Q: What are the risks of using incentives that focus too narrowly on schedule?
A: Show answer
Schedule incentives can encourage faster progress, but if they are not balanced they may also encourage unsafe work, hidden defects, premature handover, or optimistic reporting. T5's use of bonuses for meeting or beating benchmarks is presented as a way to align companies around delivery, but the project also had objectives for quality and safety. A sound incentive system should therefore define a balanced set of outcomes, establish reliable measurement, and protect against gaming. It should reward genuine project performance rather than apparent progress that creates downstream cost or operational risk.
Study instruction: Review a proposed milestone bonus and identify at least three ways it could be gamed. Add quality, safety, and readiness safeguards to the measure.
Key point: Incentives must reward balanced project outcomes rather than speed in isolation.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
48. Q: How can a project preserve cooperation when contractors have legitimate differences in commercial interests?
A: Show answer
Cooperation does not require every company to have identical commercial objectives or to abandon its contractual rights. It requires an arrangement in which the project outcome is sufficiently aligned with each party's success and in which disputes can be addressed without immediately threatening the whole relationship. T5 used shared milestones and a greater owner role in risk management, while managers relied on established relationships and direct problem-solving. Clear change control, transparent cost information, fair dispute procedures, and explicit escalation routes remain necessary. The objective is to make collaboration the normal first response while preserving the ability to resolve genuine contractual disagreements.
Study instruction: Draft a dispute-resolution pathway with working-level negotiation, project leadership intervention, formal determination, and escalation. State when each stage should be triggered.
Key point: Integrated delivery requires both collaborative behavior and credible formal mechanisms for resolving unresolved disputes.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
49. Q: How does team familiarity help manage complex project interfaces?
A: Show answer
Familiarity gives participants a practical understanding of how other organizations work, what information they need, and where coordination problems are likely to occur. At T5, many people had worked together on earlier construction projects, which created rapport before the new programme began. That history can accelerate decisions and make it easier to raise concerns directly. However, familiarity should not be mistaken for proof that an interface is controlled. New scope, new technology, and new operating constraints still require explicit interface definitions, documented assumptions, and formal acceptance criteria.
Study instruction: Identify the ten most important interfaces in a project and classify each as supported by prior working relationships, formal controls, or both. Identify gaps that familiarity may conceal.
Key point: Trust speeds coordination, but documented interface management remains necessary.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
50. Q: What role should workers play in identifying and managing project risk?
A: Show answer
Workers and supervisors often observe conditions that are difficult to detect through executive reports: blocked access, unsafe sequencing, poor component fit, missing materials, unrealistic production rates, or quality drift. T5's approach encouraged people at every level to offer ideas and report grievances, and it involved skilled workers in improving designs and benchmarks. This creates a broader risk-sensing network. For it to work, managers must respond promptly, explain decisions, and protect people from retaliation. Frontline reporting should feed into formal risk registers and corrective-action systems rather than remain informal conversation.
Study instruction: Select one work package and list risks likely to be noticed first by frontline staff. Design a reporting route that links those observations to project-level risk decisions.
Key point: Frontline knowledge is a critical source of early-warning information when the culture allows it to be heard.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Amy Edmondson
51. Q: How does T5 illustrate the connection between project culture and productivity?
A: Show answer
Culture influenced practical production conditions at T5. Contractors had reasons to help one another, workers had a common project identity, managers provided good facilities and equipment, and skilled people contributed to design and quality standards. These conditions reduced friction and made it easier to maintain productive work fronts. The chapter's examples show that culture is not separate from operations: it affects whether cranes can keep working, whether workers wait for materials, whether problems are reported, and whether teams coordinate around shared milestones. Productivity is therefore shaped by the social and commercial environment in which technical work takes place.
Study instruction: For a delayed project, classify causes into technical, logistical, commercial, and cultural categories. Identify which cultural or incentive changes could remove measurable lost time.
Key point: Culture affects productivity through concrete mechanisms such as coordination, information flow, and reduced conflict.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
52. Q: Why does the chapter describe the team as a capability that must be created before delivery accelerates?
A: Show answer
Once execution begins, schedule pressure makes it harder to establish trust, agree incentives, resolve organizational identities, and build common routines. If those foundations are missing, the team must negotiate its way through basic relationships while also managing technical work and emerging risks. T5's deliberate approach began early, allowing shared identity, working conditions, standards, and dispute mechanisms to take hold before the most demanding delivery tasks. The chapter's broader plan-slow, act-fast logic implies that preparation should include not only design and forecasting but also the relationships and operating arrangements needed for execution.
Study instruction: Prepare a pre-delivery readiness gate covering team composition, prior experience, commercial alignment, project identity, worker voice, and dispute resolution. State which gaps would prevent mobilization.
Key point: Fast execution depends on slow, deliberate preparation of the human system as well as the technical plan.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
53. Q: What does the chapter's final discussion reveal about fat-tailed project risk?
A: Show answer
The closing section returns to the book's concern with projects that can produce extremely large overruns or failures, not merely modest deviations. The chapter describes these as black-swan outcomes and says that many project types in the authors' database are fat-tailed, including nuclear power plants, hydroelectric dams, information technology, tunnels, major buildings, and aerospace. A fat-tailed risk profile means that extreme adverse outcomes are more consequential or more likely than a thin-tailed intuition might suggest. The chapter contrasts these with five project types it identifies as not fat-tailed: solar power, wind power, fossil thermal power, electricity transmission, and roads. The detailed explanation is deliberately deferred to the final chapter.
Study instruction: Define fat-tailed risk in your own words and classify three project types you work with. Explain why average cost or schedule deviations may fail to capture extreme outcomes.
Key point: Some project categories have exposure to exceptionally large downside outcomes, so ordinary averages can understate risk.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
54. Q: Which five project types does the chapter identify as not fat-tailed, and why is the distinction important?
A: Show answer
The chapter names solar power, wind power, fossil thermal power, electricity transmission, and roads as the five project types in its database that are not fat-tailed. It further states that wind and solar projects are the best-performing project types in the database by a comfortable margin. The distinction matters because project owners should not assume that all infrastructure categories share the same distribution of cost and schedule outcomes. A project type with relatively contained downside risk may be a safer investment than one with a material probability of extreme overrun. The chapter raises the puzzle but postpones its full explanation to the next and final chapter.
Study instruction: Create a comparison table for the five project types and at least three fat-tailed categories mentioned in the chapter. Record what further evidence would be needed before applying the distinction to a specific project.
Key point: Project-type risk profiles differ; the chapter identifies five exceptions but defers the explanation of their performance.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
55. Q: Why does the chapter single out wind and solar power as unusually reliable project types?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that wind and solar projects are the strongest performers in the authors' project database and asks what makes them safer bets than other categories. It does not provide the complete causal explanation here; it reserves that answer for the final chapter. The appropriate reading is therefore to treat the observation as a research finding presented by the authors and the causal mechanism as an open question at this point in the book. This distinction matters because readers should not invent a simple explanation—such as smaller components or modularity—unless the later analysis supports it.
Study instruction: Write two separate statements: one describing the chapter's reported performance pattern and another listing hypotheses that would need to be tested to explain it.
Key point: Do not confuse a reported pattern with its causal explanation; the chapter deliberately leaves the puzzle open.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
56. Q: How does the chapter connect team-building to the possibility of scaling up major projects?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that large projects need more than a strong plan: they need an integrated team capable of acting quickly across organizational boundaries. It closes by promising a model that combines ideas from earlier chapters and can be applied from small undertakings to subways, satellites, and very large infrastructure. For projects that must scale substantially, the model is presented as especially important because it could reduce cost, risk, and delivery time while improving quality. Team-building is one part of that wider model: large-scale execution depends on the ability to coordinate many people, suppliers, and systems as one delivery effort.
Study instruction: Choose a project that must scale from a pilot to a much larger deployment. Identify which team capabilities, relationships, and governance arrangements must be replicated or redesigned.
Key point: Scaling is not just increasing quantities; it requires a delivery system that can coordinate complexity reliably.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
57. Q: What is the chapter's central lesson for owners commissioning a major project?
A: Show answer
Owners should treat the delivery team as a strategic asset and make deliberate choices about who is selected, how organizations are contracted, how risks and rewards are shared, and what working culture is created. They should seek proven domain experience, favor suppliers with demonstrated capability over price alone, establish a clear project identity and purpose, provide the conditions for productive work, involve frontline expertise, and make it safe to report problems. These actions should begin before execution pressure becomes acute. The T5 case suggests that the owner cannot outsource responsibility for integration simply by awarding contracts and monitoring milestones; the owner must lead the conditions that make coordinated delivery possible.
Study instruction: Write a one-page owner action plan for the first phase of a major project. Include team selection, commercial strategy, identity, purpose, working conditions, worker voice, and operational readiness.
Key point: The owner is responsible for creating the commercial and cultural conditions in which the delivery team can succeed.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — Penguin Random House
T5 Case Study Seminar Full Report — Major Projects Association
Rows: 57
Table 9
Modularity: Building Transformative Projects from Repeatable Small Units
1. Q: What does it mean to find a project’s “Lego”?
A: Show answer
The project’s “Lego” is its smallest useful, repeatable building block. It need not be a literal physical component: it may be a classroom, station, tunnel section, turbine, server, factory block, or standardized service package. The unit should be sufficiently self-contained to design, produce, test, improve, and combine with other units. Finding it requires looking beyond the conventional asset and asking what small thing can be repeated to create the required large outcome. Once identified, that unit becomes the basis for sequencing, procurement, quality control, learning, and capacity planning.
Study instruction: Choose a complex project and propose three candidate building blocks. Assess each for functional completeness, repeatability, interfaces, and ability to improve between iterations.
Key point: The key design question is: what small unit can be repeated and assembled into the required large result?
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
2. Q: Why does the chapter contrast “one huge thing” with “many small things”?
A: Show answer
The distinction concerns project architecture, not simply physical size. A one-huge-thing project is treated as a bespoke integrated whole whose unique parts must all work before the asset can deliver its intended benefit. A many-small-things project is decomposed into repeatable modules that can be produced, tested, improved, and brought into service in stages. Both approaches can create enormous outcomes. The modular approach, however, offers more opportunities for learning, parallel work, incremental capacity, and early benefits. The chapter argues that this usually improves speed, cost, and risk performance.
Study instruction: Draw both delivery architectures for the same infrastructure need. Compare unit boundaries, dependencies, commissioning, learning opportunities, and time to first benefit.
Key point: A huge result does not have to be delivered as one indivisible project.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
3. Q: What does the Monju nuclear project illustrate about bespoke megaprojects?
A: Show answer
Monju illustrates how a technically ambitious, highly bespoke project can accumulate delay, cost, operational problems, and political exposure before producing any benefit. The chapter recounts a long development, a fire after construction, a scandal over the handling of the accident, delayed restart attempts, extensive maintenance deficiencies, and eventual closure. The case is not presented simply as proof that nuclear power cannot work. It shows the consequences of delivering a complex one-off system that must be completed as an integrated whole, offers few safe opportunities for experimentation, and remains exposed to changing conditions over many years.
Study instruction: Build a timeline of Monju’s major milestones. For each delay or decision, identify its technical, governance, financial, or external-risk consequence.
Key point: Monju demonstrates how bespoke complexity and long delivery can compound before any intended benefit is realized.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
4. Q: Why does bespoke design make a very large project difficult to deliver?
A: Show answer
Bespoke design reduces standardization and repetition. Components may be unique to the site, technology, or configuration, so suppliers and workers cannot simply reproduce a proven solution. Custom interfaces create additional coordination and integration demands, while each new design decision introduces uncertainty about compatibility, performance, maintenance, and commissioning. A team may have to solve many interdependent problems without directly comparable experience. The chapter’s tailor analogy captures the risk: customization is difficult when the maker has little experience and must get the result right on the first attempt.
Study instruction: List the custom components and interfaces in a project. Classify each as essential customization, avoidable variation, or an unresolved design decision that could be standardized.
Key point: Unnecessary uniqueness multiplies design and integration work; standardize what does not need to be unique.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
5. Q: Why are experimentation and experience scarce in many one-off megaprojects?
A: Show answer
Some assets cannot be built in a small form, operated safely, and redesigned through repeated live trials. A nuclear plant, for example, cannot be constructed merely as an expensive experiment. Rare projects also provide few opportunities for teams to accumulate direct experience, because each takes years and may differ substantially by site and technology. The team is therefore asked to commit to a complex design without the usual benefits of experimentation or repetition. The practical response is to create smaller safe tests wherever possible, using prototypes, mock-ups, supplier trials, pilot units, and staged validation before full commitment.
Study instruction: Identify five critical assumptions in a project and design the smallest safe test that could validate each before full-scale commitment.
Key point: When full-scale trial and error is impossible, deliberately create smaller test environments before committing to the whole asset.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
6. Q: What is negative learning, and why is it dangerous?
A: Show answer
Negative learning occurs when progress reveals that a project is more difficult and costly than originally understood. Unknown obstacles emerge, assumed solutions fail, and the team discovers additional work without having a practical way to redesign or restart the whole system. Instead of making later work easier, new information increases the burden of completion. This differs from positive learning, in which repetition improves methods and makes later units faster, cheaper, and more reliable. Negative learning is especially damaging when discoveries arrive after design, contracts, and capital commitments have become difficult to reverse.
Study instruction: Review a project with late discoveries. Identify which could have been exposed earlier through prototypes, design reviews, supplier trials, or modular testing.
Key point: Learning is useful when it arrives early enough to change the next decision or iteration.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
7. Q: Why can an almost-complete integrated asset still produce no economic benefit?
A: Show answer
Some assets deliver their intended service only when the entire system is complete, tested, approved, and commissioned. A power plant that is largely built but cannot safely generate electricity may have consumed most of its capital without producing the output that justifies the investment. The chapter emphasizes that money is committed throughout a long construction period while benefits are postponed until the whole facility operates. Delays therefore extend both the construction bill and the period during which capital is tied up. Modular projects can reduce this exposure when completed units can enter service independently.
Study instruction: Map the point at which each major component begins delivering usable benefits. Separate benefits that require total completion from those that can be realized in stages.
Key point: Independent, usable completions shorten the gap between expenditure and benefit.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
8. Q: How does a long delivery period increase exposure to black swan events?
A: Show answer
All projects face unpredictable shocks, but exposure grows with time. A project that takes decades passes through more political, economic, technological, social, and environmental conditions than one delivered quickly. The chapter uses Monju to show how the Fukushima disaster changed the political context for a nuclear project that had been under development for many years. The specific shock was not forecast when Monju began, but the broader risk of a major external change was inherent in its long horizon. Faster delivery cannot eliminate uncertainty, but it reduces the time during which the project remains exposed to it.
Study instruction: Create a timeline of external risks for a long project. Distinguish foreseeable risks from shocks and explain how staged delivery could limit exposure.
Key point: Long duration creates more opportunities for external events to change a project’s viability.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
9. Q: How did the Nepal school programme turn a national outcome into many small deliveries?
A: Show answer
The programme treated the classroom as the basic repeatable unit. One classroom could constitute a village school; several classrooms could form a larger school; schools could be grouped into districts; and districts together could expand the national school system. The team limited variation to three main designs, with site slope as a principal adjustment because Nepal is mountainous. Completed schools could open and serve students while subsequent schools were still being built. The chapter reports that the programme delivered about twenty thousand schools and classrooms on budget and years ahead of schedule, with independent evaluations finding that they worked well.
Study instruction: Recreate the hierarchy from classroom to national system. Identify which decisions were standardized centrally and which were adapted to local site conditions.
Key point: A national programme can be delivered as a sequence of small, useful units rather than one enormous construction event.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
10. Q: Why was simplicity important to the Nepal school designs?
A: Show answer
The team sought schools that were functional, high quality, and earthquake resistant, but as simple as possible within those requirements. Limiting the programme to three main designs reduced unnecessary variation and made repeated construction more manageable. Simplicity was not an excuse to compromise essential performance; it protected required functions while removing complexity that did not add sufficient value. This is a central discipline of modular design: define the outcomes that cannot be compromised, then avoid features and variants that make each unit harder to reproduce, inspect, procure, or maintain.
Study instruction: Write a performance specification for a repeatable unit. Separate mandatory safety and functional requirements from optional features, then remove unjustified variations.
Key point: Simplify around essential performance, not by removing requirements that make an asset safe or useful.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
11. Q: How does staged completion allow modular programmes to deliver benefits earlier?
A: Show answer
In the Nepal programme, a completed one- or two-classroom school could begin operating without waiting for every school in the district or country to be finished. Each completed unit could deliver service while later units continued through the pipeline. Early operation also generated feedback: teachers, students, and evaluators could observe what worked, and the next batch could incorporate improvements. Staged delivery therefore combines earlier benefits with ongoing learning, provided that each unit is safe, functional, and connected to the wider programme through a planned design.
Study instruction: Identify the earliest point at which each deliverable could be safely commissioned. Build a staged-benefit schedule and specify acceptance evidence for every unit.
Key point: Independent completions turn a long programme into a stream of benefits rather than a single end-loaded payoff.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
12. Q: How does repetition create positive learning in modular delivery?
A: Show answer
Repetition lets the team compare one iteration with the next. Problems can be identified, methods refined, and design changes incorporated into later units. Workers, suppliers, and managers become more familiar with the task, reducing uncertainty and improving coordination. Repetition also makes performance measurable because successive units can be compared on cost, time, defects, and output. It is not simply doing the same thing mechanically; it is using each iteration to improve the system that produces the next one. If lessons are not captured and applied, repetition may merely reproduce the same inefficiency.
Study instruction: Define five measures for each repeated unit, such as cycle time, cost, rework, safety incidents, and acceptance defects. State how the next iteration will respond to the results.
Key point: Repetition creates value when each cycle produces evidence that improves the next cycle.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
13. Q: Does modularity require every component to be identical?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter describes modularity as a matter of degree. The Empire State Building was not as modular as a Lego model, but its floors were made as similar as practical, allowing workers to repeat tasks and learn. The Pentagon used identical sides to gain similar benefits. Even when complete duplication is impossible, standardizing part of a design or process can create meaningful repetition. A project leader should therefore look for opportunities to reduce variation rather than treat modularity as an all-or-nothing choice. Common interfaces, repeated work packages, equipment, and quality procedures can all support learning.
Study instruction: Choose a project with unavoidable site variation. Identify three elements that can still be standardized and explain how flexibility will be retained where needed.
Key point: Partial standardization can produce learning and efficiency even when the whole project cannot be modularized.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
14. Q: How should a project leader decide what to standardize and what to customize?
A: Show answer
Standardize elements that perform the same function across locations or iterations, especially where repetition improves cost, quality, speed, and predictability. Customize only where a real difference in site conditions, user needs, safety, regulation, or performance justifies it. Nepal’s schools used common designs while adapting to site slope. Too little adaptation can make a module unsuitable; too much customization can erase the benefits of repetition. The reason for each variation should be explicit, and its effect on interfaces, procurement, maintenance, and future learning should be assessed.
Study instruction: Prepare a standardization matrix. For every proposed variation, state the requirement that justifies it and its effect on cost, schedule, quality, and repeatability.
Key point: Preserve necessary adaptation while preventing variation that adds complexity without improving performance.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
15. Q: How does factory production change the role of a construction site?
A: Show answer
Factory production moves repeatable fabrication into a controlled environment with more predictable workflows, specialist equipment, and less exposure to weather. The site then becomes primarily a place for foundations, access, utilities, lifting, connections, testing, and final integration. This does not eliminate site work; it changes its composition. The chapter calls attention to the transition from construction site to assembly site as a central benefit of designing for manufacture and assembly. The approach is most effective when factory and site activities, transport, tolerances, and commissioning have been coordinated from the beginning.
Study instruction: Map a project’s site activities. Mark which could move to a factory and what transport, lifting, storage, interface, and assembly controls would be needed.
Key point: Move repeatable work into controlled production where practical, leaving the site focused on assembly and integration.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
16. Q: What is design for manufacture and assembly, and why must it begin early?
A: Show answer
Design for manufacture and assembly means designing an asset so that components can be produced efficiently in a factory and joined or installed efficiently at the destination. It requires attention to manufacturing methods, transport dimensions, lifting, connections, tolerances, sequencing, and maintainability from the outset. It is not simply taking a conventional design and deciding later to prefabricate it. The chapter links this approach to Heathrow Terminal 5 and factory-made classrooms. Its value comes from reducing site work, increasing repeatability, improving quality control, and limiting exposure to weather and other site constraints.
Study instruction: Review a preliminary design and identify decisions that would obstruct factory production or on-site assembly. Propose changes before design freeze.
Key point: Manufacturing and assembly constraints must shape the design early; they cannot be added efficiently as an afterthought.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
17. Q: What determines whether a complete module or smaller submodules should be transported?
A: Show answer
The choice depends on module dimensions and weight, road and bridge capacity, turning radii, route access, lifting equipment, delivery timing, and assembly space. A complete classroom may be transported on a flatbed truck where roads permit. In remote Nepalese mountain villages, lack of roads made that approach unsuitable, so local construction was more practical. If a complete module is too large to move, it can be divided into smaller components for transport and assembly. Module size is therefore a joint design, manufacturing, and logistics decision.
Study instruction: Prepare a route and handling assessment covering maximum dimensions, weight, access restrictions, lifting capacity, storage, and site connections.
Key point: The best module is not merely easy to manufacture; it must be deliverable and assemblable at its actual location.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
18. Q: Why does the chapter reject the assumption that modular buildings must be cheap and unattractive?
A: Show answer
The chapter acknowledges that some earlier modular buildings were poorly designed, but argues that this is not inherent to modularity. It points to Sears Modern Homes, whose factory-built kits were sold widely and whose surviving examples are valued for construction quality and design. It also describes modular cars, an elegant proposed Manhattan hotel, and Apple’s headquarters, where repeated pods and manufacturing-style construction supported distinctive architecture. The distinction is between low-quality design and modular delivery. A repeatable manufacturing process can produce high standards when design, materials, quality controls, and architectural goals are treated seriously.
Study instruction: Find a product or building where modular construction and high design quality coexist. Identify the design choices and controls that prevent standardization from becoming monotony.
Key point: Modularity is a production strategy, not a requirement for low aesthetic or functional quality.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
19. Q: How does a server farm illustrate scale-free scalability?
A: Show answer
A server farm is built from nested repeatable units: servers form racks, racks form rows, rows form rooms, rooms form buildings, and buildings can form a larger campus. Capacity can be increased by adding more of the same units rather than redesigning the entire system. The scale changes while the organizing principle remains recognizable. The example shows that modularity applies to digital infrastructure as well as buildings and machinery. Expansion still depends on compatible equipment, stable interfaces, power, cooling, network connections, and operating capacity.
Study instruction: Draw a hierarchy for a scalable service or infrastructure system. Identify the smallest repeatable unit, rules for combining units, and constraints that eventually limit expansion.
Key point: A scale-free architecture grows by adding units while retaining the same basic organizing logic.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
20. Q: What does “scale-free” mean, and how is it related to fractal patterns?
A: Show answer
Scale-free means that the same basic pattern can be recognized at different sizes. The chapter connects this to the term “fractal,” associated with Benoit Mandelbrot: when a pattern is examined at a smaller scale, similar structures recur within it. In project delivery, the practical point is that rules for building a unit, combining units, and expanding the system can remain consistent across scales. This does not promise literally unlimited growth. Physical, economic, regulatory, and operational constraints still apply, but the organizing principle remains repeatable as the system expands.
Study instruction: Choose a system with nested levels. Test whether the same design and coordination rules work at component, subsystem, facility, and network levels.
Key point: Scale-free design preserves repeatable logic across levels, while real-world capacity still has constraints.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
21. Q: How did modular construction support emergency facilities during the Covid-19 outbreak?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a modular-housing manufacturer adapting an existing room design and producing units for a hospital in Wuhan that opened nine days later with one thousand beds. Hong Kong similarly assembled quarantine units and expanded the facility as requirements changed. Because units could be disconnected, relocated, or stored, the investment was not necessarily locked into one site. These cases show how an existing design, manufacturing capacity, prepared site, and repeatable assembly can shorten response time. They do not mean every emergency facility can be delivered in nine days; utilities, staffing, approvals, logistics, and commissioning remain essential.
Study instruction: Design an emergency-facility concept. Specify which module designs and supplier capacities must exist before the emergency and list site, utility, staffing, and commissioning dependencies.
Key point: Rapid modular response depends on preparation before the emergency, not just fast assembly after it begins.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
22. Q: How can modularity be applied to a project that appears indivisible, such as a hydroelectric dam?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the assumption that a giant dam is the only way to generate hydroelectricity. Small-scale hydro can divert part of a river’s flow through small turbines and return the water to the river. Each installation produces less power than a major dam, but repeated installations can create substantial capacity. The author argues that this may reduce environmental damage, public opposition, cost, and risk. This does not mean every small hydro site is benign; ecological flow, fish passage, sediment, cumulative watershed effects, and local rights still require assessment. The lesson is to question the assumed unit of delivery and compare alternative system architectures.
Study instruction: For a large infrastructure concept, identify the service required rather than assuming the conventional asset is mandatory. Compare modular alternatives on output, impacts, cost, and risk.
Key point: Reframe the required service before accepting the conventional mega-asset as the only solution.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
23. Q: What did Tesla’s Gigafactory example demonstrate about staged industrial construction?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a plan to treat a huge factory as a series of smaller factory blocks. Tesla could build one block, get it working, then add and integrate further blocks. Battery production and revenue could begin while the overall facility was still being completed. This reduces the need to wait for the entire footprint before any productive output is possible. It also creates repeated opportunities to learn about construction, equipment installation, commissioning, and operations. The approach depends on whether production blocks can operate safely and economically with shared utilities and interfaces.
Study instruction: Sketch a phased factory layout. Identify the smallest production block that can operate and the shared utilities or interfaces required for each expansion.
Key point: A large industrial project can produce value before final capacity is complete when its production blocks are genuinely separable.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
24. Q: How does Planet’s CubeSat approach differ from a bespoke satellite programme?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Planet using inexpensive commercial electronics arranged in small standardized modules. These are assembled into CubeSat units, with three modules forming the electronics for a Dove satellite. The resulting satellites were described as small, quick to build, and inexpensive compared with traditional large bespoke satellites. Hundreds could be deployed as a fleet to observe conditions on Earth. The modular model makes replacement and iteration more practical, so a single failure need not threaten the programme. The chapter also notes privacy concerns, showing that technical scalability does not remove governance responsibilities.
Study instruction: Compare a bespoke satellite with a standardized fleet on build time, unit cost, replacement, coverage, reliability, and privacy implications.
Key point: Small standardized satellites can make space capability scalable and replaceable, but fleet benefits must be balanced with governance risks.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
25. Q: What two forms of modularity helped Madrid Metro expand rapidly?
A: Show answer
Madrid Metro standardized the design of seventy-six stations, using a simple common design rather than treating each station as unique. It also treated tunnel lengths as repeatable production units. The team estimated how much tunnel a boring machine and crew could deliver in a given period, divided the total requirement into units, and deployed enough machines and crews to meet the schedule. Several machines worked simultaneously. The chapter reports that 131 kilometres of rail and seventy-six stations were delivered in two stages of four years each, at about twice the industry’s average speed and half the cost. The lesson is to modularize both facilities and the work process that produces them.
Study instruction: For a linear infrastructure project, define repeatable facilities and work packages. Estimate output per crew or machine and test how parallel deployment changes the critical path.
Key point: Modularity can apply to the asset being delivered and to the production process used to deliver it.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
26. Q: Why did Madrid Metro favor proven technologies during its expansion?
A: Show answer
The chapter says Madrid Metro relied on proven technologies with a high degree of “frozen experience.” This allowed the project to build on established knowledge rather than add uncertainty through untested technology while delivering a large expansion. Standardized stations and repeatable tunnel packages already created opportunities for efficiency and learning. New technology could have added integration, training, testing, maintenance, and supplier risks. This is not a universal argument against innovation. It is a sequencing lesson: use mature solutions where reliability and speed matter, and test new technology in a controlled setting before making it a critical dependency of a large programme.
Study instruction: Classify proposed technologies as proven, adapted, or first-of-a-kind. Decide whether to deploy, pilot separately, or defer each one.
Key point: Innovation should be introduced where its benefits justify the risk; repeatable delivery often benefits from mature components.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
27. Q: How did the shipping container transform freight transport through modularity?
A: Show answer
Malcolm McLean’s idea was to put cargo into identical steel boxes that could be stacked on ships and transferred to trains and trucks. Standard containers converted diverse cargo-handling tasks into a repeatable unit that could move through several transport modes. Loading and unloading became faster, safer, and easier to mechanize. Ships, cranes, ports, rail systems, and trucks could be designed around a common interface. The resulting cost and speed improvements changed production and distribution patterns worldwide. The wider lesson is that a modest standard can transform an entire system when many participants adopt the same unit and rules.
Study instruction: Identify a fragmented process involving individually handled items. Propose a standard container or interface and analyze its effect on handling, storage, transport, and coordination.
Key point: A common module and interface can create system-wide benefits when adopted across an interconnected network.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
28. Q: Why does the chapter associate modularity with lower fat-tail project risk?
A: Show answer
Fat-tailed risk is the possibility of extreme outcomes far beyond ordinary overruns. The chapter compares project types and observes that several types with relatively thin cost-risk tails—solar, wind, fossil thermal power, electricity transmission, and roads—are modular to a considerable degree. Many highly fat-tailed types are bespoke, integrated projects. Modular delivery can limit the scale of a failure, create more opportunities to learn, allow sections to proceed in parallel, and reduce dependence on a single final completion event. This is an empirical pattern in the author’s database, not a guarantee that every modular project will be low risk.
Study instruction: List ordinary and extreme failure scenarios for a project. Explain how modular boundaries, independent commissioning, and repeatable work would limit or fail to limit each.
Key point: Modularity can reduce exposure to catastrophic outcomes by making failure more containable and learning more frequent.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
29. Q: Why are solar and wind power described as highly modular?
A: Show answer
Solar cells combine into panels, panels into arrays, and arrays into farms; wind turbines combine factory-built foundations, towers, nacelles, and blades into strings and wind farms. Capacity can therefore be added through repeated units rather than one indivisible machine. The chapter associates this structure with lower exposure to severe cost and schedule overruns in the author’s project database. Modularity does not remove grid connection, land, permitting, storage, supply-chain, or site constraints. It changes how generating capacity itself can be built, expanded, and improved. The distinction helps explain why repeated deployment can support learning and falling costs.
Study instruction: Map a solar or wind project from component to full facility. Identify standardized stages, site-specific engineering, grid dependencies, and limits to incremental expansion.
Key point: Renewable generation can scale through repeatable units, though the wider energy system still needs integration and enabling infrastructure.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
30. Q: How can roads and electricity transmission benefit from modular delivery?
A: Show answer
A freeway can be divided into repeated sections, and lessons from one section can inform the next. Several sections may be built simultaneously when design, access, resources, and interfaces are coordinated. Transmission systems similarly use repeated towers and wires or manufactured cable sections. These assets may function as continuous networks, but they need not be delivered as one indivisible construction task. Junctions, substations, drainage, bridges, access, land, and network connections still create important interfaces. The chapter’s point is that a continuous final system can be produced through repeatable construction units.
Study instruction: Break a road or transmission programme into repeatable sections. Identify which can proceed in parallel and which interfaces or approvals must be resolved first.
Key point: A continuous network can still be delivered through separate, repeatable, and partly parallel work packages.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
31. Q: What is the relationship between modularity and parallel construction?
A: Show answer
Modularity can make parallel work practical because the project can define repeatable units, allocate teams or machines to them, and integrate the results through known interfaces. Madrid Metro estimated the output of a boring machine and crew, then calculated how many teams were required and operated several machines at once. Parallelism can shorten the schedule, but simply adding crews is not enough. Work fronts must be accessible, designs stable, resources available, and integration capacity adequate. Otherwise, parallel work creates congestion, coordination failures, or rework.
Study instruction: Create a resource-loaded plan. Identify the maximum number of units that can be built simultaneously without exceeding site, supplier, supervision, or integration capacity.
Key point: Parallel delivery works when modules and interfaces are defined well enough for teams to progress independently.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
32. Q: How can modularity limit the amount of capital exposed to one project failure?
A: Show answer
If modules are independent or semi-independent, a problem in one unit need not prevent every other unit from being completed or used. The organization can learn from a failed batch, change later units, or defer one module while others proceed. This can limit the capital committed before the approach demonstrates that it works. The protection depends on genuine separability: shared systems, common suppliers, common design flaws, or a single regulatory approval can still create correlated failures across many modules. Modular boundaries must therefore be tested against the project’s actual dependencies.
Study instruction: Identify the largest single failure that could affect a project. Map which modules would remain usable and which shared dependencies could spread the failure.
Key point: Modularization limits risk only when units have meaningful independence and common-mode risks are controlled.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
33. Q: How can a modular programme avoid multiplying the same defect across every unit?
A: Show answer
Repetition creates learning only if feedback is captured before a defect is repeated at scale. A flawed standard design can spread the same problem across every module, making standardization a multiplier of error. The team should build an early unit, inspect and test it, collect operational feedback, correct the design or method, and then release the improved version for wider production. Configuration control matters so that teams know which version is being built and which changes are approved. The first module is a learning opportunity, not proof that the design is already perfect.
Study instruction: Define first-article approval criteria, inspections, feedback, defect thresholds, change authority, and conditions for releasing the design to full production.
Key point: Standardization magnifies good and bad design alike; validate and improve the module before repeating it widely.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
34. Q: What is the difference between modularity and simply dividing a project into smaller contracts?
A: Show answer
Smaller contracts do not automatically create modularity. True modularity requires repeatable units with defined functions, interfaces, production methods, and a practical way to assemble or operate them. Contract packaging may fragment responsibility while leaving the underlying design bespoke and tightly interdependent. That can add interface disputes and coordination costs without creating repetition or learning. The chapter’s examples work because the units themselves are repeatable and their relationships can be understood at several scales. Procurement boundaries should therefore support the technical and operational architecture rather than create artificial fragmentation.
Study instruction: Compare contract packages with the project’s technical architecture. Identify packages that divide responsibility without creating repeatable units, then realign boundaries around coherent modules.
Key point: Small contracts are not the same as modular delivery; the technical and operational units must also be repeatable and integrable.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
35. Q: How should a project team choose the right module size?
A: Show answer
A module should be large enough to perform a meaningful function or be produced efficiently, but small enough to repeat, transport, assemble, replace, or commission without recreating the complexity of the whole project. The chapter’s examples range from classrooms and turbines to tunnel lengths, servers, and satellite components. The right scale depends on technology, logistics, site access, safety, demand, and the independence of the unit’s output. A unit that is too small may create excessive interfaces; one that is too large may preserve the indivisibility and risk of the original megaproject.
Study instruction: Propose three module sizes and score them for functional completeness, repetition rate, transportability, interface count, independent commissioning, and learning potential.
Key point: The best module size balances meaningful function with repeatability and manageable interfaces.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
36. Q: What conditions are needed for modules to combine into a reliable larger system?
A: Show answer
Modules need compatible physical, technical, data, operational, and organizational interfaces. Dimensions, connections, tolerances, performance levels, and safety requirements must be defined so units produced by different teams or at different times can work together. The larger system also needs integration tests and operating procedures to verify collective behavior. Server farms, wind farms, satellite fleets, and school hierarchies all depend on a stable logic for combining smaller units. Modularity does not eliminate systems engineering; it makes interface discipline more important as the number of units and combinations grows.
Study instruction: Prepare an interface-control list. For each interface, name the owner, acceptance criteria, required data, test method, and change-approval process.
Key point: Repeatable modules scale reliably only when interfaces and system-level behavior are controlled.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
37. Q: How should a modular alternative be compared with a conventional megaproject?
A: Show answer
Begin with the service or outcome required, not with the assumption that the conventional asset is the only solution. Compare lifecycle cost, time to first benefit, scalability, reliability, environmental and social effects, safety, maintainability, and exposure to extreme overruns. Assess whether the modular option has credible technology, supply-chain capacity, sites, interfaces, and operational arrangements. Also test whether flexibility creates hidden costs through duplication, logistics, or integration. The chapter encourages leaders to question conventional project forms, but it does not imply that modularity is appropriate in every context.
Study instruction: Prepare a decision matrix for modular and conventional options. Weight criteria transparently and record the evidence and uncertainty behind each score.
Key point: Choose the architecture that best delivers the required service over its lifecycle; neither modularity nor conventional scale is automatically superior.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
38. Q: What does the China comparison reveal about the limitations of the one-huge-thing model?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents China as a natural experiment because national authorities could remove many planning and opposition barriers. China sought rapid expansion of wind, solar, and nuclear generation. The comparison of new installed capacity shows wind and solar rising much faster than nuclear, despite favorable conditions for nuclear development. The author uses this to challenge the claim that nuclear’s weak scaling is mainly caused by hostile regulation or local opposition. The comparison is not controlled in every respect, and capacity additions do not measure all system costs or reliability. Its intended lesson is that architecture and repeatability matter even when institutional barriers are comparatively low.
Study instruction: Summarize the chapter’s comparison and identify confounding factors such as technology maturity, unit size, construction duration, financing, and grid integration.
Key point: Removing external obstacles does not automatically solve the structural delivery problems of a bespoke, indivisible project.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
39. Q: How should small modular reactors be interpreted in the chapter’s argument?
A: Show answer
Small modular reactors are presented as an attempt to redesign nuclear power around factory-built, smaller units that can be transported and assembled on-site. Each unit would produce less electricity than a conventional reactor, but additional units could be added as demand grows. This shifts the proposed delivery model from one enormous bespoke facility to a repeatable series of modules. The chapter explicitly cautions that, at the time of writing, the technology was unproven and the author would not predict whether it would work as hoped or how quickly. The point is recognition of modularity’s potential, not proof that technical, regulatory, economic, and safety questions are resolved.
Study instruction: Separate proposed benefits from evidence needed to validate them. List the technical, manufacturing, licensing, supply-chain, cost, waste, and operating questions a pilot must answer.
Key point: The modular concept is a delivery hypothesis requiring evidence; do not confuse design intent with proven performance.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
40. Q: Why does the chapter connect climate goals to project-delivery capability?
A: Show answer
Climate mitigation and adaptation require physical assets and industrial processes to be built, upgraded, connected, and operated at enormous scale. The chapter discusses renewable generation, electrification, carbon capture, hydrogen production, and supporting networks. Each requires project teams, suppliers, finance, approvals, skilled workers, and reliable delivery systems. A target can be credible in a scenario but still fail if the projects needed to implement it are delayed, over budget, or never completed. The International Energy Agency’s 2021 roadmap likewise sets out technology milestones and investment and deployment requirements for a net-zero energy pathway.
Study instruction: Choose a climate commitment and translate it into required assets, annual deployment quantities, enabling infrastructure, and accountable delivery organizations.
Key point: Climate targets are delivery programmes made of many interdependent physical projects.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
International Energy Agency: Net Zero by 2050
41. Q: What does the IPCC evidence cited in the chapter imply about changing extreme-weather risk?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the IPCC analysis of extreme heat to show how warming changes the frequency of events that were historically rare. Figure SPM.6 reports that an extreme heat event that occurred once in fifty years in the 1850–1900 reference climate is projected to occur 4.8 times as often at 1°C of warming, 8.6 times at 1.5°C, 13.9 times at 2°C, and 39.2 times at 4°C. These are modelled frequency changes relative to a historical baseline, not forecasts of exact event dates. The project implication is to design for changing hazards rather than rely uncritically on historical frequencies.
Study instruction: Read IPCC Figure SPM.6 and explain its baseline, warming levels, and frequency multipliers. Identify assets that need stress testing against more frequent extremes.
Key point: Climate risk changes the design basis for long-lived assets; historical rarity is not a reliable guide to future exposure.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
IPCC AR6: Figure SPM.6
42. Q: How should the chapter’s climate statistics be used in project planning?
A: Show answer
The figures communicate the scale and urgency of changing hazards; they do not replace project-specific climate analysis. A project team should use authoritative projections and relevant geographic and sector data to understand local risks. It should then test design thresholds, operating limits, emergency plans, maintenance assumptions, and service continuity. Because the chapter draws on an earlier assessment and scenario framing, decision-makers should consult the underlying IPCC material and current national or regional assessments before selecting engineering values. The essential step is translating climate evidence into explicit requirements and contingency plans.
Study instruction: For one planned asset, identify relevant heat, precipitation, flood, drought, wind, or fire hazards. Record projection sources and translate them into design or operating criteria.
Key point: Use the chapter to recognize climate risk, then use current authoritative local evidence for engineering decisions.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
IPCC AR6: Figure SPM.6
43. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize scale and speed for climate-related projects?
A: Show answer
The volume of infrastructure required for climate mitigation and adaptation is unprecedented, while the time available is constrained by climate goals and rising physical risks. Slow delivery can undermine a project’s benefit even if the final asset performs well. Cost overruns also consume capital that could fund additional projects. The response cannot simply be more traditional megaprojects. It requires delivery models combining repeatable units, industrialized production, parallel deployment, learning, and reliable integration. The IEA roadmap illustrates the scale through large annual additions of clean-energy capacity and many technology and policy milestones.
Study instruction: Take a climate infrastructure target and calculate the annual and monthly delivery rate required. Identify bottlenecks that prevent the current project model from reaching it.
Key point: Climate urgency requires repeatable delivery at high volume, not just ambitious individual projects.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
International Energy Agency: Net Zero by 2050
44. Q: What role do carbon capture and hydrogen projects play in the chapter’s climate argument?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies carbon capture, utilization, and storage and industrial-scale electrolyzers as technologies that must move from concepts or prototypes toward deployment if they are to contribute at the required scale. Carbon capture removes carbon dioxide from industrial processes or the atmosphere for storage or use. Electrolyzers use electricity to produce hydrogen for industrial and other applications. The chapter emphasizes repeated monthly build rates in the IEA pathway, illustrating that demonstration alone is not enough. Manufacturing capacity, transport and storage infrastructure, standards, finance, and operations must scale together.
Study instruction: Map one technology from prototype to demonstration, first commercial plant, repeatable design, supply-chain expansion, and portfolio deployment.
Key point: Emerging climate technologies need both innovation and a delivery system capable of reproducing them at industrial scale.
