International Energy Agency: Net Zero by 2050
45. Q: What was distinctive about Denmark’s early development of wind power?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Denmark’s response to energy insecurity after the 1973 oil embargo. Alongside conventional energy choices, pioneers experimented with wind turbines, including the multimegawatt turbine at Tvind in Jutland in 1978. Early onshore wind remained a modest industry, but experimentation created practical experience. Government policy later helped create opportunities for offshore wind projects, including projects that did not all succeed. Those early installations gave companies experience that became valuable as the industry expanded. The lesson is that a new industry can begin with small experiments and imperfect projects, then use the knowledge gained to support a larger commercial system.
Study instruction: Construct a timeline of Denmark’s wind sector from early experiments through offshore deployment. Mark the roles of private experimentation, policy, and accumulated operating experience.
Key point: A large industrial transformation can begin with small experiments that create experience for later scale.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
46. Q: How did government policy help create the conditions for Denmark’s offshore wind industry?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes government action as a catalyst rather than a substitute for enterprise. Denmark’s environment minister required companies seeking permission for coal-powered generators to also build early offshore wind farms. The projects were difficult and uneven, but they created experience and helped establish a market. The chapter argues that government-created frameworks can push the first boulder that starts a larger transformation. Public policy can create demand, set conditions, reduce early market uncertainty, coordinate infrastructure, and encourage investment. It does not guarantee that every project will succeed; it creates conditions in which firms can experiment, learn, and build capabilities.
Study instruction: Identify policy instruments that could create an early market for a new infrastructure technology. Assess incentives, risks transferred to government, performance conditions, and review mechanisms.
Key point: Government can help create the first market and learning opportunities that private firms may not initiate alone.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
47. Q: Why was DONG Energy’s inherited offshore wind experience strategically important?
A: Show answer
When Danish energy companies merged into DONG Energy in 2006, the new company inherited offshore wind farms that were small relative to its fossil-fuel business. Those assets nevertheless gave it operating experience and knowledge of a developing technology. The chapter quotes the company’s first CEO describing how this made it one of the most experienced offshore wind operators. Small or peripheral assets can become strategically valuable when a technology is poised for growth. Experience accumulated through earlier projects can create a capability advantage that later entrants cannot immediately reproduce.
Study instruction: Analyze an organization’s small projects or capabilities that could become strategically important in a future market. Identify the experience and knowledge that should be preserved.
Key point: Early operating experience can become a strategic asset when a market expands.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
48. Q: How did Ørsted’s 85/15 plan demonstrate the value of modularity?
A: Show answer
In 2009, DONG Energy’s CEO announced a plan to reverse the company’s energy mix from roughly 85 percent fossil fuels and 15 percent renewables to the opposite balance within a generation. Offshore wind farms were built from repeatable turbines, strings, and grid connections. As more projects were delivered, the company and partners standardized construction and improved the industry value chain. The chapter reports that the transition was achieved in about ten years, earlier than the original horizon, while costs fell faster than expected. The case shows how modularity, experience, and industry-wide coordination can accelerate a strategic transformation.
Study instruction: Identify the repeatable units and value-chain capabilities behind the 85/15 transformation. Separate modularity from policy support, investment, market conditions, and technology improvement.
Key point: A strategic transformation can accelerate when the new business is built from repeatable units that improve through successive projects.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
49. Q: What is the relationship between industrial standardization and cost reduction in offshore wind?
A: Show answer
The chapter attributes rapid cost reduction to improvements across the business and to industrializing and standardizing how offshore wind farms were built. Repeated projects let designers, manufacturers, installers, and operators improve components and processes. Suppliers can invest in specialized capacity when demand becomes more predictable, while project teams gain experience coordinating installation and commissioning. The chapter reports a historical cost reduction of about 60 percent over four years in the period discussed; this is not a guaranteed forecast for another technology or market. The transferable lesson is to align repeated projects and the supply chain so learning is shared rather than lost between contracts.
Study instruction: Map a technology’s value chain and identify where repeated orders, common standards, supplier learning, and shared infrastructure could lower cost. Distinguish evidence-based learning from speculative savings.
Key point: Cost reductions can compound when repeated projects create stable demand and coordinated learning throughout the supply chain.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
50. Q: What are clustering and economies of agglomeration, and why do they matter in wind energy?
A: Show answer
Clustering occurs when related firms and specialist suppliers locate near one another, allowing knowledge, workers, services, and business relationships to circulate more easily. The chapter describes a concentration of wind-energy companies in Jutland, where early experimentation helped create specialized capabilities. This resembles the clusters associated with Hollywood film production and Silicon Valley technology. A cluster can reduce search and coordination costs, improve access to experienced suppliers, and support specialized labor and innovation. It does not emerge automatically from declaring an industrial strategy; it develops through accumulated capabilities, repeated business, institutions, and relationships.
Study instruction: Map suppliers, research organizations, skilled labor, infrastructure, and customers in an emerging industry. Identify geographic or institutional connections that could support a cluster.
Key point: Repeated projects can create an ecosystem of specialized capability beyond the boundaries of individual firms.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
51. Q: What can other countries learn from Denmark without copying its exact model?
A: Show answer
The transferable lesson is not to reproduce Denmark’s institutions or history exactly. Countries differ in geography, resources, electricity networks, public institutions, industrial capabilities, and community preferences. The reusable principles are to create enabling policy conditions, support early experimentation, build practical experience, standardize repeatable units, coordinate suppliers, and scale what works. A country should adapt these principles to its own constraints and compare options using evidence. Copying a technology target without the delivery institutions, skilled workforce, grid infrastructure, finance, and local legitimacy needed to implement it can produce disappointing results.
Study instruction: Compare a country’s starting conditions with Denmark’s. Identify transferable mechanisms, necessary adaptations, and elements that are not applicable.
Key point: Transfer the mechanisms of learning and modular scale, not a context-specific policy recipe.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
52. Q: How can individuals apply modularity to projects smaller than megaprojects?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes that modularity is empowering for individuals as well as governments and corporations. A person can break a difficult goal into small experiments, learn from each one, and repeat what works. This might mean testing a product before building a full business, piloting a service with a limited group, or completing one manageable section of a renovation before committing to the rest. Small experiments reduce the cost of being wrong and make it easier to adjust direction. The essential habits are imagination, tenacity, and turning early results into better subsequent iterations rather than defending the first idea.
Study instruction: Choose a personal or professional goal. Define a short experiment with a clear output, small budget, success measures, and a rule for whether to repeat, revise, or stop.
Key point: Large ambitions become manageable when progress is organized as small tests that generate evidence for the next step.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
53. Q: What are the main failure modes when organizations adopt modular delivery?
A: Show answer
A modular programme can fail if the unit is not functionally meaningful, interfaces are unstable, prototypes are not tested, or defects are repeated before lessons are incorporated. Excessive variation can destroy repetition, while over-standardization can ignore real local requirements. Logistics may make modules impossible to transport, and weak integration can undermine separately produced units. Dividing contracts without changing a bespoke design creates fragmentation rather than true modularity. The organization may also lack supply-chain capacity, skilled workers, quality systems, or governance needed to repeat units reliably. Modularity is an operating model, not a label applied after the fact.
Study instruction: Conduct a modularity pre-mortem. List failure modes, assign owners, and define a test or control before full-scale replication begins.
Key point: Modular delivery requires coherent units, stable interfaces, learning controls, logistics, and integration capability.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
54. Q: How should a project team measure whether modularity is improving delivery?
A: Show answer
Track performance at module and system levels. Module measures include unit cost, cycle time, defects, rework, safety, and output per crew or production line. System measures include time to first service, integration failures, capacity added per period, availability, lifecycle cost, and user benefits. Compare successive units to see whether learning improves performance. Record variation between sites and batches so an unusually easy first location is not mistaken for a repeatable result. Cost reduction alone is insufficient if quality, safety, maintainability, or service outcomes deteriorate.
Study instruction: Design a scorecard with at least six measures. State the data source, frequency, owner, baseline, target, and action triggered by an adverse trend.
Key point: Measure both repeated-unit performance and the reliability and usefulness of the assembled system.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
55. Q: How can a modular programme preserve safety and quality while accelerating delivery?
A: Show answer
Speed should come from repetition, controlled production, learning, and parallel work, not from skipping safety or quality requirements. A programme should define mandatory standards, validate the first unit, control design changes, inspect factory production, verify transport and lifting plans, and test interfaces during assembly. Each module needs acceptance criteria before entering service. The assembled system also needs commissioning checks because individually acceptable units may interact poorly. The chapter’s earthquake-resistant schools and critical infrastructure examples imply that simplicity and repeatability must remain within strict functional and safety boundaries.
Study instruction: Create a quality plan for one module from design through factory production, transport, assembly, commissioning, and operation. Include hold points and independent verification.
Key point: The objective is to industrialize reliable quality, not trade safety or performance for speed.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
56. Q: What governance arrangements help a modular programme learn across projects?
A: Show answer
Learning is lost if each team treats its work as unique and fails to transfer improvements. A programme needs shared design standards, controlled change approval, common performance data, supplier feedback, and a mechanism for updating specifications. Someone must decide when a change is proven and should become the new standard. Local teams also need a clear route to request justified adaptations. Standardized stations, classrooms, and offshore wind projects show that modularity works across repeated deployments when organizations preserve and reuse knowledge rather than restarting from scratch.
Study instruction: Design a programme-level learning system. Specify who captures lessons, validates them, updates standards, informs suppliers, and approves local exceptions.
Key point: Programme governance should turn local experience into shared improvements without suppressing justified adaptation.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
57. Q: What is the chapter’s central message for governments, corporations, and project leaders?
A: Show answer
Large transformations are more likely to succeed when organized as many repeatable small deliveries rather than a single bespoke undertaking. Governments can create policy frameworks and early markets that enable experimentation. Corporations can standardize designs, industrialize production, coordinate suppliers, and invest in learning. Project leaders can identify the smallest useful unit, validate it, repeat it, and assemble the larger system while bringing completed units into service. Individuals can use the same logic through small experiments. The chapter’s final emphasis is practical: imagine a better unit of delivery, test it, learn, and persist in applying the modular model.
Study instruction: Write an application plan stating the project’s “Lego,” first pilot, evidence required to scale, standardization strategy, enabling resources, and measures of success.
Key point: Build the large outcome through repeatable small units, learning from each iteration and scaling what works.
Source links: How Big Things Get Done — official publisher page
